# John 1:1 — “In the beginning was the Word”

<!-- guide guide-john-1-1 · John 1:1 · beginner -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: title -->
John 1:1 — “In the beginning was the Word”
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: reference -->
John 1:1
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: summary -->
John shows that the Word is God.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: devotionalFrame -->
John begins his Gospel by taking you back before the beginning — and before you ever meet Jesus by name, you meet him as “the Word.” This first verse is short, but every word in it is doing real work. Take it slowly: the Greek grammar itself is quietly making one of the boldest claims in Scripture.
<!-- @end -->

**Confessional frame**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: confessionalFrame -->
The historic Christian confession — reflected, for instance, in the Nicene Creed’s “God of God … begotten, not made, being of one substance with the Father” — reads this verse as teaching that the Word is fully and truly God while remaining a distinct person from the Father he was “with.” This guide treats that as the settled reading of the wider Christian tradition; the fair summary below explains why a minority reads the last clause differently, and why the majority reading has endured.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: debateSummary.issue -->
θεός (theos) in “θεὸς ἦν ὁ λόγος” (theos ēn ho logos, “God was the Word,” word for word) has no article in front of it — a grammatical fact the diagram makes visible in the predicate-nominative line. Essentially every English Bible renders this “the Word was God.” A minority reading, “the Word was a god,” is non-standard: it is associated almost exclusively with the Jehovah’s Witnesses’ New World Translation, and is accepted by no major Christian denomination and by virtually no mainstream Greek scholarship.
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
“The Word was God” — the reading of essentially every English Bible and the whole Christian tradition
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
An anarthrous (article-less) predicate nominative placed before its verb, as θεός (theos) is here, regularly describes the QUALITY or NATURE of the subject rather than counting it as one item in a class. On this reading the Word fully possesses the nature of God, while remaining distinct in person from “God” (ho theos) named just before — which is why “God,” not “a god,” has been the standard translation across denominations and centuries.
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: debateSummary.views[0].cautions[0] -->
This pattern is sometimes called “Colwell’s rule,” but that “rule” names a tendency, not an ironclad law — it is one supporting observation, not the whole argument.
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — caution 2**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: debateSummary.views[0].cautions[1] -->
A minority reading — “the Word was a god,” tied almost exclusively to the New World Translation — argues the missing article makes θεός (theos) indefinite. It is a non-standard reading: it does not explain why the New Testament elsewhere uses anarthrous θεός in clearly non-indefinite ways (e.g. John 1:18; Romans 9:5), where “a god” makes no sense, and no major Christian denomination or mainstream Greek scholarship accepts it.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
The word order and the missing article are real, checkable facts, and they do point toward a qualitative reading — the Word sharing fully in God’s nature. Grammar frames the question more than it settles the church’s whole doctrine of Christ’s deity, which draws on all of John’s Gospel — but on this narrower translation choice, the grammar itself is not genuinely contested among serious scholars.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — One verse, three connected thoughts

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-one-verse-three-clauses].title -->
One verse, three connected thoughts
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-one-verse-three-clauses].body -->
Before you read a single word of Greek, notice the shape of the verse: John links three short, complete statements with καί (kai, “and”), like three matched beats. The diagram lays each clause on its own baseline, joined by the coordinator. Greek word order is far freer than English — so as we go, follow the diagram’s lines and dividers, not just the order words appear on the page, to see who is doing what.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-one-verse-three-clauses].implication -->
Once you can see the three clauses separately, you can follow each on its own — and by the third, you will be ready for the most important sentence in the whole verse.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — “In the beginning was the Word”

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-in-the-beginning].title -->
“In the beginning was the Word”
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-in-the-beginning].body -->
John opens by echoing the very first words of the Greek Old Testament: “In the beginning God made …” (Genesis 1:1). Here it is [[arche]] again, now paired with [[logos]], “the Word.” Even before we are told who the Word is up close, this opening line already places him before creation itself. The diagram hangs the fronted phrase “in the [[arche]]” beneath the verb [[en]] (“was”), with ὁ λόγος (ho logos, “the Word”) standing as the subject.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-in-the-beginning].implication -->
Whatever “the Word” turns out to mean, John wants you to know this first: he was already there before anything was made. Your English Bible’s “In the beginning was the Word” carries this straight across.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — “The Word was with God”

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-with-god].title -->
“The Word was with God”
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-with-god].body -->
The second clause keeps the same subject — ὁ λόγος (ho logos), the Word — and adds a relationship: he was [[pros]] τὸν θεόν (ton theon), “with God.” [[pros]] pictures more than nearby location; it is the preposition of face-to-face fellowship, one person turned toward another. Already the sentence is holding two things together: the Word is distinct enough from God to be “with” him, yet — as the next clause will show — intimately identified with him.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-with-god].implication -->
Relationship, not just proximity. From the very first verse the Word is shown in personal fellowship with God.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-with-god].caution -->
One preposition cannot carry a whole doctrine of the Trinity by itself — it simply opens a picture the rest of John’s Gospel will fill in.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — “And the Word was God”

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-word-was-god].title -->
“And the Word was God”
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-word-was-god].body -->
Now the boldest line: θεὸς ἦν ὁ λόγος (theos ēn ho logos, “God was the Word,” word for word). Read in that order it looks almost backwards — [[theos]] comes first! But Greek marks its subject by grammar, not by position: [[ho]], the little article, stands in front of [[logos]], marking ὁ λόγος (ho logos, “the Word”) as the subject — the very same subject carried through the whole verse. [[theos]] has no article at all; it is the predicate, describing what the subject is. That is why every reliable English Bible reads it the other way around, “the Word was God,” and not “God was the Word.” The diagram shows this plainly: the divider still separates subject from predicate, and the back-slanted line still points to the predicate nominative, no matter which word came first on the page.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-word-was-god].implication -->
Because [[theos]] has no article and stands right before the verb, Greek readers naturally hear it describing WHAT the Word is — fully possessing the nature of God — rather than counting him as one more “a god” among others. That is the sense your English Bible reaches for with the plain word “God.”
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: steps[step-word-was-god].caution -->
This pattern (sometimes called Colwell’s rule) is a real and useful clue, but it is a clue, not proof by itself — Greek grammar alone cannot settle every question a doctrine like the Trinity raises. Confidence that “the Word was God” belongs to John’s whole Gospel — its full prologue, and verses like John 1:18 and 20:28 — not to one word-order fact taken in isolation.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### ἀρχῇ · archē

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[arche].surface -->
ἀρχῇ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[arche].transliteration -->
archē
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[arche].lemma -->
ἀρχή
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[arche].gloss -->
beginning
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[arche].parsing -->
noun, dative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[arche].explanation -->
The head noun of the fronted phrase “in [the] beginning.” John echoes the opening word of the Greek Old Testament (Genesis 1:1, LXX: Ἐν ἀρχῇ ἐποίησεν ὁ θεός — En archē epoiēsen ho theos, “In the beginning God made …”), placing his whole Gospel deliberately alongside the creation story.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[arche].implication -->
John wants his very first phrase to remind you of Genesis 1. Whatever comes next is meant to be heard as a deeper telling of “the beginning.”
<!-- @end -->

### λόγος · logos

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[logos].surface -->
λόγος
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[logos].transliteration -->
logos
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[logos].lemma -->
λόγος
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[logos].gloss -->
word, message, reason
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[logos].parsing -->
noun, nominative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[logos].explanation -->
The subject of all three clauses in this verse. John does not yet say “Jesus” — he chooses a rich word Greek thought used for the rational principle behind the universe, and the Old Testament used for God’s powerful, creative speech (e.g. Psalm 33:6, “By the word of the LORD the heavens were made”).
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[logos].implication -->
By verse 14 you will learn this Word “became flesh” — for now, John lets the word’s rich meaning build anticipation before naming him.
<!-- @end -->

### ἦν · ēn

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[en].surface -->
ἦν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[en].transliteration -->
ēn
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[en].lemma -->
εἰμί
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[en].gloss -->
was
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[en].parsing -->
imperfect active indicative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[en].explanation -->
The imperfect tense of εἰμί (eimi, “to be”), used all three times in this verse. It describes a past state — here, simply, being — rather than narrating a one-time event.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[en].caution -->
Do not over-read the imperfect by itself as “eternally continuous.” Here it narrates a past state of being; the sense of the Word’s eternity comes from the whole clause (paired with “in the beginning”), not from the tense alone.
<!-- @end -->

### πρὸς · pros

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[pros].surface -->
πρὸς
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[pros].transliteration -->
pros
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[pros].lemma -->
πρός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[pros].gloss -->
toward, with
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[pros].parsing -->
preposition (governing the accusative τὸν θεόν — ton theon, “God” — here)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[pros].explanation -->
More vivid than a plain “near” or “beside,” πρός pictures one person facing, turned toward, another — the language of relationship, not just location.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[pros].implication -->
Even in one small preposition, John is already describing a personal relationship between the Word and God.
<!-- @end -->

### θεὸς · theos

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[theos].surface -->
θεὸς
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[theos].transliteration -->
theos
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[theos].lemma -->
θεός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[theos].gloss -->
God
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[theos].parsing -->
noun, nominative masculine singular — no article, standing before the verb as predicate nominative
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[theos].explanation -->
The predicate of the third clause: it tells you what the subject (the Word) is. Because it has no article and comes first, Greek naturally reads it here as describing the Word’s NATURE — fully God — rather than counting him as one member of a class (“a god”).
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[theos].implication -->
This is the single grammatical detail behind one of the most debated translation choices in the New Testament — see the note on this passage above for why the historic Christian reading has endured, and why a minority reading has not.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[theos].caution -->
The missing article does not make θεός mean something less than “God.” Anarthrous nouns are common throughout Greek and often simply describe. Do not let the absence of “the” suggest the Word was merely “a god” among many.
<!-- @end -->

### ὁ · ho

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[ho].surface -->
ὁ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[ho].transliteration -->
ho
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[ho].lemma -->
ὁ
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[ho].gloss -->
the
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[ho].parsing -->
article, nominative masculine singular, agreeing with λόγος (logos, “word”)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[ho].explanation -->
A small word doing a big job: because ὁ marks λόγος (logos, “word”) as definite and matches its case, careful readers (and the diagram) can identify ὁ λόγος (ho logos, “the Word”) as the subject of this clause — even though θεός (theos, “God”) was written first. Word order in Greek does not decide grammatical roles; agreement and the article do.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-john-1-1 :: greekTerms[ho].implication -->
This is why the diagram can lay out “the Word” as the subject on one side of the divider and “God” as the predicate on the other, regardless of which word appears first in the Greek sentence.
<!-- @end -->

---

# Hebrews 1:1–4 — one sentence about the Son

<!-- guide guide-hebrews-1-1-4 · Hebrews 1:1–4 · intermediate -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: title -->
Hebrews 1:1–4 — one sentence about the Son
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: reference -->
Hebrews 1:1–4
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: summary -->
Hebrews shows that God has spoken finally and fully through his Son.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: devotionalFrame -->
Before anything else, Hebrews wants you to know that God speaks. Everything this letter says about the Son is anchored to that one claim. As you follow the diagram, keep your own Bible open — the structure you are about to see is the structure your translation is faithfully carrying into English.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — Find the main verb first

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-main-verb].title -->
Find the main verb first
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-main-verb].body -->
This whole passage — four verses in your Bible — is one Greek sentence, and it has one main statement: God [[elalesen]] to us in his Son. Everything else in verses 1–4 hangs off this clause. The diagram puts that main clause on the top baseline: subject ὁ θεός (ho theos, “God”) on the left of the divider, the verb on the right.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-main-verb].implication -->
When a sentence is this rich, it helps to find its backbone before admiring its branches. The author’s central claim is simple: God has spoken — finally and personally — in the Son.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — Long ago … and in these last days

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-then-and-now].title -->
Long ago … and in these last days
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-then-and-now].body -->
Before the main verb arrives, the sentence looks back: God spoke “long ago, to the fathers, by the prophets” — all carried by the participle [[lalesas]], “having spoken.” In the diagram this whole memory of the old revelation hangs beneath the main clause as a supporting phrase. The grammar itself makes the sermon’s point: the prophets are real revelation, and they are subordinate to what God says in the Son.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-then-and-now].caution -->
Subordinate grammar does not mean the prophets are unreliable — Hebrews will quote them constantly. The contrast is between stages of one God speaking, not between false and true.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — Radiance and exact imprint — a matched pair

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-radiance-fork].title -->
Radiance and exact imprint — a matched pair
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-radiance-fork].body -->
Verse 3 describes the Son with two nouns joined by καί (kai, “and”): [[apaugasma]] (“radiance”) and [[charakter]] (“exact imprint”). The diagram draws them as a fork: two parallel lines joined at one point, both leaning back toward the little verb [[on]] (“being”). One person, two pictures — what streams out from God’s glory, and what is stamped with God’s very nature.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-radiance-fork].implication -->
The two images balance each other: “radiance” alone might sound like the Son is only an effect of God; “exact imprint” alone might sound static. Together they say the Son truly shows us God — which is why the author can call what God says in him God’s final word.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-radiance-fork].caution -->
These are metaphors reaching for something beyond language. The grammar tells you they are parallel and predicated of the same person; it does not by itself settle every question the church later answered at Nicaea.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — The sentence lands: he sat down

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-sat-down].title -->
The sentence lands: he sat down
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-sat-down].body -->
After the descriptions comes an action that completes the thought: “having made purification for sins, he [[ekathisen]] at the right hand of the Majesty on high.” Notice the order the grammar preserves: the participle [[poiesamenos]] (“having made purification”) comes first, then the sitting. The seated posture is the point — the priestly work is finished.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-sat-down].implication -->
Hebrews will spend chapters on this: priests stand because their work is never done; this priest sat down. The whole letter’s comfort is compressed into one verb at the end of one long sentence.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: steps[step-sat-down].caution -->
The aorist participle “having made purification” views the purification as a whole, prior to the sitting. That is what the grammar says — it does not add “once for all” by itself; Hebrews argues that point explicitly later (7:27; 9:26; 10:12).
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### ἐλάλησεν · elalēsen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[elalesen].surface -->
ἐλάλησεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[elalesen].transliteration -->
elalēsen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[elalesen].lemma -->
λαλέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[elalesen].gloss -->
he spoke / has spoken
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[elalesen].parsing -->
aorist active indicative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[elalesen].explanation -->
The one finite main verb of the whole sentence. The aorist presents God’s speaking in the Son as a complete fact.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[elalesen].implication -->
Everything in vv. 1–4 is grammatically in service of this verb: God has spoken in a Son.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[elalesen].caution -->
The aorist views the action as a whole; it does not by itself mean “once and never again.”
<!-- @end -->

### λαλήσας · lalēsas

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[lalesas].surface -->
λαλήσας
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[lalesas].transliteration -->
lalēsas
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[lalesas].lemma -->
λαλέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[lalesas].gloss -->
having spoken
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[lalesas].parsing -->
aorist active participle, nominative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[lalesas].explanation -->
A participle of the same verb “to speak,” describing God. It gathers all of the old-covenant revelation (“long ago, to the fathers, by the prophets”) into a background clause for the main statement.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[lalesas].implication -->
Same God, same speaking — first through prophets, now in a Son. The participle makes the continuity and the contrast at the same time.
<!-- @end -->

### ὢν · ōn

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[on].surface -->
ὢν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[on].transliteration -->
ōn
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[on].lemma -->
εἰμί
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[on].gloss -->
being
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[on].parsing -->
present active participle, nominative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[on].explanation -->
The little participle of “to be” that carries the two great predicates “radiance” and “exact imprint.” A present participle here describes what the Son simply, continuously is.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[on].caution -->
Do not over-build on one participle’s tense; the surrounding argument, not the participle alone, carries the theology.
<!-- @end -->

### ἀπαύγασμα · apaugasma

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[apaugasma].surface -->
ἀπαύγασμα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[apaugasma].transliteration -->
apaugasma
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[apaugasma].lemma -->
ἀπαύγασμα
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[apaugasma].gloss -->
radiance, effulgence
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[apaugasma].parsing -->
noun, nominative neuter singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[apaugasma].explanation -->
Light streaming out from a source — the Son as the outshining of the Father’s glory. Used only here in the New Testament.
<!-- @end -->

### χαρακτὴρ · charaktēr

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[charakter].surface -->
χαρακτὴρ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[charakter].transliteration -->
charaktēr
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[charakter].lemma -->
χαρακτήρ
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[charakter].gloss -->
exact imprint, stamp
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[charakter].parsing -->
noun, nominative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[charakter].explanation -->
The impress a die stamps into metal — an exact correspondence. The Son bears the very stamp of God’s nature (ὑπόστασις, hypostasis, “essential nature”).
<!-- @end -->

### ἐκάθισεν · ekathisen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[ekathisen].surface -->
ἐκάθισεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[ekathisen].transliteration -->
ekathisen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[ekathisen].lemma -->
καθίζω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[ekathisen].gloss -->
he sat down
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[ekathisen].parsing -->
aorist active indicative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[ekathisen].explanation -->
The climactic action: the Son took his seat at the right hand — the posture of a king whose work is accomplished (echoing Psalm 110:1).
<!-- @end -->

### ποιησάμενος · poiēsamenos

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[poiesamenos].surface -->
ποιησάμενος
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[poiesamenos].transliteration -->
poiēsamenos
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[poiesamenos].lemma -->
ποιέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[poiesamenos].gloss -->
having made
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[poiesamenos].parsing -->
aorist middle participle, nominative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[poiesamenos].explanation -->
“Having made purification for sins” — an aorist participle presenting the purification as complete and prior to the sitting.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-hebrews-1-1-4 :: greekTerms[poiesamenos].caution -->
The “finished-ness” of Christ’s work is Hebrews’ explicit argument later in the letter; the participle is consistent with it but does not prove it alone.
<!-- @end -->

# Mark 5:25–34 — the woman who touched him

<!-- guide guide-mark-5-25-34 · Mark 5:25–34 · beginner -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: title -->
Mark 5:25–34 — the woman who touched him
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: reference -->
Mark 5:25–34
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: summary -->
Mark traces a suffering woman's long journey to one healing touch.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: devotionalFrame -->
Mark tells this story almost breathlessly. In Greek it is one long sentence that keeps adding description after description — a woman who was this, who had suffered that, who had spent everything — before it ever gets to what she does. Watching that grammar unfold is watching Mark slow down time so you feel exactly how much stood behind one quiet, desperate reach.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — A sentence that makes you wait

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-participle-pile].title -->
A sentence that makes you wait
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-participle-pile].body -->
Before the sentence ever reaches its main verb, it introduces “a woman” and then piles up description after description: she was ([[ousa]]) suffering a flow of blood for twelve years; she had suffered ([[pathousa]]) much at the hands of many doctors; she had spent ([[dapanesasa]]) everything she had, with no improvement. In Greek, each of these hangs off “woman” as one long, coordinated chain — grammar that makes you wait exactly as long as the sentence needs you to, so you feel the weight of those twelve years before you ever learn what she does about it.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-participle-pile].implication -->
The grammar itself slows the reading down. That is not decoration — it is Mark teaching you, through sentence structure, how heavy this woman’s history was before she ever moved toward Jesus.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-participle-pile].caution -->
These participles describe one continuous situation, not seven separate lessons to preach on. Read them together as the circumstances leading up to the main action, not as isolated doctrinal points.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — One small action, at last

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-single-touch].title -->
One small action, at last
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-single-touch].body -->
Only after all of that does the sentence add two more participles — having heard ([[akousasa]]) about Jesus, having come into the crowd behind him — and finally arrive at the one finite verb the whole sentence has been building toward: she touched ([[hepsato]]) his cloak. Every participle we followed describes what she is or has done; this single aorist verb is what she finally does. The diagram shows it plainly: one small action on the main line, with an entire paragraph of participial clauses hanging beneath it.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-single-touch].implication -->
Twelve years, many doctors, all her money, no improvement — and it comes down to one quiet, decisive reach. Your English Bible already carries this shape for you; the Greek just shows you why the sentence lands the way it does.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — Her own words: “if only”

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-if-only].title -->
Her own words: “if only”
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-if-only].body -->
Mark lets us hear what she had been telling herself: she kept saying, “If I touch ([[hapsomai]]) even his garments, I will be healed.” The verb “touch” here is the same one she is about to act on — but this time it is a subjunctive tucked inside an “if” clause. Grammatically that marks her plan as hoped-for, not a guarantee she is claiming for herself.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-if-only].implication -->
Her faith, in her own words, is tentative and conditional — “if I only touch…” — and Jesus still meets it. The passage does not ask us to admire the size of her faith, only to notice that even a small, hesitant reach toward him was enough.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-if-only].caution -->
A subjunctive after “if” signals possibility, not the strength or weakness of someone’s faith. The story, not the mood alone, tells us how Jesus receives her.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — Fear finds its voice

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-trembling-confession].title -->
Fear finds its voice
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-trembling-confession].body -->
After the healing, the grammar does the same thing again, this time with fear instead of suffering: “the woman, having been frightened ([[phobetheisa]]) and trembling, knowing what had happened to her, came and fell down before him and told him the whole truth.” Once more a run of participles delays the main verbs — she came, she fell down, she told — so we feel her fear before we hear her confession.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-trembling-confession].implication -->
The same grammatical pattern that painted twelve years of desperation now paints one moment of trembling honesty. Mark’s sentence shape is doing quiet work: it will not let you skip past what this cost her, coming or going.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: steps[step-trembling-confession].caution -->
Her trembling is not shown here as a lack of faith to be corrected — Jesus’s very next words call her “daughter” and commend exactly this faith. Don’t read fear and faith as opposites in this story.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### οὖσα · ousa

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[ousa].surface -->
οὖσα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[ousa].transliteration -->
ousa
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[ousa].lemma -->
εἰμί
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[ousa].gloss -->
being
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[ousa].parsing -->
present active participle, nominative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[ousa].explanation -->
The first link in the chain describing the woman: she “was” in a flow of blood for twelve years. A present participle here simply describes her ongoing condition — the whole scene is set before any main verb arrives.
<!-- @end -->

### παθοῦσα · pathousa

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[pathousa].surface -->
παθοῦσα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[pathousa].transliteration -->
pathousa
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[pathousa].lemma -->
πάσχω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[pathousa].gloss -->
having suffered
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[pathousa].parsing -->
aorist active participle, nominative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[pathousa].explanation -->
She had suffered much under many physicians. The aorist here views that long ordeal as a whole, summarized in one word, and adds it to the growing description of who she is as the sentence reaches for its main verb.
<!-- @end -->

### δαπανήσασα · dapanēsasa

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[dapanesasa].surface -->
δαπανήσασα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[dapanesasa].transliteration -->
dapanēsasa
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[dapanesasa].lemma -->
δαπανάω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[dapanesasa].gloss -->
having spent
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[dapanesasa].parsing -->
aorist active participle, nominative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[dapanesasa].explanation -->
She had spent everything she had, with no benefit — a third participle stacked onto the first two. Mark is not simply informing us of her medical history; the grammar is making us wait through it with her.
<!-- @end -->

### ἀκούσασα · akousasa

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[akousasa].surface -->
ἀκούσασα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[akousasa].transliteration -->
akousasa
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[akousasa].lemma -->
ἀκούω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[akousasa].gloss -->
having heard
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[akousasa].parsing -->
aorist active participle, nominative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[akousasa].explanation -->
Having heard about Jesus — the participle that finally turns her long suffering toward hope, and the last link before the sentence’s one main verb.
<!-- @end -->

### ἥψατο · hēpsato

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hepsato].surface -->
ἥψατο
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hepsato].transliteration -->
hēpsato
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hepsato].lemma -->
ἅπτω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hepsato].gloss -->
she touched
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hepsato].parsing -->
aorist middle indicative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hepsato].explanation -->
The one finite main verb the whole long sentence has been building toward. Everything since “a woman” has been circumstance; this is what she does — she touches his cloak.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hepsato].implication -->
A sentence this loaded with description resolves in a single, small, decisive action. That contrast is the sentence’s whole point.
<!-- @end -->

### ἅψωμαι · hapsōmai

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hapsomai].surface -->
ἅψωμαι
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hapsomai].transliteration -->
hapsōmai
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hapsomai].lemma -->
ἅπτω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hapsomai].gloss -->
I shall touch / if I touch
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hapsomai].parsing -->
aorist middle subjunctive, 1st singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hapsomai].explanation -->
The same verb “to touch,” but here subjunctive after “if” (ἐὰν, ean) — her own hoped-for plan, quoted directly, rather than a report of what already happened.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[hapsomai].caution -->
The subjunctive mood marks this as contingent and hoped-for; it does not by itself measure how strong or weak her faith was.
<!-- @end -->

### φοβηθεῖσα · phobētheisa

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[phobetheisa].surface -->
φοβηθεῖσα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[phobetheisa].transliteration -->
phobētheisa
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[phobetheisa].lemma -->
φοβέομαι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[phobetheisa].gloss -->
having been frightened
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[phobetheisa].parsing -->
aorist passive participle, nominative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-mark-5-25-34 :: greekTerms[phobetheisa].explanation -->
Opens a second short chain of participles (frightened, trembling, knowing) that once again delay the main verbs — she came, fell down, and told him everything — so the reader feels her fear before her confession.
<!-- @end -->

# Matthew 28:19–20 — the Great Commission

<!-- guide guide-matthew-28-19-20 · Matthew 28:19–20 · beginner -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: title -->
Matthew 28:19–20 — the Great Commission
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: reference -->
Matthew 28:19–20
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: summary -->
Jesus commands his disciples to make disciples of all nations.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: devotionalFrame -->
These two verses are often quoted a phrase at a time — “go,” “baptize,” “teach,” “I am with you.” The Greek sentence shows how they actually fit together: one command, carried out three ways, sent to everyone, backed by a promise. Keep your own Bible open as you follow the diagram.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — Find the one command

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-main-command].title -->
Find the one command
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-main-command].body -->
Underneath all the familiar phrases, this sentence has exactly one main verb — an imperative: [[matheteusate]], “make disciples.” The diagram puts it on the main baseline as the whole clause’s predicate. Everything else you read in these two verses — the going, the baptizing, the teaching, even “all nations” — is grammatically built around this one command.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-main-command].implication -->
Jesus’ parting instruction is not a list of four equal tasks. It is one command — disciple-making — with the rest of the sentence showing what that looks like in practice.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — “Going” still carries real weight

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-going-participle].title -->
“Going” still carries real weight
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-going-participle].body -->
The sentence opens with [[poreuthentes]], “going” — grammatically a participle attached to [[matheteusate]] rather than a command of its own. The diagram shows it hanging beneath the main verb, not standing beside it on the baseline. But do not read that as Jesus quietly demoting “go.” Participles of this kind regularly borrow the force of the command they serve — which is exactly why your English Bible renders it “Go therefore” rather than a weaker “While going.”
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-going-participle].caution -->
Do not turn “participle versus imperative” into a contrast that dismisses “go.” The participle is not optional or decorative — it shares the main verb’s own weight. Your translation is not softening the Greek by keeping “go” as a command.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — All nations — no one left out

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-all-nations].title -->
All nations — no one left out
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-all-nations].body -->
The object of “make disciples” is [[ethne]], “nations,” and it is not a narrow group — it is qualified by [[panta]], “all.” The diagram shows this direct object sitting right on the main baseline next to the verb: the plainest, most central position in the sentence. The scope of the command is as wide as the command itself.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-all-nations].implication -->
This is why the church has always read the Great Commission as universal in scope, not addressed to one people alone — the grammar puts “all” directly on the word “nations.”
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — Baptizing and teaching fill out the command

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-baptizing-teaching].title -->
Baptizing and teaching fill out the command
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-baptizing-teaching].body -->
Two more participles describe how disciples are made: [[baptizontes]], “baptizing … in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit,” and [[didaskontes]], “teaching them to observe all that I commanded you.” Like “going,” both hang beneath the main verb in the diagram — not because they are minor details, but because this is exactly how Greek attaches supporting actions to the command they carry out.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-baptizing-teaching].implication -->
Disciple-making, in this sentence, is not left vague. It has a shape: bring people in through baptism, and form them through ongoing teaching of everything Jesus commanded.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — The command comes with a promise

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-i-am-with-you].title -->
The command comes with a promise
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-i-am-with-you].body -->
The next sentence stands on its own: “And behold, [[eimi]] with you always, to the end of the age.” Grammatically it is separate from the command in verses 19–20a, but it is placed right where it is needed — immediately after a command this large. The diagram shows a short, simple clause: “I” as subject, “am” as the verb, “with you … always” filling it out.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: steps[step-i-am-with-you].implication -->
The command to go, baptize, and teach “all nations” is enormous. Matthew ends the Gospel by pairing it with an equally large promise: Jesus himself will be present the whole time, to the very end.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### μαθητεύσατε · mathēteusate

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[matheteusate].surface -->
μαθητεύσατε
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[matheteusate].transliteration -->
mathēteusate
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[matheteusate].lemma -->
μαθητεύω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[matheteusate].gloss -->
make disciples (of)
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[matheteusate].parsing -->
aorist active imperative, 2nd plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[matheteusate].explanation -->
The one finite main verb of the sentence — a command given to the disciples as a group. Everything else (going, baptizing, teaching, “all nations”) is grammatically built to support this single instruction.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[matheteusate].implication -->
This is the grammatical center of the Great Commission: not four separate tasks, but one command carried out in three ways.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[matheteusate].caution -->
The aorist simply presents the command as a whole action to be carried out; it does not by itself mean the task is quick or finished in a moment — Jesus immediately unpacks it as an ongoing work of baptizing and teaching.
<!-- @end -->

### πορευθέντες · poreuthentes

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[poreuthentes].surface -->
πορευθέντες
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[poreuthentes].transliteration -->
poreuthentes
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[poreuthentes].lemma -->
πορεύομαι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[poreuthentes].gloss -->
going / having gone
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[poreuthentes].parsing -->
aorist passive participle, nominative masculine plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[poreuthentes].explanation -->
A participle attached to the main verb “make disciples” rather than a separate command of its own — but this kind of participle regularly carries the same force as the verb it serves.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[poreuthentes].implication -->
This is why English Bibles rightly translate it as an active command, “Go therefore,” rather than a weak “while going.”
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[poreuthentes].caution -->
Being grammatically dependent does not mean optional. “Go” is not demoted just because it is a participle — it shares the imperative’s own weight.
<!-- @end -->

### πάντα · panta

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[panta].surface -->
πάντα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[panta].transliteration -->
panta
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[panta].lemma -->
πᾶς
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[panta].gloss -->
all
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[panta].parsing -->
adjective, accusative neuter plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[panta].explanation -->
Modifies “nations” directly, stressing that no nation is excluded from the scope of the command.
<!-- @end -->

### ἔθνη · ethnē

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[ethne].surface -->
ἔθνη
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[ethne].transliteration -->
ethnē
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[ethne].lemma -->
ἔθνος
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[ethne].gloss -->
nations, peoples
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[ethne].parsing -->
noun, accusative neuter plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[ethne].explanation -->
The direct object of “make disciples.” Paired with πάντα (panta, “all”), the full phrase πάντα τὰ ἔθνη (panta ta ethnē, “all the nations”) marks the command’s scope as every people group, not one nation alone.
<!-- @end -->

### βαπτίζοντες · baptizontes

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[baptizontes].surface -->
βαπτίζοντες
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[baptizontes].transliteration -->
baptizontes
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[baptizontes].lemma -->
βαπτίζω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[baptizontes].gloss -->
baptizing
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[baptizontes].parsing -->
present active participle, nominative masculine plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[baptizontes].explanation -->
A participle attached to “make disciples,” naming baptism — into the name of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit — as part of how disciples are made.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[baptizontes].caution -->
The present tense here describes the action in view without claiming that any one person is baptized repeatedly; context (a single rite, applied to each new believer) supplies that detail, not the tense by itself.
<!-- @end -->

### διδάσκοντες · didaskontes

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[didaskontes].surface -->
διδάσκοντες
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[didaskontes].transliteration -->
didaskontes
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[didaskontes].lemma -->
διδάσκω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[didaskontes].gloss -->
teaching
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[didaskontes].parsing -->
present active participle, nominative masculine plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[didaskontes].explanation -->
A second participle attached to “make disciples,” alongside “baptizing” — teaching new disciples to keep everything Jesus commanded.
<!-- @end -->

### εἰμι · eimi

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[eimi].surface -->
εἰμι
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[eimi].transliteration -->
eimi
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[eimi].lemma -->
εἰμί
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[eimi].gloss -->
I am
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[eimi].parsing -->
present active indicative, 1st singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[eimi].explanation -->
The verb of Jesus’ closing promise, in its own sentence: “I am with you always, to the end of the age.”
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-matthew-28-19-20 :: greekTerms[eimi].implication -->
The command to reach “all nations” is matched by an equally unlimited promise of Jesus’ own presence with those who carry it out.
<!-- @end -->
