# Matthew 6:11 & Luke 11:3 — one petition, two pictures

<!-- guide guide-lords-prayer-bread · Matthew 6:11 & Luke 11:3 · intermediate -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: title -->
Matthew 6:11 & Luke 11:3 — one petition, two pictures
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: reference -->
Matthew 6:11 & Luke 11:3
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: summary -->
Matthew and Luke picture the same prayer for daily bread two ways.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: devotionalFrame -->
Two Gospels remember this same short prayer, worded almost identically — but each writer’s Greek paints its own small picture of what it looks like to ask the Father for daily bread. As you compare the two diagrams, notice that neither picture cancels the other. Together they show how rich one simple request can be.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — Matthew: one plain request

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-matthew-request].title -->
Matthew: one plain request
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-matthew-request].body -->
In Matthew, Jesus teaches: [[dos]] us [[sEmeron]] our daily bread. The diagram puts the command ["give"] on the main line, with "bread" standing as its direct object and "us" as the one receiving it. The verb’s form here — an aorist imperative — presents the whole act of asking as one simple request: give.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-matthew-request].implication -->
This is the shape behind "Give us this day our daily bread": a single, direct petition, asked plainly, for today.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — A word no one is fully sure of

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-matthew-epiousios].title -->
A word no one is fully sure of
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-matthew-epiousios].body -->
The bread Jesus tells us to ask for is [[epiousios]] — usually translated "daily." The diagram shows it as a simple modifier hanging beneath "bread," alongside "our" (a genitive: bread that belongs to us). But the Greek word itself is a puzzle: it barely occurs anywhere else in surviving Greek writing, so nobody can be entirely certain what shade of meaning it carried.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-matthew-epiousios].caution -->
This is a genuinely uncertain word, not a hidden key that overturns your translation. "Daily" is a reasonable, time-honored rendering — so are "for the coming day" and "the bread we need" — and careful scholars still weigh all three.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — Luke: the same prayer, a different verb form

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-luke-present].title -->
Luke: the same prayer, a different verb form
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-luke-present].body -->
Luke hands down the same line almost word for word — "our bread, the daily one" — but where Matthew has [[dos]], Luke has [[didou]]. Below you can see Matthew's wording at the same time: [[dos]] stays highlighted in the stacked panel, so the two verb forms sit side by side rather than one replacing the other on screen. Same verb, same meaning "give," but a present-tense imperative instead of an aorist one. The aorist in Matthew viewed the asking as a whole, a single act; the present in Luke pictures the asking as ongoing, still in progress as it is spoken.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-luke-present].implication -->
This is why some English Bibles render Luke’s line with more of a sense of continuation ("keep giving," "give … day by day") than Matthew’s. Both Gospels are translating the same underlying request faithfully — they simply preserve two verb pictures Jesus’ own words offered.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-luke-present].caution -->
Do not read the present tense here as "always continuously giving" as a fixed rule — it pictures the asking as ongoing rather than as one completed act. How much weight that carries comes from the rest of the sentence, including the phrase Luke adds next.
<!-- @end -->

**Stacked-frame heading**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-luke-present].secondaryTitle -->
Matthew 6:11 — δός (dos, "give"), an aorist imperative
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — "Day by day" — spelled out, not just implied

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-luke-each-day].title -->
"Day by day" — spelled out, not just implied
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-luke-each-day].body -->
Luke does not leave the "ongoing" sense to the verb alone. He adds a whole phrase: [[kathHemeran]], "according to the day" — "each day" or "day by day." In the diagram it hangs beneath the verb as its own modifying phrase, governing "day" the way a preposition governs its object. Below, Matthew's [[sEmeron]] stays highlighted in the stacked panel, so you can compare the two Gospels' modifiers directly: Matthew names the day itself, Luke names its recurrence. Luke states outright what the present tense only suggests.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-luke-each-day].implication -->
Luke’s sense of a daily, repeated request comes as much from these two added words as from his choice of tense. Tense and vocabulary are working together, not one doing all the work.
<!-- @end -->

**Stacked-frame heading**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-luke-each-day].secondaryTitle -->
Matthew 6:11 — σήμερον (sēmeron), "today"
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — One petition, two faithful pictures

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-two-pictures].title -->
One petition, two faithful pictures
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-two-pictures].body -->
Set side by side, Matthew asks for [[dos]] — bread for today, one plain request. Luke asks for [[didou]] [[kathHemeran]] — bread, day by day, an asking pictured as ongoing. Below, both diagrams are stacked and highlighted together — Matthew's [[dos]] and [[sEmeron]] above Luke's [[didou]] and καθ' ἡμέραν (kath' hēmeran, "day by day") — so the comparison is visible, not just described. Neither Gospel is correcting the other, and neither reading contradicts your English Bible, whichever wording it uses. The grammar simply lets us see two true angles on what it means to depend on the Father for daily bread — right now, and every day after this one.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-two-pictures].implication -->
This is exactly why English translations of Matthew and Luke differ here — and why both are faithful. The difference is a difference in picture, not in truth.
<!-- @end -->

**Stacked-frame heading**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: steps[step-two-pictures].secondaryTitle -->
Matthew 6:11 — the same petition, one plain request
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### δὸς · dos

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[dos].surface -->
δὸς
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[dos].transliteration -->
dos
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[dos].lemma -->
δίδωμι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[dos].gloss -->
give
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[dos].parsing -->
aorist active imperative, 2nd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[dos].explanation -->
Matthew’s command form of "give." The aorist imperative presents the whole act of asking as one simple, complete request — "give" — rather than drawing attention to repetition.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[dos].implication -->
This is the grammar behind "Give us this day our daily bread": one direct petition, asked plainly.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[dos].caution -->
The aorist here does not mean "give once and never again." It simply views the request as a single, whole act — Jesus assumes his disciples will pray this prayer daily.
<!-- @end -->

### σήμερον · sēmeron

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[sEmeron].surface -->
σήμερον
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[sEmeron].transliteration -->
sēmeron
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[sEmeron].lemma -->
σήμερον
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[sEmeron].gloss -->
today
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[sEmeron].parsing -->
adverb
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[sEmeron].explanation -->
Matthew anchors the request to this one day: "give us today our daily bread." It modifies the verb "give," telling us when the asking happens rather than how often.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[sEmeron].implication -->
This matches "this day" or "today" in most English translations of Matthew’s version of the prayer.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐπιούσιον · epiousion

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[epiousios].surface -->
ἐπιούσιον
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[epiousios].transliteration -->
epiousion
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[epiousios].lemma -->
ἐπιούσιος
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[epiousios].gloss -->
daily / for the coming day / needful
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[epiousios].parsing -->
adjective, accusative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[epiousios].explanation -->
The word translated "daily." It is a famously rare word — it appears almost nowhere else in surviving Greek literature outside the Lord’s Prayer — so its precise sense has been debated since the earliest centuries: bread "for today," bread "for the coming day," or simply the bread "we need."
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[epiousios].implication -->
Whichever choice a translation makes, it is reaching for the same idea: dependence on the Father for the bread that sustains us.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[epiousios].caution -->
This is a genuinely uncertain word. No single English rendering can claim certainty here — "daily" is a reasonable, time-honored choice, not the only defensible one.
<!-- @end -->

### δίδου · didou

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[didou].surface -->
δίδου
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[didou].transliteration -->
didou
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[didou].lemma -->
δίδωμι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[didou].gloss -->
give / keep giving
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[didou].parsing -->
present active imperative, 2nd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[didou].explanation -->
Luke’s command form of the same verb "give." A present-tense imperative pictures the asking as ongoing, in progress, rather than presenting it as one simple completed act — a different camera angle on the same request Matthew records.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[didou].implication -->
This is the grammar behind translations that render Luke’s line with a sense of continuation, such as "give us each day" or "keep giving us."
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[didou].caution -->
The present tense does not by itself mean "ask forever, non-stop, as a fixed rule." It pictures the request as ongoing; how much weight that carries depends on the rest of the sentence — including the phrase Luke adds right after it.
<!-- @end -->

### καθ · kath

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[kathHemeran].surface -->
καθ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[kathHemeran].transliteration -->
kath
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[kathHemeran].lemma -->
κατά
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[kathHemeran].gloss -->
according to / each
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[kathHemeran].parsing -->
preposition, governing ἡμέραν (accusative)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[kathHemeran].explanation -->
This small preposition governs "day" (ἡμέραν, hēmeran) to form the phrase "day by day" or "each day," attached to Luke’s verb as its own modifying phrase. Luke states outright, in so many words, what the present tense on its own only hints at.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-bread :: greekTerms[kathHemeran].implication -->
Luke’s sense of an ongoing, repeated request comes as much from this added phrase as from his choice of verb tense — the two work together, not the tense alone.
<!-- @end -->

---

# Matthew 6:9–13 — praying the Lord’s Prayer in commands

<!-- guide guide-lords-prayer-petitions · Matthew 6:9–13 · beginner -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: title -->
Matthew 6:9–13 — praying the Lord’s Prayer in commands
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: reference -->
Matthew 6:9–13
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: summary -->
Jesus teaches his disciples to pray the Lord's Prayer as bold commands.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: devotionalFrame -->
Before you meet a single Greek word, notice this: Jesus does not teach his disciples to pray with timid maybes. Six times in a row, in the short space of five verses, he puts a command on their lips. Follow the diagram through Matthew 6:9–13 and watch the same shape surface again and again — not because we get to command God from above, but because he invites us to ask him this boldly, this plainly, like children who trust they will be heard.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — Six petitions, every one a command

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-shape-of-prayer].title -->
Six petitions, every one a command
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-shape-of-prayer].body -->
Read in English, the Lord’s Prayer’s six requests sound like gentle wishes: “hallowed be thy name,” “thy kingdom come,” “thy will be done,” “give us … forgive us … deliver us.” Not one of the underlying Greek verbs is a wish. Every single petition is a command form — a true imperative mood (with one exception you will meet at the end, which Greek phrases a different way but which still functions as a command). Look at the diagram below: three short clauses, coordinated by “and,” each built the very same way — a bare command verb standing as the whole clause’s predicate, nothing softening it grammatically.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-shape-of-prayer].implication -->
Once you can see that shape — command verb, nothing hedging it — you are ready to look closely at what kind of command each one is, and why English translates some of them so differently from others.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — The three “thy” petitions — commands English cannot say in third person

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-thy-petitions].title -->
The three “thy” petitions — commands English cannot say in third person
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-thy-petitions].body -->
The first three petitions are all spoken ABOUT God, in the third person: [[hagiasthaeto]] — literally “let it be hallowed” — asks that God’s name be treated as holy. [[eltheto]] — “let it come” — asks that his kingdom arrive. [[genaetheto]] — “let it be done” — asks that his will happen. English simply has no ordinary third-person command form (you cannot say “name hallow!” the way you can say “you, give!”), so English Bibles reach for an old, subjunctive-sounding construction instead: “hallowed BE,” “kingdom COME” (not “comes”), “will BE done.” That is not English going soft on the grammar — it is the best available English shape for a mood Greek marks plainly on the verb itself.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-thy-petitions].implication -->
This is why so many English Bibles keep the same archaic-sounding wording across centuries of revision: “hallowed be,” “thy kingdom come,” “thy will be done” is not old-fashioned piety for its own sake — it is English straining to hold onto a real command that its grammar cannot phrase any more directly.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-thy-petitions].caution -->
All three verbs here are AORIST. Do not read that as “once, and never needing to happen again” — it simply presents each request as one whole, complete act being asked for, not as a claim about how often it happens. Jesus assumes his disciples will pray this prayer again and again.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — The “us” petitions — second-person imperatives, bold asking

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-us-petitions].title -->
The “us” petitions — second-person imperatives, bold asking
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-us-petitions].body -->
The next two petitions turn from speaking ABOUT God to speaking straight TO him: “Give us … our daily bread” uses [[dos]], and “forgive us our debts” uses [[aphes]] — both plain second-person commands, the same imperative mood English can say directly: “You — give,” “You — forgive.” Nothing here is hedged or softened either; these are just as much true commands as the three petitions before them, only now addressed face to face.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-us-petitions].implication -->
Notice that Jesus does not teach his disciples to hint or apologize their way toward God. Both halves of the prayer — the “thy” petitions and the “us” petitions — are built from the same bold grammar: commands, not requests dressed up as questions.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-us-petitions].caution -->
Both verbs here are also aorist. As before, that does not mean “ask once and be done” — it simply views each asking as one complete, whole request, said plainly, this time.
<!-- @end -->

**Stacked-frame heading**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-us-petitions].secondaryTitle -->
Matthew 6:12 — ἄφες (aphes), "forgive"
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — The one petition that is NOT an imperative

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-not-imperative].title -->
The one petition that is NOT an imperative
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-not-imperative].body -->
The last line has two halves, and they are built differently. “But deliver us from the evil one” uses [[rhysai]] — a normal second-person imperative, exactly like [[dos]] and [[aphes]] before it. But “lead us not into temptation” is NOT phrased with an imperative at all: it pairs the negating word [[mE]] with [[eisenenkes]], a verb in the SUBJUNCTIVE mood. Greek generally avoids negating an aorist imperative directly; its everyday way to phrase "do not do this" is [[mE]] plus an aorist subjunctive, exactly as here. Grammatically that is a different mood than the other five petitions — but functionally, paired with [[mE]], it carries every bit as much force as a command. That is why every English Bible still translates it "lead us not," a plain command, matching the other five in force even though its Greek engine runs on different grammar.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-not-imperative].implication -->
So of the prayer’s six petitions, five are imperatives — three spoken about God in the third person, two spoken straight to him in the second — and one is a subjunctive doing an imperative’s job because that is how Greek negates a command. Every single line, without exception, asks boldly.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-not-imperative].caution -->
The subjunctive [[eisenenkes]] is also aorist in form. As with every verb in this prayer, that pictures the asking as one whole, complete request — not a claim that it happens only once, and not a claim about how often temptation itself might come.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — What it means to pray in commands

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-praying-in-imperatives].title -->
What it means to pray in commands
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-praying-in-imperatives].body -->
Step back and look at the whole prayer again: [[hagiasthaeto]] … [[eltheto]] … [[genaetheto]] … [[dos]] … [[aphes]] … [[rhysai]] — six petitions, five imperatives and one prohibition doing an imperative’s work, and not one polite hedge among them. Jesus is not teaching his disciples a technique for getting God’s attention through hesitant, deferential language. He is teaching them to come to their Father the way a child comes to a parent they trust completely: directly, specifically, without first talking themselves into believing they are allowed to ask.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: steps[step-praying-in-imperatives].implication -->
This is not a loophole for commanding God as though we stood over him. It is the opposite: Jesus hands his disciples bold, direct words BECAUSE the Father he is teaching them to address delights in being asked plainly. The grammar itself is an invitation to confident, urgent prayer.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### ἁγιασθήτω · hagiasthētō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[hagiasthaeto].surface -->
ἁγιασθήτω
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[hagiasthaeto].transliteration -->
hagiasthētō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[hagiasthaeto].lemma -->
ἁγιάζω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[hagiasthaeto].gloss -->
let it be hallowed / treated as holy
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[hagiasthaeto].parsing -->
aorist passive imperative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[hagiasthaeto].explanation -->
A third-person command form: it tells "your name" (the subject of this clause) what to have done to it — be hallowed, be treated as holy — rather than describing a wish about it. English has no ordinary way to give a third-person command, so translators supply the old-sounding "be" construction ("hallowed BE thy name") to carry across a mood Greek marks directly on the verb.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[hagiasthaeto].implication -->
This is the grammar behind "hallowed be thy name": not a soft hope, but the church’s oldest prayer asking, in a true command form, that God’s name be honored as holy.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[hagiasthaeto].caution -->
The aorist here does not mean "let this happen once and be finished." It simply presents the request as one whole, complete act being asked for — Jesus assumes his disciples will keep praying this same request.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐλθέτω · elthetō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eltheto].surface -->
ἐλθέτω
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eltheto].transliteration -->
elthetō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eltheto].lemma -->
ἔρχομαι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eltheto].gloss -->
let it come
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eltheto].parsing -->
aorist active imperative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eltheto].explanation -->
Another third-person command, this time telling "your kingdom" (the subject) to come. English again has no direct third-person imperative, so translations use the subjunctive-sounding "kingdom COME" (not "comes") to carry the same command force.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eltheto].implication -->
This is why "thy kingdom come" reads a little archaic to modern ears: it is not timid, poetic language — it is English’s best available shape for a genuine command aimed at God’s kingdom’s arrival.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eltheto].caution -->
Do not read this aorist as "let the kingdom arrive in one single moment, never gradually." It views the coming of the kingdom as one whole reality being asked for — it is not making a claim about the timeline.
<!-- @end -->

### γενηθήτω · genēthētō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[genaetheto].surface -->
γενηθήτω
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[genaetheto].transliteration -->
genēthētō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[genaetheto].lemma -->
γίνομαι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[genaetheto].gloss -->
let it be done / let it happen
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[genaetheto].parsing -->
aorist passive imperative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[genaetheto].explanation -->
The third of the "thy" petitions, built exactly like ἁγιασθήτω (hagiasthētō, "let it be hallowed"): a third-person command telling "your will" (the subject) to be done. It is also the verb that carries the added phrase "as in heaven, so also on earth" — the one place among the three where the request is explicitly extended to cover the whole creation.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[genaetheto].implication -->
This is the grammar behind "thy will be done, on earth as it is in heaven": a command, not a resigned wish, asking that heaven’s pattern become earth’s pattern too.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[genaetheto].caution -->
As with the other two "thy" petitions, the aorist here pictures the request as one complete act, not a claim that God’s will need only be accomplished a single time.
<!-- @end -->

### δὸς · dos

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[dos].surface -->
δὸς
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[dos].transliteration -->
dos
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[dos].lemma -->
δίδωμι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[dos].gloss -->
give
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[dos].parsing -->
aorist active imperative, 2nd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[dos].explanation -->
A plain, direct second-person command — "You, give" — spoken straight to the Father rather than about him in the third person. English can say this kind of command exactly as Greek does, which is why "give us this day our daily bread" needs no special grammatical workaround.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[dos].implication -->
The prayer turns here from speaking ABOUT God (the first three petitions) to speaking straight TO him — but the boldness of the command does not change at all.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[dos].caution -->
The aorist here, as throughout this prayer, does not mean "give once and never again" — it simply presents the asking as one whole, complete request for today’s bread.
<!-- @end -->

### ἄφες · aphes

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[aphes].surface -->
ἄφες
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[aphes].transliteration -->
aphes
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[aphes].lemma -->
ἀφίημι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[aphes].gloss -->
forgive / let go, release
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[aphes].parsing -->
aorist active imperative, 2nd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[aphes].explanation -->
The second direct, second-person command in the prayer — "You, forgive" — built the same way as δὸς (dos, "give"). The verb’s ordinary sense is "release" or "let go," so "forgive us our debts" pictures sins as debts being released rather than merely overlooked.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[aphes].implication -->
This command comes with a built-in measuring line right in the same verse — "as we forgive our debtors" — so the boldness of asking is paired immediately with a call to extend the same forgiveness outward.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[aphes].caution -->
Again, the aorist views this as one whole act of forgiving being requested, not a claim that forgiveness, once given, will never need to be asked for again.
<!-- @end -->

### ῥῦσαι · rhysai

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[rhysai].surface -->
ῥῦσαι
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[rhysai].transliteration -->
rhysai
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[rhysai].lemma -->
ῥύομαι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[rhysai].gloss -->
deliver / rescue
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[rhysai].parsing -->
aorist middle imperative, 2nd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[rhysai].explanation -->
The third and final direct, second-person command — "You, deliver" — a plain imperative just like δὸς (dos, "give") and ἄφες (aphes, "forgive"), though this particular verb happens to form its imperative in the middle voice rather than the active. That voice difference is simply how this verb behaves in Greek; it does not soften the command in any way.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[rhysai].implication -->
Placed right beside the one petition that is NOT an imperative (see the next term), this verb shows the contrast plainly: this half of the sentence is a true command, spoken with exactly the same directness as "give" and "forgive."
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[rhysai].caution -->
The aorist here pictures deliverance as one whole act being requested — it does not by itself specify how long that deliverance lasts or whether the danger could return.
<!-- @end -->

### μὴ · mē

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[mE].surface -->
μὴ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[mE].transliteration -->
mē
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[mE].lemma -->
μή
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[mE].gloss -->
not
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[mE].parsing -->
negative particle, negating a verb in the subjunctive mood
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[mE].explanation -->
The small word that turns "lead us … into temptation" into "lead us NOT into temptation." Paired with an aorist verb, Greek almost never negates with a plain imperative; instead it reaches for μή (mē, "not") plus the aorist SUBJUNCTIVE — exactly the pattern here with εἰσενέγκῃς (eisenenkēs, "bring in") — as its everyday way to phrase a negative command.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[mE].implication -->
This is why the sixth petition looks grammatically different from the other five even though every English Bible still renders it as a command, "lead us not": Greek is not softening the request, it is simply using its normal machinery for negating one.
<!-- @end -->

### εἰσενέγκῃς · eisenenkēs

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eisenenkes].surface -->
εἰσενέγκῃς
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eisenenkes].transliteration -->
eisenenkēs
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eisenenkes].lemma -->
εἰσφέρω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eisenenkes].gloss -->
bring in / lead
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eisenenkes].parsing -->
aorist active subjunctive, 2nd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eisenenkes].explanation -->
Grammatically a SUBJUNCTIVE, not an imperative — the one verb in the whole prayer that is not. Paired with μή (mē, "not"), it does an imperative’s job: Greek’s standard way to phrase a negative command pairs μή with an aorist subjunctive (rather than an aorist imperative), so functionally this line carries exactly the same command force as the other five petitions.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eisenenkes].implication -->
This is the single grammatical exception in an otherwise unbroken chain of commands — and it turns out not to be an exception to the PRAYER’s boldness at all, only to its verb FORM. Jesus still teaches his disciples to ask this plainly.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-lords-prayer-petitions :: greekTerms[eisenenkes].caution -->
Do not over-read the aorist aspect here either: it presents "being led into trial" as one whole event being prayed against, not a claim about how many times temptation itself might come.
<!-- @end -->

---

# 1 John 2:1 & 3:6–9 — one act of sin, or a life of it?

<!-- guide guide-1-john-sin · 1 John 2:1; 3:6–9 · intermediate -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: title -->
1 John 2:1 & 3:6–9 — one act of sin, or a life of it?
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: reference -->
1 John 2:1; 3:6–9
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: summary -->
John contrasts a single act of sin with a settled, sinning way of life.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: devotionalFrame -->
These two passages sit only a chapter apart, and read alone they can sound like they pull in opposite directions: “if anyone sins, we have an advocate” — and then, “no one who is born of God practices sin.” Follow the diagrams closely and you will see John reaching for two different pictures of sin: one a single occurrence, the other a settled direction of life. Both are true, and both are for you.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: debateSummary.issue -->
How do we hold “if anyone sins, we have an advocate” (2:1) together with “no one who abides in him sins … whoever is born of God cannot sin” (3:6, 9) without making John contradict himself?
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
Aspectual / habitual-pattern reading
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
The aorist ἁμάρτῃ (hamartē, “should sin”) in 2:1 pictures a specific, occasional act of sin, while the present-tense ἁμαρτάνει (hamartanei, “sins”) and ποιεῖ (poiei, “practices”) in 3:6, 9 describe a continuing, characteristic practice or direction of life. On this reading John is not claiming Christians never sin — he has already denied that possibility in 1:8–10 — but that a person born of God and abiding in Christ is not defined or ruled by sin as a settled pattern.
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: debateSummary.views[0].cautions[0] -->
Verb aspect is a real signal, but it does not by itself prove “habitual” — some present-tense verbs are simply general statements, not necessarily iterative ones. This reading still needs the rest of chapter 3 (the new birth, the devil’s pattern, love versus hate) to carry most of the weight, not the tense alone.
<!-- @end -->

### View 2

**View 2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: debateSummary.views[1].label -->
Rhetorical / polemical reading
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: debateSummary.views[1].summary -->
Some scholars set the aspectual contrast aside as the main key and instead read 3:6–9 as strong, black-and-white language aimed at a specific target — teachers in John’s community who denied the seriousness of sin (echoed in 1:8, 10) or who claimed a sinlessness of their own. On this view John’s absolute-sounding statements are an ideal contrast between “children of God” and “children of the devil,” typical of his stark either/or style, and the tension is resolved more by the letter’s argument and historical situation than by grammar.
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: debateSummary.views[1].cautions[0] -->
This reading can undersell how deliberately John repeats present-tense verbs across several verses in a row; it should not be used to explain away the real moral seriousness of 3:6–9.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
The aorist/present contrast is a genuine feature of the Greek and a fair place to start — it shows John using two different verbal pictures, not the same one repeated. But the tenses alone cannot tell us how absolute to take “cannot sin,” nor settle whether John means “does not live characterized by sin” or something more categorical. That depends on reading 3:6–9 inside the letter’s full argument: the advocate of 2:1–2, abiding in Christ, and the new birth — none of which a single verb’s tense can carry by itself.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — “If anyone should sin” — a real, specific case

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-if-anyone-sins].title -->
“If anyone should sin” — a real, specific case
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-if-anyone-sins].body -->
John has just said, “If we say we have no sin, we deceive ourselves” (1:8) — he is not naive about failure. Here [[hamarte]] pictures one concrete case: “if anyone should sin.” The aorist views the sinning as a single, whole event in mind — a specific lapse, not a description of someone’s whole way of life. John writes this to ordinary believers, expecting that sin will sometimes happen.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-if-anyone-sins].implication -->
Notice what John does not say: he is not describing the kind of person someone is. He is naming the honest moment after a specific failure — which is exactly why the next clause matters so much.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-if-anyone-sins].caution -->
The aorist here is not a special tense that means “just this once, and never again.” It simply views this instance of sinning as a complete whole, the way you would refer to any single event. What John does with that instance — pointing to an advocate — comes from the sentence he builds around it, not from the tense by itself.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — We have an advocate — Jesus Christ the righteous

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-we-have-an-advocate].title -->
We have an advocate — Jesus Christ the righteous
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-we-have-an-advocate].body -->
The verb answering that scenario is [[echomen]], “we have” — a standing possession, not a one-time provision. Whom do we have? [[parakleton]], “an advocate,” and John names him at once: Jesus Christ, the Righteous. The diagram lays the whole title — advocate, Jesus, Christ, righteous — as one chain resting on that single verb.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-we-have-an-advocate].implication -->
This is the answer to every specific failure that [[hamarte]] pictures: not excuse, not despair, but an Advocate — himself righteous — pleading our case with the Father.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — “Does not sin” — a settled, present-tense pattern

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-abiding-does-not-sin].title -->
“Does not sin” — a settled, present-tense pattern
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-abiding-does-not-sin].body -->
A chapter later, John describes something different. [[menon]] (“abiding”) and [[hamartanei]] (“sins”) are both present tense: “Everyone who abides in him does not sin.” The present tense views the action as ongoing, in progress — not a single case like [[hamarte]], but a continuing condition. John has moved from an isolated lapse to the settled direction of a life.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-abiding-does-not-sin].implication -->
Set beside 2:1, the contrast is not that John forgot what he wrote three chapters earlier. He is describing two different things: a specific act (aorist) and a persisting pattern (present). The grammar leaves room for both statements to stand.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-abiding-does-not-sin].caution -->
Present tense does not by itself mean “always, without exception, habitually.” It means the action is pictured as ongoing. How much weight to put on that here — a settled direction of life, not sinless perfection — depends on reading this in context, not on the tense alone.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — Born of God: sin is not the practice of the new life

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-born-of-god].title -->
Born of God: sin is not the practice of the new life
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-born-of-god].body -->
The pattern repeats in 3:9: “everyone [[gegennemenos]] of God does not [[poiei]] sin” — literally, does not “practice” or “keep doing” sin. [[poiei]] is present tense again, the same ongoing picture as [[hamartanei]] in verse 6. But now John gives the reason: God’s seed abides in him. The new birth, not willpower, is why sin is no longer the shape of this life.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-born-of-god].implication -->
This is why the whole letter, not one tense, is the real answer to the apparent tension: 2:1 assures the failing believer of an Advocate; 3:6–9 calls that same believer to a life no longer defined by sin, because of a new birth and an abiding relationship with Christ. Aspect helps frame the contrast; the letter’s larger argument — the advocate, abiding in Christ, the new birth — is what actually carries it.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: steps[step-born-of-god].caution -->
Do not flatten this to a rule — “aorist always means one time, present always means habitual.” Some interpreters resolve the tension mainly through the letter’s argument and rhetorical purpose rather than through the verb tenses themselves; see the debate note below.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### ἁμάρτῃ · hamartē

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamarte].surface -->
ἁμάρτῃ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamarte].transliteration -->
hamartē
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamarte].lemma -->
ἁμαρτάνω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamarte].gloss -->
should sin
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamarte].parsing -->
aorist active subjunctive, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamarte].explanation -->
Governed by ἐάν (ean, “if”), this pictures one potential act of sin as a single whole — a specific case in view, not a description of a lifestyle.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamarte].implication -->
This is the scenario 2:1 is built to answer: a real believer, after a real failure.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamarte].caution -->
The aorist does not mean “just once, and it can never happen again” — it simply views this one occurrence as a complete whole.
<!-- @end -->

### ἔχομεν · echomen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[echomen].surface -->
ἔχομεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[echomen].transliteration -->
echomen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[echomen].lemma -->
ἔχω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[echomen].gloss -->
we have
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[echomen].parsing -->
present active indicative, 1st plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[echomen].explanation -->
The main verb of the sentence’s second half: a standing possession believers have, not a one-time grant.
<!-- @end -->

### παράκλητον · paraklēton

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[parakleton].surface -->
παράκλητον
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[parakleton].transliteration -->
paraklēton
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[parakleton].lemma -->
παράκλητος
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[parakleton].gloss -->
an advocate
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[parakleton].parsing -->
noun, accusative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[parakleton].explanation -->
A legal-flavored term for one who speaks on another’s behalf. Here it stands in one chain of apposition with “Jesus Christ, the Righteous.”
<!-- @end -->

### μένων · menōn

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[menon].surface -->
μένων
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[menon].transliteration -->
menōn
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[menon].lemma -->
μένω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[menon].gloss -->
abiding
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[menon].parsing -->
present active participle, nominative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[menon].explanation -->
Describes an ongoing relationship of remaining “in him” (Christ) — the ground for the claim that follows.
<!-- @end -->

### ἁμαρτάνει · hamartanei

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamartanei].surface -->
ἁμαρτάνει
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamartanei].transliteration -->
hamartanei
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamartanei].lemma -->
ἁμαρτάνω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamartanei].gloss -->
sins
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamartanei].parsing -->
present active indicative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamartanei].explanation -->
The same verb as ἁμάρτῃ (hamartē, “should sin”) in 2:1, now present tense: sin viewed as ongoing rather than as one instance.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[hamartanei].caution -->
Present tense pictures the action as in progress; it does not automatically mean “without exception, forever.” Context — the letter’s argument about the new birth and abiding in Christ — carries most of that weight.
<!-- @end -->

### ποιεῖ · poiei

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[poiei].surface -->
ποιεῖ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[poiei].transliteration -->
poiei
<!-- @end -->

<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[poiei].lemma -->
ποιέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[poiei].gloss -->
practices
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[poiei].parsing -->
present active indicative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[poiei].explanation -->
“Does not practice sin” — present tense again, picturing sin as an ongoing activity rather than a single lapse.
<!-- @end -->

### γεγεννημένος · gegennēmenos

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[gegennemenos].surface -->
γεγεννημένος
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[gegennemenos].transliteration -->
gegennēmenos
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[gegennemenos].lemma -->
γεννάω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[gegennemenos].gloss -->
having been born
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[gegennemenos].parsing -->
perfect passive participle, nominative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[gegennemenos].explanation -->
The perfect tense views the new birth as a past event with a continuing effect: someone who has been born of God and remains so.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-john-sin :: greekTerms[gegennemenos].implication -->
This is the reason 3:9 gives for the claim beside it: not self-effort, but a completed new birth whose effect abides.
<!-- @end -->

---

# 1 Peter 3:18–22 — Christ's proclamation, and baptism's pledge

<!-- guide guide-1-peter-3-18-22 · 1 Peter 3:18–22 · advanced -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: title -->
1 Peter 3:18–22 — Christ's proclamation, and baptism's pledge
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: reference -->
1 Peter 3:18–22
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: summary -->
Peter connects Christ's proclamation to imprisoned spirits with baptism's saving pledge.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: devotionalFrame -->
This short passage has comforted suffering Christians for two thousand years — and puzzled careful readers for just as long. Peter packs an enormous claim into a few tightly built Greek clauses: Christ's suffering, a mysterious proclamation, Noah's flood, and baptism, all in one breath. This guide will not settle every question the church has argued about here. What it can do is show you exactly where the sentence itself leaves room for more than one faithful reading, and where Peter supplies his own qualifiers. Keep your own Bible open alongside it — translation cannot decide this for you, and neither can a diagram; both can only show you clearly what the words actually say.
<!-- @end -->

**Confessional frame**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: confessionalFrame -->
A confessional note (confessional Reformed): many in this tradition read "baptism now saves you" sacramentally but not mechanically — baptism is an effectual sign, given by Christ to his church, that saves precisely as, and only as, it is joined by the Spirit to a believer's faith in the risen Christ. That qualification is not read into the text: Peter states his own qualifier in the very same sentence (an appeal, or pledge, of a good conscience, grounded in the resurrection). This is one responsible reading among those summarized below, offered here as a labelled confessional note, not the one settled answer.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: debateSummary.issue -->
Who are the "spirits in prison" Christ proclaimed to (3:19), and how does baptism "now save" (3:21)?
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
Christ, through the Spirit, preached through Noah to that generation
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
On this reading, "in which" (ἐν ᾧ, en hō) reaches back to the Spirit of v.18: the pre-incarnate Christ was himself preaching righteousness through Noah to Noah's disobedient contemporaries, who are now, at the time Peter writes, spirits held in the prison of judgment. This view keeps the proclamation tied to real people who heard a gospel-shaped call to repent while they were still alive to answer it.
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: debateSummary.views[0].cautions[0] -->
This reading has to supply "through Noah" and "at that time" — the sentence itself names no preacher besides Christ and no vehicle besides "having gone."
<!-- @end -->

### View 2

**View 2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: debateSummary.views[1].label -->
Christ proclaimed victory or judgment to imprisoned spirit-powers
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: debateSummary.views[1].summary -->
On this reading the "spirits" are not human at all, but the rebellious powers often linked to Genesis 6 and to the fallen "sons of God" behind Noah's generation. Between his death and resurrection (or at his resurrection), Christ proclaimed his triumph or pronounced judgment over them. Supporters point out that a bare plural "spirits" almost always names spirit-beings elsewhere in the New Testament, and that 3:22's picture of "angels, authorities, and powers" made subject to Christ reads as the resolution of exactly this scene.
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: debateSummary.views[1].cautions[0] -->
This view places the event in a window of time — between death and resurrection — that the sentence itself does not date precisely.
<!-- @end -->

### View 3

**View 3 — label**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: debateSummary.views[2].label -->
A proclamation to the human dead
<!-- @end -->

**View 3 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: debateSummary.views[2].summary -->
A further reading takes "spirits" as departed human souls in general, and the proclamation as some announcement made to the dead — sometimes linked to 1 Peter 4:6, "the gospel was preached even to the dead." This view takes the plain sense of "spirits" as people who have died, and reads the passage as answering a real pastoral question the early church asked: what about those who died before Christ came?
<!-- @end -->

**View 3 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: debateSummary.views[2].cautions[0] -->
This is the hardest reading to square with the pattern (elsewhere in this era's Greek) of a bare plural "spirits" naming spirit-beings, and it opens further questions this verse alone does not answer.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
The grammar gives real, load-bearing clues: the dative relative pronoun ἐν ᾧ (en hō, "in which") almost certainly reaches back into the previous sentence rather than starting a thought from nothing; a bare plural "spirits" usually names spirit-beings elsewhere in this era's Greek; and one continuous participle chain links the proclamation, the imprisonment, Noah's generation, and Christ's final exaltation over "angels, authorities, and powers" into a single scene. But the grammar does not name who "in which" refers to, does not date the proclamation, and does not spell out its content — those calls are made from wider theology and the rest of Scripture, not from this sentence's syntax alone. The same is true in v.21: the apposition "not the removal of dirt from the body but an appeal of a good conscience" is Peter's own grammar-level qualifier, and it genuinely narrows the question — but it does not by itself settle exactly how baptism relates to regeneration, faith, or the moment of salvation. Careful readers hold several of these views in good conscience; the diagram's job is to show you precisely where those forks sit in the sentence, not to close them for you.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — Put to death, made alive — a matched pair

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-put-to-death].title -->
Put to death, made alive — a matched pair
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-put-to-death].body -->
Just before the hardest verses in this section, Peter anchors everything in Christ himself: he was put to death in the flesh, but made alive in [[pneumati]] — "the spirit," or perhaps "the Spirit." The diagram draws these two ideas as a matched pair of participles, joined by μὲν … δὲ (men … de), Greek's own way of saying "on the one hand … on the other." Keep an eye on that dative word [[pneumati]] — the very next sentence opens with "in which," and matching case and number is one real clue, among several, to what "which" is reaching back to.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-put-to-death].implication -->
Peter builds the very next, more difficult sentence on top of this one. Reading carefully here pays off in a moment.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-put-to-death].caution -->
This pairing describes a real turning point in Christ's own story — it is a genuine clue for what follows, not a formula that solves the next verse by itself.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — "In which" — a pointer the sentence itself must supply

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-in-which].title -->
"In which" — a pointer the sentence itself must supply
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-in-which].body -->
Verse 19 opens with two small words, ἐν ᾧ (en hō) — "in which" or "in whom." [[ho]] is a relative pronoun in the dative case, and Greek does not spell out its antecedent inside this clause; you have to reason it out from what comes before and after, the same work every translator already does before choosing English words. Whatever "which" points to, the sentence continues: Christ "went" and [[ekeryxen]] — a verb that simply means to announce or proclaim, without telling us, on its own, the content of what was proclaimed.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-in-which].implication -->
Two open questions sit right here in the grammar: what does "which" refer to, and what did this proclamation actually announce? The rest of the sentence — and the rest of Scripture — is where readers go looking for the answer.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — The spirits in prison

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-spirits-in-prison].title -->
The spirits in prison
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-spirits-in-prison].body -->
The ones proclaimed to are called [[pneumasin]] — "spirits" — "in prison," described as having been disobedient long ago, "when God's patience kept waiting in the days of Noah." In the diagram, "spirits" sits right where the proclamation lands, with "disobedient" and "in prison" hanging off it as descriptions. Elsewhere in the New Testament, a bare plural "spirits" like this usually names spirit-beings rather than deceased people — but the mention of Noah's generation right alongside it is exactly why careful readers land in more than one place. This is one of the most argued-over corners of the New Testament; see the debate summary below for a fair account of the main readings.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-spirits-in-prison].caution -->
Fair, careful readers disagree here. The grammar narrows the options — it does not, by itself, hand you a name for who these "spirits" are.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — Baptism now saves you — with Peter's own qualifier

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-baptism-now-saves].title -->
Baptism now saves you — with Peter's own qualifier
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-baptism-now-saves].body -->
Verse 21 makes its own turn: "which" now reaches back further, to the flood water that carried Noah's family to safety, and Peter says [[baptisma]] "now saves you." But he immediately qualifies his own sentence with a Greek "not … but" (οὐ … ἀλλά, ou … alla): not a washing that removes dirt from the body, but [[eperotema]] — an appeal, or pledge, of a good conscience toward God, grounded in the resurrection of Jesus Christ. The diagram draws that qualifier as an apposition riding right alongside "baptism" — Peter is telling you, in his own grammar, what he does and does not mean.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-baptism-now-saves].implication -->
The apposition sits in the sentence itself, not added by a later commentator: Peter narrows what he means by "baptism now saves you" in the very clause where he says it.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-baptism-now-saves].caution -->
The present-tense "saves" describes an ongoing reality Peter affirms — it is not, by itself, a claim about exactly how, or apart from what, baptism saves. His own next words are the explanation.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — Every power made subject to him

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-gone-into-heaven].title -->
Every power made subject to him
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-gone-into-heaven].body -->
The passage closes on ascension: Christ "is at the right hand of God," having gone into heaven, with angels and authorities and powers [[hypotagenton]] — "having been subjected" — to him. However you read "the spirits in prison," the sentence's last word is Christ's supremacy over every spiritual power. The diagram lets you see that this triumphant close is its own clause, connected to the proclamation of verse 19 by the letter's argument, but grammatically standing on its own.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: steps[step-gone-into-heaven].implication -->
Whatever position you take on verse 19, the letter's argument arrives at the same place: Christ reigns over every power, visible or invisible. That is the pastoral comfort Peter is building toward for a suffering church.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### πνεύματι · pneumati

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumati].surface -->
πνεύματι
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumati].transliteration -->
pneumati
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumati].lemma -->
πνεῦμα
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumati].gloss -->
in [the] spirit
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumati].parsing -->
noun, dative neuter singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumati].explanation -->
Paired with "in the flesh" (σαρκί, sarki) by μὲν … δὲ (men … de, "on the one hand … on the other"): Christ was put to death in one sphere and made alive in this one. Dative case here marks the sphere or respect in which each verb happens.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumati].implication -->
This word's case and number (dative, neuter, singular) match the relative pronoun that opens the very next sentence — one real clue, among several, that readers use when deciding what "in which" refers to.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumati].caution -->
A grammatical match is a genuine clue, not a proof. Good readers weigh it alongside the passage's wider argument.
<!-- @end -->

### ᾧ · hō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ho].surface -->
ᾧ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ho].transliteration -->
hō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ho].lemma -->
ὅς
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ho].gloss -->
which / whom
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ho].parsing -->
relative pronoun, dative neuter singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ho].explanation -->
A relative pronoun with no antecedent stated inside its own clause — Greek regularly lets a relative pronoun reach back across a sentence boundary, which is exactly what makes "in which" (or "in whom") such a discussed phrase here.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ho].implication -->
This is where the whole debate over verse 19 begins grammatically: the pronoun itself is plain; what it points back to is the interpretive fork.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ho].caution -->
The pronoun's form does not, by itself, name its antecedent. That is decided by context and theology, not by this word alone.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐκήρυξεν · ekēryxen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ekeryxen].surface -->
ἐκήρυξεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ekeryxen].transliteration -->
ekēryxen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ekeryxen].lemma -->
κηρύσσω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ekeryxen].gloss -->
he proclaimed / preached
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ekeryxen].parsing -->
aorist active indicative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ekeryxen].explanation -->
The main verb of the sentence: Christ "went" and proclaimed. This verb means to herald or announce; unlike some other New Testament preaching verbs, it does not by itself specify the content of the announcement.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[ekeryxen].implication -->
Because this verb alone does not say what was proclaimed, readers reasonably differ over whether it was good news, judgment, or victory being announced.
<!-- @end -->

### πνεύμασιν · pneumasin

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumasin].surface -->
πνεύμασιν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumasin].transliteration -->
pneumasin
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumasin].lemma -->
πνεῦμα
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumasin].gloss -->
spirits
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumasin].parsing -->
noun, dative neuter plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumasin].explanation -->
The recipients of the proclamation, described as "in prison" and "having disobeyed" long ago, in Noah's day. Elsewhere in the New Testament, a bare plural like this usually names spirit-beings rather than deceased people.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumasin].implication -->
This word is the heart of the debate over who Christ proclaimed to — see the debate summary for a fair account of the main readings.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[pneumasin].caution -->
The plural noun by itself does not name who these "spirits" are; that call is made from the wider context, not from the word's grammar.
<!-- @end -->

### βάπτισμα · baptisma

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[baptisma].surface -->
βάπτισμα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[baptisma].transliteration -->
baptisma
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[baptisma].lemma -->
βάπτισμα
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[baptisma].gloss -->
baptism
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[baptisma].parsing -->
noun, nominative neuter singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[baptisma].explanation -->
The grammatical subject of "now saves you." The diagram shows an adjective ("corresponding," ἀντίτυπον, antitypon, echoing the flood water of the previous sentence) and a qualifying phrase both attached directly to this word.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[baptisma].implication -->
Because Peter attaches his own qualifier to this very word in this very sentence, the diagram lets you see exactly how far his claim reaches, and where he limits it himself.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐπερώτημα · eperōtēma

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[eperotema].surface -->
ἐπερώτημα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[eperotema].transliteration -->
eperōtēma
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[eperotema].lemma -->
ἐπερώτημα
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[eperotema].gloss -->
a pledge, an appeal
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[eperotema].parsing -->
noun, nominative neuter singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[eperotema].explanation -->
Peter's own definition of what baptism is, set in apposition to "baptism" itself: not a bodily washing, but an appeal (or pledge) of a good conscience toward God, resting on Christ's resurrection.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[eperotema].implication -->
This is Peter's own guardrail against reading "baptism now saves you" as a claim about water or ritual acting on its own.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[eperotema].caution -->
Interpreters still debate the exact nuance of this rare word (an appeal made to God, or a pledge made toward him) — both readings keep Peter's point that it is a matter of the conscience and of faith, not of the body.
<!-- @end -->

### ὑποταγέντων · hypotagentōn

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[hypotagenton].surface -->
ὑποταγέντων
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[hypotagenton].transliteration -->
hypotagentōn
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[hypotagenton].lemma -->
ὑποτάσσω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[hypotagenton].gloss -->
having been subjected
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[hypotagenton].parsing -->
aorist passive participle, genitive masculine plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[hypotagenton].explanation -->
Describes "angels, authorities, and powers" as now subject to the ascended Christ. The construction stands apart from its clause, describing a circumstance surrounding Christ's exaltation.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-peter-3-18-22 :: greekTerms[hypotagenton].implication -->
Whichever reading of "spirits in prison" you hold, this closing image is where the passage lands: every spiritual power, however you have identified it, is subject to Christ.
<!-- @end -->
