# Acts 2:38 — repent, all of you; be baptized, each one

<!-- guide guide-acts-2-38 · Acts 2:38 · advanced -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: title -->
Acts 2:38 — repent, all of you; be baptized, each one
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: reference -->
Acts 2:38
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: summary -->
Peter calls the crowd to repent, be baptized, and receive forgiveness.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: devotionalFrame -->
Peter's answer to a stunned crowd is short, but its grammar is doing careful work: one command in the plural, one in the singular, and a promise in the future tense — all held together by a tiny preposition Christians have debated for centuries. Read the diagram slowly. It will not settle every question, but it will show you precisely what is, and is not, being claimed.
<!-- @end -->

**Confessional frame**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: confessionalFrame -->
Reformed and Anglican teaching typically reads this verse alongside the rest of Acts (where forgiveness is repeatedly tied to believing — e.g. Acts 10:43; 16:31) and holds that baptism here functions as a sign and seal of the forgiveness received through repentance and faith, publicly marking what God has done, rather than a ritual that produces forgiveness apart from faith. Other traditions weigh the same grammar differently; this note names one confessional reading, not the only faithful one.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: debateSummary.issue -->
What does the prepositional phrase εἰς ἄφεσιν τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν (eis aphesin tōn hamartiōn, "for/unto the forgiveness of sins") attach to, and what relationship does it describe — is baptism the means, the accompanying sign, or the occasion of forgiveness? The identical phrase εἰς ἄφεσιν ἁμαρτιῶν (eis aphesin hamartiōn) appears in Matthew 26:28, where Jesus says his blood is poured out "for the forgiveness of sins" — a use everyone agrees does not make the blood a mechanical ritual cause, which shapes how interpreters weigh the same preposition here.
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
Causal/telic, tied to the whole call
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
εἰς ἄφεσιν τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν (eis aphesin tōn hamartiōn, "for the forgiveness of sins") describes the goal of the whole compound response Peter has just commanded — repenting AND being baptized together — not of baptism considered on its own. Forgiveness is the outcome toward which the entire turn to Christ, professed and enacted, is headed.
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: debateSummary.views[0].cautions[0] -->
The diagram attaches this phrase directly to βαπτισθήτω (baptisthētō, "let him be baptized"), the nearer verb, so this reading has to argue past the immediate syntax — from the flow of the passage and Peter's other sermons — rather than from this clause alone.
<!-- @end -->

### View 2

**View 2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: debateSummary.views[1].label -->
"With a view to" / "because of" forgiveness (the disputed causal εἰς)
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: debateSummary.views[1].summary -->
εἰς (eis, "into, unto, for") + accusative can mark purpose or result ("so that you receive forgiveness") or, some argue, a backward-looking basis ("because forgiveness has already been granted") — the same debate surrounds εἰς in Matthew 26:28. Both uses are grammatically possible; which one is meant here cannot be read off the preposition by itself.
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: debateSummary.views[1].cautions[0] -->
A purely retrospective/causal εἰς (eis, "into, unto, for") is contested by many grammarians as a rare use, hard to establish from other examples in Acts.
<!-- @end -->

### View 3

**View 3 — label**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: debateSummary.views[2].label -->
Baptism as the appointed sign accompanying the repentance that receives forgiveness
<!-- @end -->

**View 3 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: debateSummary.views[2].summary -->
On this reading, repentance is where forgiveness is received by faith, and baptism is the God-appointed public sign and seal that goes with it — close enough together, in the sentence and in the event, that Peter can describe them with one shared purpose clause without making baptism itself the cause of forgiveness.
<!-- @end -->

**View 3 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: debateSummary.views[2].cautions[0] -->
This view has to explain why βαπτισθήτω (baptisthētō, "be baptized"), not μετανοήσατε (metanoēsate, "repent"), is the verb nearest εἰς ἄφεσιν (eis aphesin, "for/unto forgiveness") in the Greek word order — a fair question it answers from theology found elsewhere in Acts and the epistles, not from this verse's syntax alone.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
εἰς (eis, "into, unto, for") + accusative is genuinely flexible — purpose, result, reference, and (more rarely argued) cause are all attested uses of the construction. The lowfat parse attaches the phrase to βαπτισθήτω (baptisthētō, "be baptized") because it is the nearest verb, which narrows the search but does not by itself decide whether that attachment means forgiveness is baptism's goal, its accompanying sign, or something else baptism merely marks. That is a question the wider witness of the New Testament — not one prepositional phrase — has to help answer.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — Peter's answer: three linked actions

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-overview].title -->
Peter's answer: three linked actions
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-overview].body -->
Peter turns to the crowd and answers with three actions held together by "and": [[metanoesate]] ("repent"), [[baptistheto]] ("be baptized"), and — at the end of the verse — "you will receive" the gift of the Spirit. The diagram lays out all three as coordinate clauses, joined at the same level. Before looking closely at any one word, notice the shape: this is one connected answer, not three separate demands.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-overview].implication -->
Repentance, baptism, and the gift of the Spirit are not separate transactions to manage one at a time — Peter presents them as one connected answer to one question: "what shall we do?"
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — "Repent" (all of you) ... "be baptized" (each one)

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-number-shift].title -->
"Repent" (all of you) ... "be baptized" (each one)
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-number-shift].body -->
Look closely at the two commands, and a small grammatical detail becomes striking: [[metanoesate]] is second-person PLURAL — "repent, all of you," spoken to the whole crowd together. But [[baptistheto]] shifts to third-person SINGULAR, with its own subject [[hekastos]] ("each one") — "let each one of you be baptized." The diagram draws this shift as two separate clauses, each with its own baseline: the plural command stands alone, and the singular command has [[hekastos]] standing on the subject side of its own line.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-number-shift].implication -->
The call to repent lands on the crowd as a crowd; the call to be baptized lands on each person one at a time. The grammar itself carries a pastoral point: the whole company repents together, but each person meets the water individually — no one is baptized simply by being part of a crowd.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-number-shift].caution -->
This is a shift in grammatical number, not a change in urgency or importance. Luke is not saying repentance matters less because its verb is plural — he is describing two moments of one response: together, then one by one.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — "For the forgiveness of sins" — where does it attach?

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-eis-aphesin].title -->
"For the forgiveness of sins" — where does it attach?
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-eis-aphesin].body -->
The phrase [[eis]] [[aphesin]] τῶν ἁμαρτιῶν (tōn hamartiōn) ("for/unto the forgiveness of sins") comes right after the baptism command, and the diagram draws it as a prepositional phrase hanging beneath [[baptistheto]] itself — the nearest verb it can attach to. This is exactly the phrase Christians have long debated: does the grammar mean baptism causes forgiveness, describes its accompanying sign, or names the goal of the whole response Peter has just called for?
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-eis-aphesin].implication -->
However you read the attachment, the sentence keeps forgiveness tied to a real, embodied response, not an abstract feeling. See the debate note attached to this guide for a fair summary of the major readings — the diagram shows you what the Greek actually connects, which is the necessary first step, not the last one.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-eis-aphesin].caution -->
The syntax narrows the question — it attaches this phrase to the nearby verb — but attachment is not the same as causation. Where a phrase is grammatically hooked and what relationship it describes are two different questions.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — "In the name of Jesus Christ" — the same construction, different work

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-in-the-name].title -->
"In the name of Jesus Christ" — the same construction, different work
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-in-the-name].body -->
Just before εἰς ἄφεσιν (eis aphesin, "for/unto forgiveness"), another prepositional phrase attaches to that very same verb [[baptistheto]]: ἐπὶ τῷ [[onomati]] Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ (epi tō onomati Iēsou Christou), "in the name of Jesus Christ." Seeing both phrases in the diagram, stemmed beneath the same verb, is a reminder that Peter names two things about this baptism in the same breath: whose name it invokes, and what it looks toward. Neither phrase competes with the other — the diagram simply shows both hanging from the one act.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-in-the-name].implication -->
Baptism "in the name of Jesus Christ" ties this command to the whole Pentecost sermon's argument: it is Jesus, crucified and raised, whom the crowd is responding to — not baptism as a generic ritual.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — And you will receive

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-promised-gift].title -->
And you will receive
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-promised-gift].body -->
The verse does not end with a command — it ends with a promise: καὶ (kai, "and") [[lempsesthe]] τὴν δωρεὰν τοῦ ἁγίου πνεύματος (tēn dōrean tou hagiou pneumatos), "and you will receive the gift of the Holy Spirit." The diagram shows this as a third clause coordinated with the first two, but its verb is future indicative, not imperative — a statement of what God will do, not another thing the crowd must do. Repent, be baptized... and here is what follows.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: steps[step-promised-gift].implication -->
The sentence's own shape preaches: two calls to respond, then one promise of gift. Whatever place baptism has in this verse, it stands beside a promise that is God's to keep, not the hearers' to earn.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### Μετανοήσατε · metanoēsate

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[metanoesate].surface -->
Μετανοήσατε
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[metanoesate].transliteration -->
metanoēsate
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[metanoesate].lemma -->
μετανοέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[metanoesate].gloss -->
Repent
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[metanoesate].parsing -->
aorist active imperative, 2nd person plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[metanoesate].explanation -->
The first command, addressed to the whole crowd together — a 2nd-person plural aorist imperative. The aorist presents the turning as a whole, decisive act; it is not a claim about how long the turning takes or how many times it must happen.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[metanoesate].implication -->
It is issued to the crowd as a crowd: "all of you, turn."
<!-- @end -->

### βαπτισθήτω · baptisthētō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[baptistheto].surface -->
βαπτισθήτω
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[baptistheto].transliteration -->
baptisthētō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[baptistheto].lemma -->
βαπτίζω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[baptistheto].gloss -->
let him be baptized / be baptized
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[baptistheto].parsing -->
aorist passive imperative, 3rd person singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[baptistheto].explanation -->
A 3rd-person singular passive imperative — literally "let each one be baptized." The passive voice matters: the verb does not say "baptize yourselves," but pictures each person receiving baptism from someone else, as happened at Pentecost.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[baptistheto].caution -->
Passive voice tells you who acts (someone baptizes each person); it does not by itself answer every question about what baptism accomplishes — see the debate note attached to this guide.
<!-- @end -->

### ἕκαστος · hekastos

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[hekastos].surface -->
ἕκαστος
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[hekastos].transliteration -->
hekastos
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[hekastos].lemma -->
ἕκαστος
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[hekastos].gloss -->
each one / every one
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[hekastos].parsing -->
adjective used substantivally, nominative masculine singular (subject of βαπτισθήτω)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[hekastos].explanation -->
"Each one" stands as the singular subject of βαπτισθήτω (baptisthētō, "be baptized"), in deliberate contrast to the plural crowd addressed by μετανοήσατε (metanoēsate, "repent"). It personalizes a general command into an individual one.
<!-- @end -->

### εἰς · eis

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[eis].surface -->
εἰς
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[eis].transliteration -->
eis
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[eis].lemma -->
εἰς
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[eis].gloss -->
into, unto, for
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[eis].parsing -->
preposition governing the accusative
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[eis].explanation -->
"Into," "unto," or "for" — a preposition that can mark purpose or result, reference, or (more rarely and more controversially) a backward-looking cause, depending on context. Here it governs the accusative noun ἄφεσιν (aphesin, "forgiveness").
<!-- @end -->

### ἄφεσιν · aphesin

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[aphesin].surface -->
ἄφεσιν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[aphesin].transliteration -->
aphesin
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[aphesin].lemma -->
ἄφεσις
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[aphesin].gloss -->
forgiveness, release
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[aphesin].parsing -->
noun, accusative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[aphesin].explanation -->
"Forgiveness" — literally a "release" or "letting go." The same phrase, εἰς ἄφεσιν ἁμαρτιῶν (eis aphesin hamartiōn, "for the forgiveness of sins"), describes what Christ's blood is poured out for in Matthew 26:28, a parallel worth holding alongside this verse.
<!-- @end -->

### ὀνόματι · onomati

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[onomati].surface -->
ὀνόματι
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[onomati].transliteration -->
onomati
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[onomati].lemma -->
ὄνομα
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[onomati].gloss -->
name
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[onomati].parsing -->
noun, dative neuter singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[onomati].explanation -->
"Name" — the dative object of ἐπί (epi), "in/on the name." Invoking Jesus' name over the baptism ties the act to him specifically, distinguishing it from John's baptism mentioned earlier in Acts.
<!-- @end -->

### λήμψεσθε · lēmpsesthe

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[lempsesthe].surface -->
λήμψεσθε
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[lempsesthe].transliteration -->
lēmpsesthe
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[lempsesthe].lemma -->
λαμβάνω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[lempsesthe].gloss -->
you will receive
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[lempsesthe].parsing -->
future middle indicative, 2nd person plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-38 :: greekTerms[lempsesthe].explanation -->
Future middle indicative — a promise, not a command. After two imperatives, the sentence turns to what God will do for those who respond.
<!-- @end -->

# Acts 2:39 — the promise, and its covenant echo

<!-- guide guide-acts-2-39 · Acts 2:38–39 · Genesis 17:12 · intermediate · discourse guide -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: title -->
Acts 2:39 — the promise, and its covenant echo
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: reference -->
Acts 2:38–39 · Genesis 17:12
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: summary -->
See Peter's "repent and be baptized… to you and your children" beside the Abrahamic covenant formula it echoes — four color-coded correspondences, read side by side.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: devotionalFrame -->
Peter calls the crowd to repent and be baptized, then tells them WHY: "to you is the promise, and to your children, and to all that are afar off." Those words are not new. Laid beside Genesis 17, they echo the covenant God made with Abraham — sign given, "to you and to your offspring… even the foreigner in your household." This guide loads both passages together, in the ASV English translation, and highlights four matching phrases in turn so you can watch the promise — and its sign — carried forward and thrown open wider.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: debateSummary.issue -->
Who are the "you and your children" of the promise, and does the parallel with circumcision settle who should be baptized? The verse fixes the recipients — the hearers, their children, and all who are far off — and bounds them with "as many as the Lord our God calls." It does not, in its own words, state who should be baptized; that step draws on how one reads the covenant sign across both Testaments.
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
A covenantal (paedobaptist) reading
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
Peter's "to you and to your children" repeats the covenant formula spoken to Abraham, where the sign fell on households, children included. On this reading baptism is the new-covenant counterpart to circumcision, and the promise still runs to believers together with their children — so baptism is rightly given to them, as circumcision was.
<!-- @end -->

### View 2

**View 2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: debateSummary.views[1].label -->
A credobaptist reading
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: debateSummary.views[1].summary -->
"Your children" and "all that are afar off" describe the promise's wide REACH across generations and nations, while the closing clause — "as many as the Lord our God calls" — marks its actual recipients as those who are called and, answering, repent and believe. Baptism, on this reading, is not simply circumcision's replacement but the sign given to those who make that response.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
The text settles this much outright: the promise is addressed to households and nations together, its reach defined by God's calling, and its call to repent-and-be-baptized stands right beside it. What it does not settle by wording alone is whether baptism functions as circumcision's exact new-covenant counterpart, or the subjects and mode of baptism more broadly. The covenant echo with Genesis 17 — sign and promise both — is genuine and worth weighing; how much it decides depends on how one reads the continuity and newness of the covenant, which is exactly where faithful readers still differ.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — A promise, addressed to a household

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-the-promise].title -->
A promise, addressed to a household
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-the-promise].body -->
The two passages are laid out together — Genesis 17:12 above, Acts 2:38–39 below. Start with Peter's words in Acts: he calls the crowd to "repent, and be baptized… unto the remission of your sins," then names the promise's recipients as a series — "to you is the promise, and to your children, and to all that are afar off" — not isolated individuals who decide, but a household and, beyond it, the nations. The very word "promise" is covenant language: something God has pledged and will keep. Then look up to the Genesis covenant sign it echoes.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-the-promise].implication -->
The gospel here is first a promise, attached to a sign (baptism), and a promise is directed — it is FOR someone. The four highlighted pairs below trace who.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — "You" ↔ "you" (blue)

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-you].title -->
"You" ↔ "you" (blue)
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-you].body -->
The blue highlight links "to you is the promise" (Acts 2:39) with "among you" in the covenant sign given to Abraham's household (Genesis 17:12). Both address the hearer directly, first of all: the promise — like the sign before it — starts with the people standing right there.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-you].implication -->
The promise is not addressed to strangers in the abstract; it opens by naming the very people listening.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — "Your children" ↔ "your generations" (green)

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-children].title -->
"Your children" ↔ "your generations" (green)
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-children].body -->
The green highlight links "and to your children" (Acts 2:39) with "throughout your generations" (Genesis 17:12). Both carry the promise forward in time, past the first hearers, to the households that come after them.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-children].implication -->
The promise is not a one-generation event; both texts build in continuity across time.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — "All that are afar off" ↔ "the stranger… not of thy seed" (orange)

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-afar-off].title -->
"All that are afar off" ↔ "the stranger… not of thy seed" (orange)
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-afar-off].body -->
The orange highlight links "and to all that are afar off" (Acts 2:39) with "any stranger, that is not of thy seed" (Genesis 17:12). Both texts widen the promise's reach beyond the immediate family — to the Gentile nations in Acts, to the household-adopted foreigner in Genesis.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-afar-off].implication -->
Even in Genesis 17, the covenant sign was never ONLY for physical descendants; Acts 2 widens the same reach further still.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — "Be baptized" ↔ "be circumcised" (purple) — where readers differ

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-sign].title -->
"Be baptized" ↔ "be circumcised" (purple) — where readers differ
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-sign].body -->
The purple highlight links "be baptized" (Acts 2:38) with "shall be circumcised" (Genesis 17:12) — the two SIGNS themselves, not just the promise surrounding them. This is a PROPOSED correspondence, drawn here for you to weigh, not a settled equation: it is exactly the question the paedobaptist and credobaptist readings below answer differently.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-sign].implication -->
If baptism functions as the new-covenant counterpart to circumcision, that shapes who should receive it and when — which is precisely where "Where readers differ" applies.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-pair-sign].caution -->
A shared color here shows a PROPOSED parallel between two signs in two different covenants, in English translation — it is not a claim about the underlying Greek and Hebrew syntax, and it does not by itself settle the baptism debate.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 6 — What the display shows — and what it does not

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-what-a-diagram-shows].title -->
What the display shows — and what it does not
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-what-a-diagram-shows].body -->
The Discourse view shows four ADDRESS-and-sign correspondences matching across the two passages — the covenant's reach, from household to foreigner to "all that are afar off," and the proposed link between the two signs. What it cannot do is settle, on its own, who should be baptized or when. That question turns on how one reads the newness of the new covenant across the whole of Scripture, and faithful readers weigh it differently.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-what-a-diagram-shows].implication -->
Four parallels are real evidence and worth seeing plainly. But a diagram opens a question; it rarely closes a doctrine by itself.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: steps[step-what-a-diagram-shows].caution -->
The links drawn here are covenant ECHOES in wording, shown as proposed parallels in English translation — not a claim about the underlying Greek and Hebrew syntax, and not a proof-text for either side of the baptism debate.
<!-- @end -->

## Discourse ranges & sample arcs

**Range 1 — section label**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.ranges[0].label -->
Genesis 17:12 (ASV)
<!-- @end -->

**Range 1 — fallback notice**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.ranges[0].fallback.notice -->
The ASV text for Genesis 17:12 could not be fetched, so the bundled BSB translation is shown instead.
<!-- @end -->

**Range 2 — section label**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.ranges[1].label -->
Acts 2:38–39 (ASV)
<!-- @end -->

**Range 2 — fallback notice**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.ranges[1].fallback.notice -->
The ASV text for Acts 2:38–39 could not be fetched, so the bundled BSB translation is shown instead.
<!-- @end -->

**Arc 2:39/1 ↔ 17:12/2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.seededArcs[ga_acts239_you].label -->
you ↔ you
<!-- @end -->

**Arc 2:39/1 ↔ 17:12/2 — notes**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.seededArcs[ga_acts239_you].notes -->
"to you is the promise" addresses the very hearers, echoing "among you" in the Genesis sign.
<!-- @end -->

**Arc 2:39/2 ↔ 17:12/3 — label**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.seededArcs[ga_acts239_children].label -->
children ↔ generations
<!-- @end -->

**Arc 2:39/2 ↔ 17:12/3 — notes**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.seededArcs[ga_acts239_children].notes -->
"to your children" carries the promise forward exactly as "throughout your generations" carries the sign.
<!-- @end -->

**Arc 2:39/3 ↔ 17:12/4 — label**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.seededArcs[ga_acts239_far_off].label -->
afar off ↔ the stranger
<!-- @end -->

**Arc 2:39/3 ↔ 17:12/4 — notes**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.seededArcs[ga_acts239_far_off].notes -->
"all that are afar off" widens the promise the same way the sign already reached "any stranger… not of thy seed."
<!-- @end -->

**Arc 2:38/2 ↔ 17:12/1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.seededArcs[ga_acts239_sign].label -->
baptized ↔ circumcised
<!-- @end -->

**Arc 2:38/2 ↔ 17:12/1 — notes**
<!-- @field guide-acts-2-39 :: discourse.seededArcs[ga_acts239_sign].notes -->
A PROPOSED correspondence between the two signs — exactly the question the two readings below answer differently.
<!-- @end -->

# Romans 6:3–4 — baptized into his death

<!-- guide guide-romans-6-3-4 · Romans 6:3–4 · intermediate -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: title -->
Romans 6:3–4 — baptized into his death
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: reference -->
Romans 6:3–4
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: summary -->
Paul pictures baptism as union with Christ's death and new life.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: devotionalFrame -->
These two verses are one connected argument, and the Greek grammar carries it plainly: the same “into” shows up twice, and the next verse opens with a single word built from “with” + “bury.” Follow the diagram and watch how the sentence itself insists that what happened to Christ has happened, grammatically and really, to us.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — A question everyone should already know the answer to

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-do-you-not-know].title -->
A question everyone should already know the answer to
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-do-you-not-know].body -->
Paul begins with a rhetorical question: “ἢ ἀγνοεῖτε ὅτι…” (ē agnoeite hoti…) — “Or do you not know that…?” The diagram puts [[agnoeite]] on the main baseline, with the whole ὅτι-clause (hoti, “that”) hanging beneath it as its direct object. Paul is not introducing new teaching here; he is reminding the Romans of something baptism itself already pictures, if they think through what it means.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-do-you-not-know].implication -->
Before we look at any individual word, the shape of the sentence tells us something: what follows is common ground, not a new argument. Paul is drawing out a meaning already built into the practice of baptism.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — Baptized into Christ Jesus

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-into-christ].title -->
Baptized into Christ Jesus
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-into-christ].body -->
Look at the first clause inside the ὅτι (hoti, “that”): “ὅσοι ἐβαπτίσθημεν εἰς Χριστὸν Ἰησοῦν” (hosoi ebaptisthēmen eis Christon Iēsoun) — “as many of us as were baptized into Christ Jesus.” The verb [[ebaptisthemen]] is passive: something was done TO us. And the preposition [[eis]] with the accusative does not just mean “in connection with” Christ — it pictures baptism as a movement INTO him, into union with his person.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-into-christ].implication -->
“Baptized into Christ” uses the same grammar you would use for water poured into a jar, or a river running into the sea. Paul reaches for that picture because union — not merely imitation or agreement — is exactly his point: to be baptized is to be joined to Christ himself.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-into-christ].caution -->
The passive and the aorist together simply say something was done to us, viewed as a whole event; they do not, by themselves, spell out every detail of how or when that union takes hold in a believer's life.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — Baptized into his death

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-into-his-death].title -->
Baptized into his death
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-into-his-death].body -->
The sentence is not finished. The whole clause you just traced — “we who were baptized into Christ Jesus” — turns out to be the grammatical SUBJECT of a second baptism: “…were baptized into his death.” The diagram shows this plainly: the subject beneath the second baptismal clause is not a single word but that entire earlier clause. And the preposition repeats — the same [[eis]] + accusative pattern that carried us into Christ now carries us into his death, made personal by the pronoun αὐτοῦ (autou), “his.”
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-into-his-death].implication -->
Union with Christ is not vague in this sentence. The grammar insists that being joined to Christ MEANS being joined to what happened to him — his death included. You cannot have the one without the other.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-into-his-death].caution -->
This is grammar picturing union, not a manual explaining exactly how or when that union takes effect in a person's experience. Paul builds the theology elsewhere; here the sentence simply insists the two baptisms — into Christ, into his death — are one and the same act.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — Buried with him

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-buried-with].title -->
Buried with him
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-buried-with].body -->
Verse 4 opens with a single Greek word built from a preposition and a verb: [[synetaphemen]], “we were buried together with.” The σύν- (syn-, “with”) prefix is doing real grammatical work — it is why the next word, [[auto]] (“him”), stands in the dative case, the case Greek uses for accompaniment. The diagram hangs both “with him” and the phrase “through baptism into death” beneath this one verb, all describing a single, shared burial.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-buried-with].implication -->
The prefix alone preaches: our burial was never a solo experience. Whatever happened to Christ in his burial, the grammar says, happened to us together WITH him.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-buried-with].caution -->
The aorist passive here presents "we were buried" as one simple, whole event — much as English often does too. It does not, by itself, specify a moment or a mechanism; Paul's point is union with Christ, not a step-by-step account of how baptism accomplishes it.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — So that we might walk in newness of life

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-newness-of-life].title -->
So that we might walk in newness of life
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-newness-of-life].body -->
The verse now leans toward a goal: [[hina]] (“so that”) introduces the purpose of being buried with Christ. Inside that purpose clause Paul builds a comparison with ὥσπερ … οὕτως καί (hōsper … houtōs kai, “just as … so also”): just as Christ was raised from the dead by the Father's glory, so also we might [[peripatesomen]] — “walk” — in newness of life. The diagram lays the comparison out visibly: the resurrection clause supports the “just as,” and “we … might walk” carries the “so also,” both hanging beneath the same purpose clause.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-newness-of-life].implication -->
This is the destination of the whole grammar hook: union with Christ in his death and burial is never presented as an end in itself. It aims at a new way of living — walking, day by day, in a genuinely new kind of life, patterned after resurrection rather than death.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: steps[step-newness-of-life].caution -->
[[peripatesomen]] is subjunctive, not indicative — the mood itself marks this as a goal to pursue, not a report that it happens automatically. And as with the earlier aorists in these verses, resist turning "were baptized," "were buried," or "was raised" into a precise timeline: Paul's point in this passage is union with Christ and its moral consequence — a new manner of life — not a mechanical theory of exactly how or when baptism accomplishes that union.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### ἀγνοεῖτε · agnoeite

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[agnoeite].surface -->
ἀγνοεῖτε
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[agnoeite].transliteration -->
agnoeite
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[agnoeite].lemma -->
ἀγνοέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[agnoeite].gloss -->
are you unaware / do you not know
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[agnoeite].parsing -->
present active indicative, 2nd plural (in a negated rhetorical question)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[agnoeite].explanation -->
From ἀ- (a-, "not") + a root meaning "to know" — literally "to not know, to be ignorant of." Paired with the rhetorical ἢ (ē, "or"), it expects the answer "of course we know."
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[agnoeite].implication -->
Paul treats what follows as common Christian knowledge, not new teaching — something the meaning of baptism already tells us once we think it through.
<!-- @end -->

### εἰς · eis

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[eis].surface -->
εἰς
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[eis].transliteration -->
eis
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[eis].lemma -->
εἰς
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[eis].gloss -->
into
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[eis].parsing -->
preposition governing the accusative case
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[eis].explanation -->
εἰς + accusative pictures motion INTO something, not merely being near or associated with it. Paul uses this exact construction twice in one verse — baptized "into Christ Jesus," baptized "into his death" — the same grammar of entry both times.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[eis].implication -->
Translations render this "into" because that is what the case and preposition together actually say: baptism unites believers with Christ, not merely alongside him.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐβαπτίσθημεν · ebaptisthēmen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[ebaptisthemen].surface -->
ἐβαπτίσθημεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[ebaptisthemen].transliteration -->
ebaptisthēmen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[ebaptisthemen].lemma -->
βαπτίζω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[ebaptisthemen].gloss -->
we were baptized
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[ebaptisthemen].parsing -->
aorist passive indicative, 1st plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[ebaptisthemen].explanation -->
Passive voice: this is something done TO believers, not something they perform themselves. The aorist views the baptizing as a simple, whole event rather than describing its inner stages.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[ebaptisthemen].caution -->
The aorist does not mean "once for all" by itself — it simply views the action as a complete whole. Its theological weight here comes from Paul's argument, not from the tense form alone.
<!-- @end -->

### συνετάφημεν · synetaphēmen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[synetaphemen].surface -->
συνετάφημεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[synetaphemen].transliteration -->
synetaphēmen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[synetaphemen].lemma -->
συνθάπτομαι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[synetaphemen].gloss -->
we were buried together with
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[synetaphemen].parsing -->
aorist passive indicative, 1st plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[synetaphemen].explanation -->
Built from σύν (syn, "with, together") + a root meaning "to bury." The σύν- prefix is not decorative — compounds like this are one of Paul's regular ways of naming believers' shared experience with Christ.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[synetaphemen].implication -->
One word does double duty: it names an action (burial) and a companion (Christ) at the same time.
<!-- @end -->

### αὐτῷ · autō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[auto].surface -->
αὐτῷ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[auto].transliteration -->
autō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[auto].lemma -->
αὐτός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[auto].gloss -->
with him
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[auto].parsing -->
dative masculine singular pronoun
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[auto].explanation -->
A dative of association ("with him"), called for by the σύν- (syn-, "with") built into συνετάφημεν (synetaphēmen, "we were buried together with"). The case quietly confirms what the compound verb already says: this burial has a companion.
<!-- @end -->

### ἵνα · hina

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[hina].surface -->
ἵνα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[hina].transliteration -->
hina
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[hina].lemma -->
ἵνα
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[hina].gloss -->
so that
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[hina].parsing -->
subordinating conjunction introducing a purpose clause
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[hina].explanation -->
ἵνα regularly introduces a clause of purpose or result — the goal something aims at. Here it opens the reason burial-with-Christ matters: not burial for its own sake, but a new way of life on the other side of it.
<!-- @end -->

### περιπατήσωμεν · peripatēsōmen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].surface -->
περιπατήσωμεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].transliteration -->
peripatēsōmen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].lemma -->
περιπατέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].gloss -->
we might walk / should walk
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].parsing -->
aorist active subjunctive, 1st plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].explanation -->
"Walk" is a common biblical image for the pattern of a person's daily conduct. The subjunctive mood marks this as the goal set before believers, matched to Christ's resurrection by the ὥσπερ … οὕτως καί (hōsper … houtōs kai, "just as … so also") comparison earlier in the sentence.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-6-3-4 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].caution -->
Do not read the aorist subjunctive here as one isolated act of walking; the subjunctive mood already signals a purposed goal, and "walking" itself pictures an ongoing pattern of life, not a single event.
<!-- @end -->

---

# Colossians 2:11–12 — circumcision, burial, and baptism

<!-- guide guide-colossians-2-11-12 · Colossians 2:11–12 · advanced -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: title -->
Colossians 2:11–12 — circumcision, burial, and baptism
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: reference -->
Colossians 2:11–12
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: summary -->
Paul links believers' circumcision, burial, and baptism to Christ.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: devotionalFrame -->
Colossians 2 is Paul's answer to teachers who would add rules, festivals, and (behind them) the old covenant's circumcision to the Colossians' faith. His reply is that they already have everything in Christ: a circumcision not made with hands, a burial, and a resurrection — all received in union with him. The grammar of these two verses stacks those images into a single connected shape. Watch the diagram build the argument, and notice, at the end, one place — a single relative pronoun — where careful readers still trace the Greek two different ways.
<!-- @end -->

**Confessional frame**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: confessionalFrame -->
A confessional note (confessional Reformed): this tradition reads Colossians 2:11–12 as one of the clearest New-Testament links between circumcision and baptism — the base tree's own hinge (περιετμήθητε, perietmēthēte, "you were circumcised," governing the "buried with him … in baptism" clause) is taken to show that baptism is the new-covenant sign that answers to the old-covenant circumcision, which grounds the covenantal (including infant-baptism) reading of the sign. This is offered as a labelled conviction, not a claim that the grammar forces it: the passage joins the images without spelling out who receives the sign or when, and the fair summary below includes the credobaptist reading and the "circumcision of Christ = his death" reading that many careful interpreters hold.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: debateSummary.issue -->
Colossians 2:11–12 stacks three images — a circumcision "made without hands," a burial "in baptism," and a raising "through faith." Two lexical questions sit underneath the grammar: what is "the circumcision of Christ," and — at ἐν ᾧ (en hō) in v.12b — what raised you: the baptism just named, or Christ? This guide takes a position on both, and gives the alternates a fair hearing below.
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
"The circumcision of Christ" = the circumcision Christ performs in the believer — this guide's reading
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
A second question sits beneath the antecedent question: what is ἡ περιτομὴ τοῦ Χριστοῦ (hē peritomē tou Christou), "the circumcision of Christ"? This guide reads it as the spiritual circumcision Christ himself performs on the believer at conversion — the cutting away of the old self, worked by union with him, of which the outward, hand-made rite was always only a shadow. The genitive τοῦ Χριστοῦ (tou Christou, "of Christ") names Christ as the one who does the circumcising, and the surrounding "made without hands … putting off the body of the flesh" language fits a work Christ performs IN the believer better than a rite he alone underwent. Some careful readers take the same genitive the other way — as the "circumcision" Christ himself underwent, his death, the stripping off of the body of flesh on the cross. That reading is real and defensible; the genitive by itself does not adjudicate between the two.
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: debateSummary.views[0].cautions[0] -->
The credobaptist reading belongs here too: even where baptism and circumcision are linked as covenant signs, this passage describes an already-believing, already-buried-and-raised people, so it does not by itself settle who should receive baptism or when. That question is argued from the whole of Scripture, not from these two verses alone.
<!-- @end -->

### View 2

**View 2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: debateSummary.views[1].label -->
ἐν ᾧ antecedent = baptism ("in baptism, in which you were also raised") — this guide's reading
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: debateSummary.views[1].summary -->
This guide takes the relative ᾧ (hō) in v.12b to reach back to the nearest noun, βαπτισμῷ (baptismō, "baptism"): "buried with him in baptism, in which you were also raised through faith," and DRAWS it as a relative clause modifying βαπτισμῷ. Burial and resurrection are both pictured as happening in that one baptism — the reading many English versions render ("wherein / in which you were also raised"), the shape the Nestle1904 base tree already draws, and the reading this guide teaches as standard. (The SBLGNT base encodes the same sense differently — as a parallel/appositional link between the two ἐν-phrases, drawn with an "=" in the plain view; the guide normalizes it to the relative clause to make the reading legible.)
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: debateSummary.views[1].cautions[0] -->
Reading the raising as "in baptism" does not by itself make baptism the instrument that raises apart from faith — the same clause immediately grounds the raising "through faith in the working of God." The antecedent question is about reference, not about mechanism.
<!-- @end -->

### View 3

**View 3 — label**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: debateSummary.views[2].label -->
ἐν ᾧ antecedent = Christ ("in whom you were also raised") — the leading alternate
<!-- @end -->

**View 3 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: debateSummary.views[2].summary -->
The leading alternate takes ᾧ (hō) to reach back past baptism to the person of the whole context — the Christ you were buried WITH (συνταφέντες αὐτῷ, syntaphentes autō) and circumcised in (ἐν ᾧ, en hō, v.11a) — so "in whom you were also raised." Greek relative pronouns can reach to a governing person rather than the nearest noun, and v.11a's own ἐν ᾧ (en hō) already refers to Christ, which this reading takes as the pattern for v.12b — a real, defensible construal, argued from the wider context and the v.11a parallel rather than from proximity. The app carries it as the alternate reading; open it on the contested step to see the raised-clause re-attach to "buried with him" instead of to "baptism."
<!-- @end -->

**View 3 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: debateSummary.views[2].cautions[0] -->
The nearest-noun pull toward βαπτισμῷ (baptismō, "baptism") is real — which is why this guide teaches that reading as standard — but the wider-context argument here is a genuine, defensible construal, not a correction of the base.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
The syntax settles a great deal. It draws the hinge plainly — the "buried with him … in baptism" clause hangs off "you were circumcised," so circumcision, burial, and baptism are one connected movement — and this guide reads βαπτισμῷ (baptismō, "baptism") as both the location of the burial AND the antecedent of ἐν ᾧ (en hō): "in baptism, in which you were also raised." What the syntax does not settle by itself is the sense of two words: whether ᾧ (hō) could instead reach past baptism to Christ, and whether "the circumcision of Christ" names his own death or the circumcision he performs in the believer. This guide takes the nearest-noun, believer-centered reading on both counts as the natural sense of the words in context; the alternates above are real and defensible, which is why English translations occasionally render them differently at ἐν ᾧ (en hō).
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — Circumcised — but "made without hands"

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-circumcised-without-hands].title -->
Circumcised — but "made without hands"
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-circumcised-without-hands].body -->
Paul tells the Colossians they were [[peritmethete]] — "you were circumcised" — with a circumcision [[acheiropoieto]], "made without hands." The diagram hangs περιτομῇ (peritomē, "with a circumcision") beneath the verb, and [[acheiropoieto]] slants beneath that noun, qualifying it. The whole phrase turns an outward, physical rite into something God does inwardly. Notice the verb is passive: they did not circumcise themselves — it was done TO them.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-circumcised-without-hands].implication -->
The very first move sets Paul's theme: whatever the false teachers offer through a rite "made with hands," the Colossians already have from God through a circumcision "made without hands."
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-circumcised-without-hands].caution -->
The aorist "you were circumcised" views the act as a whole; it does not by itself say "once-for-all" or pin the moment — the force comes from what Paul pairs it with, not from the tense.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — The circumcision of Christ — his own work in you

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-circumcision-of-christ].title -->
The circumcision of Christ — his own work in you
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-circumcision-of-christ].body -->
The phrase resolves into "in the circumcision [[christou]] — of Christ." The diagram draws [[christou]] as a genitive beneath περιτομῇ (peritomē, "circumcision"), and the whole "in the circumcision of Christ" phrase hangs adverbially beneath "you were circumcised." This is the circumcision Christ himself performs on the believer — the inward cutting-away of the old self, worked by union with him, not a rite he alone underwent on the cross. (Some readers take the genitive the other way, as Christ's own "circumcision" — his death; see this guide's "Where readers differ" for that alternate reading, fairly presented.)
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-circumcision-of-christ].implication -->
This circumcision belongs to Christ, not to the ritual knife — his work, done in the believer by union with him — and that union is where Paul turns next.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-circumcision-of-christ].caution -->
A genitive ("of Christ") only marks a relationship; it does not by itself label which kind. This guide reads the surrounding "made without hands" language as pointing to Christ's work IN the believer rather than to an event he alone underwent — but the genitive alone does not force that reading, which is why "Where readers differ" gives the alternate a fair hearing.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — The hinge: buried with him

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-syntaphentes-hinge].title -->
The hinge: buried with him
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-syntaphentes-hinge].body -->
Now watch the sentence turn. [[syntaphentes]] αὐτῷ (autō) — "having been buried with him" — opens a new clause, and the diagram draws it hanging OFF the verb "you were circumcised" (the highlighted link). That attachment is the whole argument in miniature: the circumcision "made without hands" IS a burial with Christ. The participle's σύν- (syn-, "with/together") prefix ("buried-together-WITH") ties them into one act, and αὐτῷ (autō, "with him") names the one you were buried with. This hinge — circumcision leading straight into burial — is what the base tree already draws, before any reading is chosen.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-syntaphentes-hinge].implication -->
The grammar itself joins circumcision and burial: to be "circumcised without hands" is, in the same breath, to be "buried with Christ." One connected shape carries the whole thought.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — In baptism

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-in-baptism].title -->
In baptism
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-in-baptism].body -->
The burial has a location: [[syntaphentes]] αὐτῷ (autō) ἐν τῷ [[baptismo]] — "buried with him in baptism." The diagram hangs the "in baptism" phrase beneath the participle.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-in-baptism].implication -->
Baptism is where Paul locates the burial with Christ — the visible sign attached to an invisible circumcision "made without hands."
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — Raised — in that same baptism

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-raised-in-whom].title -->
Raised — in that same baptism
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-raised-in-whom].body -->
Then: ἐν ᾧ (en hō) καὶ [[synegerthete]] — "in which you were also raised." This guide takes the relative pronoun ᾧ (hō) back to the nearest noun, βαπτισμῷ (baptismō, "baptism"): "in baptism, in which you were also raised" — and DRAWS it that way. The ἐν ᾧ … συνηγέρθητε clause hangs as a relative clause beneath βαπτισμῷ (the highlighted link), modifying that noun the way any relative clause modifies its antecedent. Burial and resurrection are both pictured as happening in that one baptism, with the raising immediately grounded in faith in God's working. A note on the diagram you are seeing: the two default Greek source trees encode this spot differently. The Nestle1904 tree draws exactly this relative clause; the SBLGNT tree instead encodes the two ἐν-phrases (ἐν τῷ βαπτισμῷ and ἐν ᾧ …) as PARALLEL / APPOSITIONAL — it makes the raised clause the object of the first ἐν and hangs τῷ βαπτισμῷ on it by apposition (shown by the "=" mark in the plain view). Same wording, same "in baptism" sense; this guide simply normalizes the SBLGNT encoding to the relative-clause shape so the reading is easy to see. (Some readers instead take ᾧ (hō) to reach past baptism to Christ himself — "in whom you were also raised," on the pattern of v.11a's own ἐν ᾧ (en hō) = Christ; open "Where readers differ" below to see that alternate reading drawn structurally.) Nothing about the Greek words changes between the readings — only where the pronoun is understood to point, and that question stays genuinely open.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-raised-in-whom].caution -->
The nearest-noun pull toward "baptism" is the grammar's normal default, which is why this guide reads ᾧ (hō) that way and draws it so; the Christ-antecedent reading is a real, defensible alternative some careful readers hold on the pattern of v.11a — see "Where readers differ" for a fair hearing of it. The antecedent question is genuinely open.
<!-- @end -->

**Contested-reading lead-in**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-raised-in-whom].contested.note -->
This step draws the standard "in baptism" reading as a relative clause modifying βαπτισμῷ. The alternate "in whom" (Christ) construal is in the app's alternate-readings registry — open it to preview that reading drawn structurally, with the raised-clause hanging off "buried with him" instead of off "baptism."
<!-- @end -->

### Step 6 — Through faith in the working of God

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-through-faith].title -->
Through faith in the working of God
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-through-faith].body -->
Whichever way you take that pronoun, the clause lands the same way: you were raised διὰ τῆς [[pisteos]] τῆς ἐνεργείας τοῦ [[theou]] (dia tēs pisteōs tēs energeias tou theou) — "through faith in the working of God." The diagram hangs "through faith" beneath the verb, then cascades genitives beneath it: faith — in the working — of God. The power that raises is God's, "who raised Christ from the dead" (the phrase the sentence closes on); the way it is received is faith. Baptism pictures it, but the working belongs to God.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: steps[step-through-faith].implication -->
The sentence that began with a circumcision "made without hands" ends where it was always heading: the working of God, received through faith. Every image — circumcision, burial, baptism, resurrection — hangs on that.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### περιετμήθητε · perietmēthēte

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[peritmethete].surface -->
περιετμήθητε
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[peritmethete].transliteration -->
perietmēthēte
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[peritmethete].lemma -->
περιτέμνω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[peritmethete].gloss -->
you were circumcised
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[peritmethete].parsing -->
aorist passive indicative, 2nd person plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[peritmethete].explanation -->
The chain's opening verb, and it is PASSIVE: the circumcision was done to the Colossians, not by them. Paul immediately calls it "made without hands," moving it from an outward rite to God's inward work.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[peritmethete].caution -->
The aorist views the act as a whole; it does not mean "once-for-all" or fix the moment. What the circumcision IS comes from "made without hands" and "of Christ," not from the tense.
<!-- @end -->

### ἀχειροποιήτῳ · acheiropoiētō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[acheiropoieto].surface -->
ἀχειροποιήτῳ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[acheiropoieto].transliteration -->
acheiropoiētō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[acheiropoieto].lemma -->
ἀχειροποίητος
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[acheiropoieto].gloss -->
made without hands
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[acheiropoieto].parsing -->
adjective, dative feminine singular (agreeing with περιτομῇ)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[acheiropoieto].explanation -->
"Not hand-made" — the same word John's Gospel uses of a temple "not made with hands." It marks this circumcision as God's doing, over against the physical rite the false teachers pressed.
<!-- @end -->

### Χριστοῦ · Christou

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[christou].surface -->
Χριστοῦ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[christou].transliteration -->
Christou
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[christou].lemma -->
Χριστός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[christou].gloss -->
of Christ
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[christou].parsing -->
proper noun, genitive masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[christou].explanation -->
The genitive in "the circumcision of Christ." This guide reads it as the circumcision Christ himself performs in the believer at conversion — the spiritual "cutting away" worked by union with him (some readers instead take it as the circumcision Christ himself underwent, his death; see "Where readers differ").
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[christou].implication -->
This is Christ's own work of union — the believer receives it, rather than performing it, and it comes not through a ritual "made with hands."
<!-- @end -->

### συνταφέντες · syntaphentes

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[syntaphentes].surface -->
συνταφέντες
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[syntaphentes].transliteration -->
syntaphentes
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[syntaphentes].lemma -->
συνθάπτομαι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[syntaphentes].gloss -->
having been buried with
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[syntaphentes].parsing -->
aorist passive participle, nominative masculine plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[syntaphentes].explanation -->
A σύν- (syn-, "with/together") compound — "buried-together-WITH." It opens the clause that the base tree hangs off "you were circumcised," welding circumcision and burial into one act. αὐτῷ (autō, "with him") names Christ as the one buried with.
<!-- @end -->

### βαπτισμῷ · baptismō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[baptismo].surface -->
βαπτισμῷ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[baptismo].transliteration -->
baptismō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[baptismo].lemma -->
βαπτισμός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[baptismo].gloss -->
baptism
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[baptismo].parsing -->
noun, dative masculine singular (object of ἐν)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[baptismo].explanation -->
Where Paul locates the burial with Christ: "in baptism." In the SBLGNT base data this word ships with no gloss at all, so the diagram fills it from its dictionary form (βαπτισμός, baptismos → "baptism") in English-gloss mode. This guide also takes this same noun as the antecedent of ἐν ᾧ (en hō) in v.12b: "in baptism, in which you were also raised" (see "Raised — in that same baptism").
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[baptismo].caution -->
Locating the burial "in baptism" describes where the union is pictured; the same sentence grounds the raising "through faith in the working of God," so the sign and the faith are not played off against each other.
<!-- @end -->

### συνηγέρθητε · synēgerthēte

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[synegerthete].surface -->
συνηγέρθητε
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[synegerthete].transliteration -->
synēgerthēte
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[synegerthete].lemma -->
συνεγείρω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[synegerthete].gloss -->
you were raised with
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[synegerthete].parsing -->
aorist passive indicative, 2nd person plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[synegerthete].explanation -->
Another σύν- (syn-, "with/together") compound and another passive: "you were raised together with [him]." It is the verb governed by ἐν ᾧ (en hō) — read on this guide's standard reading as "in baptism, in which you were also raised" (see "Where readers differ" for the alternate "in whom [Christ]").
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[synegerthete].caution -->
The aorist views the raising as a whole; it is Paul's way of speaking of a settled reality, not a claim about "once-for-all" timing. The open question here is what ᾧ (hō) points to, not what the tense means.
<!-- @end -->

### πίστεως · pisteōs

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[pisteos].surface -->
πίστεως
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[pisteos].transliteration -->
pisteōs
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[pisteos].lemma -->
πίστις
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[pisteos].gloss -->
faith
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[pisteos].parsing -->
noun, genitive feminine singular (object of διά)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[pisteos].explanation -->
"Through faith" — the means by which the raising is received. Whichever way you take ᾧ (hō), the clause immediately names faith as the channel and God's working as the power.
<!-- @end -->

### θεοῦ · theou

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[theou].surface -->
θεοῦ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[theou].transliteration -->
theou
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[theou].lemma -->
θεός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[theou].gloss -->
of God
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[theou].parsing -->
noun, genitive masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-colossians-2-11-12 :: greekTerms[theou].explanation -->
The genitive that anchors "the working of God" — the power that raised the Colossians is the same that "raised Christ from the dead," the phrase the whole sentence closes on.
<!-- @end -->

---

# Romans 8:28–30 — the golden chain

<!-- guide guide-romans-8-28-30 · Romans 8:28–30 · intermediate -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: title -->
Romans 8:28–30 — the golden chain
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: reference -->
Romans 8:28–30
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: summary -->
Paul traces God's golden chain of five verbs from foreknowledge to glory.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: devotionalFrame -->
Romans 8:28 is one of the most-quoted promises in the Bible — and verses 29–30 are the reason Paul dares to make it. "All things work together for good" is not optimism; it rests on a chain of divine acts that runs from before creation to beyond the end. Watch the diagram: one subject does every verb, and the same people are carried through every link. The promise is as strong as the hands that hold the chain.
<!-- @end -->

**Confessional frame**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: confessionalFrame -->
A confessional note (confessional Reformed): this tradition reads Romans 8:29–30 as the unbreakable chain of salvation — one purpose of God running from foreknowledge to glory, in which everyone he fore-loved is finally glorified, none lost along the way. Here "foreknew" is read relationally, in the Hebrew sense of "know" as covenant commitment (Amos 3:2; Genesis 18:19): God set his love on these particular persons before the chain's first link, rather than foreseeing a choice of theirs. That is why these verses anchor the doctrines of election and the perseverance of the saints: the chain's links are all God's acts, and the same "whom… these also" thread runs through every one. This is offered as a labelled confessional conviction; the fair summary of other readings of προέγνω (proegnō, "foreknew") is below, and readers who construe that word differently still stand under the same promise of verse 28.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: debateSummary.issue -->
What does προέγνω (proegnō, "he foreknew") mean, and whom does the chain carry? The grammar fixes a great deal: the same relative-pronoun thread ("those whom… these also") runs through all five verbs, and God is the subject of every one. What the syntax does not settle is what "foreknew" is — fore-love of persons, foresight of faith, or the choice of a people in the Messiah. Careful readers take each view.
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
Relational fore-love — the standard reading in this guide
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
In Scripture "to know" often means to love and commit to someone, not merely to have information about them ("you only have I known," Amos 3:2). On this reading προέγνω (proegnō) means God fore-loved particular persons — set his covenant affection on them beforehand — and the chain then carries those same persons all the way to glory. The verb's object is people ("those whom"), not facts about people, which this view takes as decisive.
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: debateSummary.views[0].cautions[0] -->
That "know" can mean "love" does not prove it must here; the claim rests on the Hebrew idiom and the shape of the chain, and it should be argued from those, not asserted from the lexicon alone.
<!-- @end -->

### View 2

**View 2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: debateSummary.views[1].label -->
Foresight of faith — an alternate reading (classic Arminian)
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: debateSummary.views[1].summary -->
On this reading God foreknew who would believe — his eternal knowledge of each person's free response — and predestined those believers to be conformed to his Son. This keeps a real human response inside the chain's first link and is how many careful interpreters, ancient and modern, have read the verb. It takes προγινώσκω (proginōskō) in its ordinary Greek sense of knowing in advance.
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: debateSummary.views[1].cautions[0] -->
The text says God foreknew persons ("those whom"), not that he foreknew their faith — the object "their faith" has to be supplied. Advocates answer that knowing persons includes knowing their response; the syntax itself does not adjudicate.
<!-- @end -->

### View 3

**View 3 — label**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: debateSummary.views[2].label -->
Corporate election (a people in the Messiah)
<!-- @end -->

**View 3 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: debateSummary.views[2].summary -->
On this reading the chain describes God's purpose for a people as a people: the group God fore-loved in Christ is destined, called, justified, and glorified, and individuals share the chain's destiny by belonging to that body. The plural pronouns and the goal — a Son with "many brothers and sisters" — fit a corporate horizon well.
<!-- @end -->

**View 3 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: debateSummary.views[2].cautions[0] -->
The chain's hand-off grammar ("whom he called, these he also justified") still speaks of the same ones at every link, so a corporate reading must explain how the group's destiny relates to the persons in it — a question the diagram raises but cannot answer.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
The syntax settles the chain's shape and refuses to settle its first word. It makes God the one subject of all five verbs, and the relative-pronoun relay (οὓς… τούτους καί — hous… toutous kai, "those whom… these also") explicitly carries the same object group from link to link — that much is drawn in the diagram, not inferred. But the meaning of προέγνω (proegnō, "he foreknew") is a question of lexical sense and biblical idiom, which no diagram can decide. English translations rightly render it "foreknew" and leave the theological question open — exactly what the Greek does.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — All things work together for good — for whom?

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-all-things-for-good].title -->
All things work together for good — for whom?
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-all-things-for-good].body -->
Paul opens with what "we know": πάντα (panta) [[sunergei]] εἰς ἀγαθόν (eis agathon) — "all things work together for good." The diagram draws πάντα (panta, "all things") as the subject of the verb [[sunergei]], with εἰς ἀγαθόν (eis agathon, "for good") hanging beneath it, and the whole clause standing as the object of "we know." Notice the promise is not addressed to everyone: the dative phrase "to those who love God" hangs from the verb, marking whose good all things serve.
