(A famous textual variant adds ὁ θεός (ho theos) as an explicit subject — "God works all things together for good"; see the alternate reading below. Either way, the next two verses name God as the one at work.)
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-all-things-for-good].implication -->
The verse is a promise with an address on it: all things serve the good of those who love God. Verses 29–30 will explain why Paul can say something so sweeping — and who is really doing the working.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-all-things-for-good].caution -->
Whether the subject is "all things" or (with some early manuscripts) "God," no reading of this verse leaves events working for good on their own — the chain that follows puts God's purpose behind every link.
<!-- @end -->

**Contested-reading lead-in**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-all-things-for-good].contested.note -->
Some weighty early manuscripts (𝔓46 A B) read "God works all things together for good," adding ὁ θεός (ho theos) as the explicit subject. Open the alternate reading to see the variant fairly presented — the base text and the variant both end in the same promise.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — Called according to his purpose

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-called-according-to-purpose].title -->
Called according to his purpose
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-called-according-to-purpose].body -->
Paul immediately re-describes the same people: "those who are [[kletois]] — called — according to his [[prothesin]], his purpose." The diagram hangs κατὰ πρόθεσιν (kata prothesin, "according to purpose") beneath the participle, qualifying the calling: it did not begin with them. This one small phrase is the doorway into verses 29–30. Paul now explains what that purpose is — and he does it with a chain of five verbs.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-called-according-to-purpose].implication -->
"Those who love God" and "those called according to his purpose" are the same people described from two directions — their love for God, and God's prior purpose for them. The chain will keep both truths in view.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — Whom he foreknew, he also predestined

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-foreknew-predestined].title -->
Whom he foreknew, he also predestined
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-foreknew-predestined].body -->
The chain begins: οὓς (hous, "whom") [[proegno]] καὶ (kai, "also") [[proorisen]] — "those whom he foreknew, he also predestined." Look at the diagram's structure: the relative clause "whom he foreknew" is itself the OBJECT of "he predestined." The people known are handed, as a whole clause, into the next verb. And notice what is missing: neither verb has a written subject. Greek can drop the pronoun, and Paul does — the diagram shows two verbs standing with no subject word, because the subject is carried silently from verse 28: God. Here "foreknew" carries the relational, covenantal sense of biblical "know" (as in "you only have I known," Amos 3:2): God set his love on these particular persons before the chain's first link, not merely foresaw their future faith — see the debate summary for the readings some interpreters take instead.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-foreknew-predestined].implication -->
The first link sets the pattern for every link after it: a group of people ("whom"), a divine verb, and a hand-off to the next verb. The chain is built out of relative clauses — that is what makes it a chain.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-foreknew-predestined].caution -->
[[proegno]]'s object is people, not facts — "those whom he foreknew." The syntax alone does not settle what kind of knowing that is; this guide reads it relationally (see the debate note for the alternate readings some interpreters prefer).
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — Predestined — to what? Conformed to the image of his Son

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-conformed-to-image].title -->
Predestined — to what? Conformed to the image of his Son
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-conformed-to-image].body -->
Predestination in this sentence has a stated destination: [[summorphous]] τῆς εἰκόνος τοῦ υἱοῦ αὐτοῦ (tēs eikonos tou huiou autou, "of the image of his Son") — "conformed to the image of his Son." The diagram draws [[summorphous]] as a complement of "predestined," with a cascade of genitives beneath it: conformed — to the image — of the Son — of him. And the purpose clause beneath that gives the goal behind the goal: "that he might be the [[prototokon]] — firstborn — among many brothers and sisters." God's eternal purpose is not an abstraction; it is a family portrait, with the Son at its head.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-conformed-to-image].implication -->
Whatever else predestination means, its stated goal here is Christlikeness — a destiny of family resemblance to the Son. The chain exists so that Christ will have many brothers and sisters who look like him.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — The relay: predestined → called → justified

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-called-justified].title -->
The relay: predestined → called → justified
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-called-justified].body -->
Now watch the sentence run. "Those whom he predestined, these he also [[ekalesen]] — called; and whom he called, these he also [[edikaiosen]] — justified." The diagram shows the relay mechanism plainly: each new clause opens with a relative clause that REPEATS the previous link's verb ("whom he predestined…", "whom he called…"), hands its people to the pronoun τούτους (toutous, "these"), and passes them to the next verb with καί (kai, "also"). No one is added between links, and no one is dropped: the ones predestined are the ones called, and the ones called are the ones justified. The grammar itself is the argument.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-called-justified].implication -->
The "whom… these also" relay is Paul's way of welding the links shut: each verb's finished group is exactly the next verb's starting group. That is why this passage reads like a chain and diagrams like one.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 6 — And glorified — a future written as finished

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-glorified].title -->
And glorified — a future written as finished
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-glorified].body -->
The last link is the astonishing one: "whom he justified, these he also [[edoxasen]] — GLORIFIED." Glorification is future — Paul has just spent this chapter groaning for it (8:18–25) — yet he writes it with the same past-viewed verb form as "called" and "justified," as one more completed link in the chain. This is not a slip. Paul presents a future reality with the settled confidence of an accomplished fact: seen from inside God's purpose, the glorification of these people is as certain as their calling. Translators rightly render it "glorified," letting English readers feel the same startling finality.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-glorified].implication -->
The chain's last link is written in the grammar of certainty: what God has purposed for these people is so sure that Paul can speak of glory — still ahead of them — as already theirs.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-glorified].caution -->
The aorist does NOT mean "once-for-all" or "already happened in time" — it simply views an action as a whole. The force here comes from Paul's CHOICE to view a future event that way, alongside four past ones: a deliberate expression of confident anticipation, not a property of the tense itself.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 7 — Step back: one subject, one thread, one chain

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-one-chain].title -->
Step back: one subject, one thread, one chain
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-one-chain].body -->
Now see the whole sentence at once. Five verbs — [[proegno]], [[proorisen]], [[ekalesen]], [[edikaiosen]], [[edoxasen]] — stand on parallel baselines, joined as coordinated clauses. Not one of them has a written subject: the same unnamed "he" — God — does every verb. And one thread of people runs through every clause: "those whom… these also… whom… these also…" The coordination and the relative clauses are what make the chain VISIBLE — the diagram shows a single connected structure from foreknowledge to glory, which is exactly Paul's point. This is why verse 28 can promise what it promises: all things work for good because one purpose, in one pair of hands, carries one people the whole way.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-one-chain].implication -->
Read as a chain seen whole — which is how the string of aorists presents it — the sentence grounds assurance not in the strength of our grip but in the continuity of God's acts: the same "he" and the same "these" from the first link to the last.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: steps[step-one-chain].caution -->
The five aorists view the chain as a whole; they do not each mean "a single once-for-all event." Calling, justifying, and glorifying have their own timing in a believer's life — the tense choice presents the sequence as one finished panorama, and that presentation is the point.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### συνεργεῖ · synergei

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[sunergei].surface -->
συνεργεῖ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[sunergei].transliteration -->
synergei
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[sunergei].lemma -->
συνεργέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[sunergei].gloss -->
works together
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[sunergei].parsing -->
present active indicative, 3rd person singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[sunergei].explanation -->
"Works together" — the source of the English word "synergy." In the base text its subject is πάντα (panta), "all things"; some early manuscripts add ὁ θεός (ho theos), making God the explicit subject ("God works all things together").
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[sunergei].caution -->
Either text yields the same theology once verses 29–30 are read: the working is not luck. The variant makes explicit the subject the chain implies.
<!-- @end -->

### πρόθεσιν · prothesin

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prothesin].surface -->
πρόθεσιν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prothesin].transliteration -->
prothesin
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prothesin].lemma -->
πρόθεσις
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prothesin].gloss -->
purpose, plan
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prothesin].parsing -->
noun, accusative feminine singular (object of κατά, kata, "according to")
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prothesin].explanation -->
"Purpose" — literally a "setting forth" of intention. With κατά (kata, "according to"), forming "according to purpose": the calling of verse 28 happens on a plan, and verses 29–30 spell that plan out link by link.
<!-- @end -->

### κλητοῖς · klētois

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[kletois].surface -->
κλητοῖς
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[kletois].transliteration -->
klētois
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[kletois].lemma -->
κλητός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[kletois].gloss -->
called
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[kletois].parsing -->
verbal adjective, dative masculine plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[kletois].explanation -->
"Called ones" — a verbal adjective marking the people as summoned by God. In Paul, "called" is effective language: the same word family reappears as a verb — ἐκάλεσεν (ekalesen, "he called") — inside the chain at verse 30.
<!-- @end -->

### προέγνω · proegnō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proegno].surface -->
προέγνω
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proegno].transliteration -->
proegnō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proegno].lemma -->
προγινώσκω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proegno].gloss -->
he foreknew
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proegno].parsing -->
aorist active indicative, 3rd person singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proegno].explanation -->
The chain's first link: "he knew beforehand." Its object is people ("those whom"), not information. Whether that knowing is relational fore-love, foresight of faith, or the choice of a people in Christ is the passage's great debated question — see the debate summary.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proegno].caution -->
The aorist views the act as a whole; it says nothing about "once-for-all-ness." And the verb's meaning is a lexical question the diagram cannot settle — hold your reading with charity toward the others.
<!-- @end -->

### προώρισεν · proōrisen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proorisen].surface -->
προώρισεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proorisen].transliteration -->
proōrisen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proorisen].lemma -->
προορίζω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proorisen].gloss -->
he predestined
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proorisen].parsing -->
aorist active indicative, 3rd person singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[proorisen].explanation -->
"He marked out beforehand" — from ὁρίζω (horizō), to set a boundary (the root of "horizon"). In this sentence its destination is stated: conformity to the image of the Son. It appears twice, closing verse 29 and opening verse 30's relay.
<!-- @end -->

### συμμόρφους · symmorphous

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[summorphous].surface -->
συμμόρφους
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[summorphous].transliteration -->
symmorphous
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[summorphous].lemma -->
σύμμορφος
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[summorphous].gloss -->
conformed to, sharing the form of
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[summorphous].parsing -->
adjective, accusative masculine plural (complement of προώρισεν, proōrisen, "he predestined")
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[summorphous].explanation -->
"Sharing the form of" — the stated goal of predestination: people shaped to the image of the Son. Paul uses the same word family for the resurrection body (Philippians 3:21); the destiny is full family likeness.
<!-- @end -->

### πρωτότοκον · prōtotokon

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prototokon].surface -->
πρωτότοκον
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prototokon].transliteration -->
prōtotokon
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prototokon].lemma -->
πρωτότοκος
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prototokon].gloss -->
firstborn
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prototokon].parsing -->
adjective used substantivally, accusative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[prototokon].explanation -->
"Firstborn" — the Son's rank among the "many brothers and sisters" the chain produces. The purpose clause makes the chain's final cause the honor of Christ as head of a large family.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐκάλεσεν · ekalesen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[ekalesen].surface -->
ἐκάλεσεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[ekalesen].transliteration -->
ekalesen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[ekalesen].lemma -->
καλέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[ekalesen].gloss -->
he called
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[ekalesen].parsing -->
aorist active indicative, 3rd person singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[ekalesen].explanation -->
"He called" — the link where God's eternal purpose reaches into a person's history, through the gospel (2 Thessalonians 2:14). It picks up κλητοῖς (klētois, "called ones") from verse 28: the "called ones" are called by this verb.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐδικαίωσεν · edikaiōsen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edikaiosen].surface -->
ἐδικαίωσεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edikaiosen].transliteration -->
edikaiōsen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edikaiosen].lemma -->
δικαιόω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edikaiosen].gloss -->
he justified
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edikaiosen].parsing -->
aorist active indicative, 3rd person singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edikaiosen].explanation -->
"He justified" — declared righteous, the great courtroom verb of Romans (3:24–26; 5:1). In the chain it stands between calling and glory: the verdict of the last day, rendered in the middle of history.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐδόξασεν · edoxasen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edoxasen].surface -->
ἐδόξασεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edoxasen].transliteration -->
edoxasen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edoxasen].lemma -->
δοξάζω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edoxasen].gloss -->
he glorified
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edoxasen].parsing -->
aorist active indicative, 3rd person singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edoxasen].explanation -->
"He glorified" — the chain's last link, and the only one still future for Paul's readers, yet written with the same past-viewed verb form as the rest. Paul presents what God will certainly do with the settled voice of what God has done.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-8-28-30 :: greekTerms[edoxasen].caution -->
Do not read the aorist as "already happened" or "once-for-all": the tense views the act as a whole, and Paul's striking CHOICE to so view a future act is a deliberate expression of certainty — confident anticipation, not completed chronology.
<!-- @end -->

---

# Ephesians 1:3–14 — one long, breathless blessing

<!-- guide guide-ephesians-1-3-14 · Ephesians 1:3–14 · advanced -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: title -->
Ephesians 1:3–14 — one long, breathless blessing
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: reference -->
Ephesians 1:3–14
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: summary -->
Paul praises God in one long sentence tracing his plan of salvation.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: devotionalFrame -->
Open your own Bible alongside this diagram. Your translation almost certainly breaks these twelve verses into several sentences — a kindness to the English reader, since Paul does not pause for one breath in the Greek. As you move through the steps, watch two things: how often the phrase "in Christ" (or "in him," "in whom") recurs, and how the sentence keeps resolving into the same note of praise. This passage is not a theological outline to be dissected; it is a single, overflowing blessing, and the grammar itself is part of the good news.
<!-- @end -->

**Confessional frame**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: confessionalFrame -->
A confessional note (confessional Reformed reading): the Thirty-Nine Articles (Article XVII, "Of Predestination and Election") read passages like this as teaching a real, gracious, unconditional election "in Christ" — but insist, in the same breath, that the doctrine is properly received only as Paul presents it here: full of praise, addressed to believers for their comfort, never as a tool for speculating about anyone else's standing before God. Other faithful Christian traditions read the "chose / predestined" language differently, as the debate summary above shows fairly; this note simply names where this app's own confessional heritage sits, not the only faithful reading of the passage.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: debateSummary.issue -->
What do "he chose us" (ἐξελέξατο, exelexato, v. 4) and "having predestined us" (προορίσας, proorisas, v. 5; προορισθέντες, prooristhentes, v. 11) mean for how God's choice relates to human faith?
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
Unconditional election (this guide's standard reading; classical Reformed)
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
God's choice of particular people for salvation "before the foundation of the world" (v. 4) rests entirely in his own gracious will, not on anything foreseen in the people chosen — the passage's own phrase is "according to the good pleasure of his will" (v. 5), not "according to foreseen faith."
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: debateSummary.views[0].cautions[0] -->
Read apart from this passage's own doxological setting, the doctrine can sound cold or fatalistic — which is exactly the tone this sentence never takes.
<!-- @end -->

### View 2

**View 2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: debateSummary.views[1].label -->
Corporate / Christ-centered election (an alternate reading)
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: debateSummary.views[1].summary -->
The repeated anchor "in Christ" / "in him" (vv. 4, 11) is the key: God's primary choice is of Christ himself, and believers are "chosen" by being joined to him through faith — the emphasis falls on the church's identity in Christ more than on a pre-temporal list of individual names.
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: debateSummary.views[1].cautions[0] -->
This reading has to give a full account of the very personal "us" and "we" language that runs through the whole passage, not only the corporate "in him."
<!-- @end -->

### View 3

**View 3 — label**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: debateSummary.views[2].label -->
Conditional election (foreknowledge-based reading; an alternate reading)
<!-- @end -->

**View 3 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: debateSummary.views[2].summary -->
God's choice, while real and gracious, is grounded in his foreknowledge of who would respond in faith — election described here as an outworking of God's foreknowledge rather than a choice made without reference to it.
<!-- @end -->

**View 3 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: debateSummary.views[2].cautions[0] -->
Critics note the passage grounds the choice in God's own will and pleasure (v. 5, v. 11) rather than explicitly naming foreseen faith as its basis.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
The grammar is unambiguous about two things: WHERE the choosing happens ("in him," vv. 4, 11 — never apart from Christ) and WHAT it is FOR (the three-fold refrain "to the praise of his glory," vv. 6, 12, 14). It is far less clear about HOW God's choice relates to human believing — that question is answered from the passage's theology and from the rest of Scripture, not from parsing this one long sentence. The diagram can show you the shape of Paul's confidence here; it cannot settle centuries of careful debate for you.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — A shout of praise, before there's even a verb

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-blessed-be-god].title -->
A shout of praise, before there's even a verb
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-blessed-be-god].body -->
Paul opens with a single word, [[eulogetos]] — "Blessed…" — followed immediately by "God," and nothing else. No verb yet. The oldest kind of Jewish praise simply cries an adjective over God before saying anything further about him. Almost at once, though, a participle steps in to do the sentence's real work: [[eulogesas]], "the one having blessed us." From here the participle reaches for its favorite phrase, repeated across this whole passage: [[enChristo]], "in Christ." Watch for that phrase — it will not let go for the rest of the sentence.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-blessed-be-god].implication -->
The letter's very first move is worship, not argument. Everything the rest of this passage says — election, redemption, the Spirit — is unpacked as REASONS for this one opening shout of blessing, not the other way around.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-blessed-be-god].caution -->
A verbless opening like this is not a hidden signal or a puzzle to solve — it is the ordinary shape of a Jewish benediction (compare Psalm 41:13 LXX; 2 Corinthians 1:3; 1 Peter 1:3). English translations rightly supply "Blessed be God," because that is exactly what the Greek means.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — Chosen and predestined — inside the blessing, not instead of it

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-chosen-in-love].title -->
Chosen and predestined — inside the blessing, not instead of it
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-chosen-in-love].body -->
The two verbs most readers associate with "predestination" both appear here: [[exelexato]], "he chose us" (v. 4), and [[proorisas]], "having predestined us" (v. 5). Paul is specific about both the timing and the ground of that choice: it happened "before the foundation of the world" (v. 4) — before any of us existed to do anything — and its stated ground, a verse later, is simply "the good pleasure of his will" (v. 5), not anything foreseen in the people chosen. Notice where the diagram puts these two verbs — not standing on their own as a separate topic, but nested two layers beneath the main participle "having blessed," introduced by "just as" (καθὼς, kathōs). Follow the line further and both verbs lead, a few words later, into the sentence's first refrain: [[epainon]], "praise" — "to the praise of his glorious grace." Grammatically, the goal of God's choosing is stated outright: praise.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-chosen-in-love].implication -->
The sentence never lets "chosen" and "predestined" stand alone as a cold decree, and it never roots either verb in anything God foresaw in us — the ground it names is his own "good pleasure" (v. 5), full stop. Both verbs are grammatically subordinate to the opening praise, and both are aimed — by the sentence's own structure — at more praise. Whatever else these words mean, their stated function here is doxology and assurance, not anxiety.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-chosen-in-love].caution -->
The aorist tense of both verbs simply views God's choosing and predestining as complete, whole acts — by itself it does not settle every fine point of timing or mechanism that later doctrinal debates raise among those who hold this reading (see this guide's debate summary for how other traditions read these verses differently). What the sentence does state plainly is WHERE this happens ("in him," v. 4), WHAT GROUNDS it ("the good pleasure of his will," v. 5 — not anything foreseen in us), and WHAT FOR ("to the praise of his glory").
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — In him we have redemption — and all things united in Christ

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-in-whom-redemption].title -->
In him we have redemption — and all things united in Christ
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-in-whom-redemption].body -->
A new sentence begins in most English Bibles, but the Greek keeps circling back: [[enHo]], "in whom," picks up exactly where "in Christ" left off. "In him we have redemption through his blood" — the same anchor, a new gift. A few lines later the scope widens dramatically: God's plan, once hidden and now disclosed (a μυστήριον, mystērion — a truth revealed, not a riddle left unsolved), is "to unite all things in Christ, things in heaven and things on earth." The personal blessing of verses 3–6 turns out to be one thread inside a plan for the whole creation — and the anchor point does not change.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-in-whom-redemption].implication -->
Redemption for "us" (vv. 3–6) and the summing-up of "all things" (vv. 9–10) are not two different topics — grammatically they are both located "in Christ" and "in him." The sentence keeps insisting that nothing it describes happens anywhere else.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-in-whom-redemption].caution -->
"Mystery" (μυστήριον, mystērion) in Paul does not mean an unsolved puzzle or a secret code — it names something God kept hidden and has now made known. The word points to disclosure, not to esoteric knowledge for a select few.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — Marked as God's own — the second refrain

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-marked-as-his-own].title -->
Marked as God's own — the second refrain
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-marked-as-his-own].body -->
The sentence loops back to its anchor a third time: "in him" ([[enHo]]) "we too were made [God's] own" — the diagram shows this main verb, then a participle beneath it, προορισθέντες (prooristhentes), "having been predestined." It is the same verb family as [[proorisas]] two steps ago, now describing what happened TO us rather than what God did. What follows is worth lingering over: "according to the purpose (πρόθεσιν, prothesin) of the one who works all things according to the counsel (βουλὴν, boulēn) of his will" — words of settled, personal intention, not blind fate. And the whole clause resolves, again, into [[epainon]] — the second time this sentence says "to the praise of his glory."
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-marked-as-his-own].implication -->
The refrain is not decoration — the sentence structures itself around it. Each time Paul names something God has done (choosing and predestining in vv. 4–6; making us his own in vv. 11–12), the grammar routes that action toward the same destination: praise for God's glory.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-marked-as-his-own].caution -->
Words like "purpose," "counsel," and later "good pleasure" describe a Father's settled intention, not an impersonal mechanism. The passage's own vocabulary keeps this warm — resist letting the English word "predestined," heard on its own, flatten that.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — Sealed with the Spirit — the third refrain, and the guarantee

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-sealed-with-the-spirit].title -->
Sealed with the Spirit — the third refrain, and the guarantee
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-sealed-with-the-spirit].body -->
The sentence turns, for the first time, from "we" to "you also" — the letter's Gentile readers are drawn into the same blessing. "In him" ([[enHo]]) "you also, having heard… having believed, were sealed with the promised Holy Spirit, who is the guarantee (ἀρραβὼν, arrabōn) of our inheritance." A down payment, a pledge of what is still to come. And then, a third and final time, the sentence lands on [[epainon]]: "to the praise of his glory." Twelve verses, one sentence, three refrains — the whole cascade of blessing, election, redemption, and sealing was always moving toward this one note.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-sealed-with-the-spirit].implication -->
The sentence that opened with a wordless shout of blessing closes with a Spirit given as a guarantee. The grammar's whole arc — from "Blessed be God" to "sealed… to the praise of his glory" — is designed to leave the reader not with a settled argument but with settled confidence.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: steps[step-sealed-with-the-spirit].caution -->
"Having heard… having believed" describes how these Gentile readers came to be included in the blessing already lavished on "us" — it is not a checklist that earns the sealing. The sentence keeps the initiative with God (he blessed, chose, predestined, and sealed) from its very first word to its last.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### Εὐλογητὸς · eulogētos

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogetos].surface -->
Εὐλογητὸς
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogetos].transliteration -->
eulogētos
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogetos].lemma -->
εὐλογητός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogetos].gloss -->
blessed
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogetos].parsing -->
adjective, nominative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogetos].explanation -->
The very first word of the whole twelve-verse sentence, with no verb yet supplied — the oldest kind of Jewish praise simply cries this adjective over God before saying anything further about him (compare Psalm 41:13 LXX; 2 Corinthians 1:3; 1 Peter 1:3).
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogetos].implication -->
The letter's first move is worship, not argument. Everything that follows is unpacked as reasons for this one opening shout of blessing.
<!-- @end -->

### εὐλογήσας · eulogēsas

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogesas].surface -->
εὐλογήσας
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogesas].transliteration -->
eulogēsas
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogesas].lemma -->
εὐλογέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogesas].gloss -->
having blessed
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogesas].parsing -->
aorist active participle, nominative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogesas].explanation -->
A participle describing God: "the one having blessed us." Once "Blessed be God" is said, this word becomes the sentence's real working part, going on to carry three straight prepositional phrases and then the subordinate clauses about choosing and predestining.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[eulogesas].implication -->
Nearly the whole passage hangs from this one participle and the chain that follows it. Once you can spot this word, the sentence's engine becomes visible.
<!-- @end -->

### Χριστῷ · Christō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enChristo].surface -->
Χριστῷ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enChristo].transliteration -->
Christō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enChristo].lemma -->
Χριστός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enChristo].gloss -->
Christ
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enChristo].parsing -->
proper noun, dative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enChristo].explanation -->
The dative noun after ἐν (en, "in") forms the phrase "in Christ." This exact phrase — or its equivalent "in him" / "in whom" — recurs roughly a dozen times across these twelve verses, more densely than in any comparable stretch elsewhere in the New Testament.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enChristo].implication -->
Grammatically, every spiritual blessing this sentence names is located "in Christ." Nothing described here stands outside of him.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐξελέξατο · exelexato

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[exelexato].surface -->
ἐξελέξατο
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[exelexato].transliteration -->
exelexato
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[exelexato].lemma -->
ἐκλέγομαι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[exelexato].gloss -->
he chose
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[exelexato].parsing -->
aorist middle indicative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[exelexato].explanation -->
God is the understood subject (carried over from "having blessed"), and "us" is the object. The clause is introduced by καθὼς (kathōs, "just as"), which grammatically subordinates the choosing to the blessing already announced in verse 3.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[exelexato].implication -->
God's choosing us doesn't stand on its own as a separate topic — the syntax presents it as an INSTANCE of the blessing already being praised. Adoration comes first; explanation comes second.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[exelexato].caution -->
The aorist here views the choosing as one completed whole; by itself it does not settle finer questions of timing or mechanism (see this guide's debate summary for how different traditions engage those questions). But the very next clause names the choice's ground plainly: "according to the good pleasure of his will" (v. 5), not anything foreseen in us.
<!-- @end -->

### προορίσας · proorisas

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[proorisas].surface -->
προορίσας
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[proorisas].transliteration -->
proorisas
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[proorisas].lemma -->
προορίζω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[proorisas].gloss -->
having predestined
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[proorisas].parsing -->
aorist active participle, nominative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[proorisas].explanation -->
Another aorist participle describing God, nested a level further down than "he chose." Its object, again, is "us," and its stated goal a few words later is "adoption as sons."
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[proorisas].implication -->
The grammar nests this word two layers beneath the sentence's main verb, inside a chain about God's initiative. It is never presented as a stand-alone thesis — it is one more layer inside one long blessing.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[proorisas].caution -->
"Predestined" can sound cold heard in isolation. In the Greek sentence its nearest neighbors are "in love" (v. 4) just before it and "to the praise of his glorious grace" (v. 6) just after — affection and worship, not a courtroom.
<!-- @end -->

### ᾧ · hō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enHo].surface -->
ᾧ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enHo].transliteration -->
hō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enHo].lemma -->
ὅς
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enHo].gloss -->
whom
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enHo].parsing -->
relative pronoun, dative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enHo].explanation -->
A relative pronoun picking up the thread of "in Christ" at the start of each new sentence English translations carve out of this one long Greek period (vv. 7, 11, 13 twice). Every time the sentence seems to start over, it is in fact still circling back to the same anchor.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[enHo].implication -->
Redemption (v. 7), being made God's own (v. 11), and the sealing of the Spirit (v. 13) are all still "in him," still riding the same participle from verse 3 — the paragraph breaks are for readability, not new topics.
<!-- @end -->

### ἔπαινον · epainon

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[epainon].surface -->
ἔπαινον
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[epainon].transliteration -->
epainon
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[epainon].lemma -->
ἔπαινος
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[epainon].gloss -->
praise
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[epainon].parsing -->
noun, accusative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[epainon].explanation -->
The noun that closes each of the sentence's three great movements: "to the praise of his glorious grace" (v. 6), "to the praise of his glory" (v. 12), and "to the praise of his glory" again (v. 14). The grammar attaches each refrain directly to the action it follows — choosing and predestining, being made God's own, sealing with the Spirit — as their stated goal.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-1-3-14 :: greekTerms[epainon].implication -->
Three times the sentence tells you why: not merely that God chose, predestined, redeemed, and sealed, but that he did it so his glory would be praised. This refrain is close to the sentence's real thesis statement, repeated like a chorus.
<!-- @end -->

---

# Ephesians 2:8–10 — saved by grace through faith

<!-- guide guide-ephesians-2-8-10 · Ephesians 2:8–10 · intermediate -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: title -->
Ephesians 2:8–10 — saved by grace through faith
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: reference -->
Ephesians 2:8–10
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: summary -->
A gospel presentation from Paul: God saves by grace through faith, not from works, and creates his people for good works.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: devotionalFrame -->
These three verses may be the clearest short summary of the gospel Paul ever wrote. They assume the bad news of the verses before them — "you were dead in trespasses and sins" (2:1) — because only the dead need saving. Dead people do not improve; they are raised. So watch what Paul hangs on which words: grace beneath the verb as its ground, faith as the channel it comes through, two firm "not from" phrases closing every door to self-rescue, and then — surprise — good works returning at the end, not as the price of salvation but as the path God prepared for the saved. If you have ever wondered whether God could accept you, these verses were written for you: salvation is his gift, received with empty hands.
<!-- @end -->

**Confessional frame**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: confessionalFrame -->
A confessional note (confessional Reformed): this guide reads Ephesians 2:8–10 the way the Reformation confessions do — salvation is by grace alone, through faith alone, in Christ alone, not on account of works, so that no one may boast. Grace is not God's help for those who first help themselves; it is his mercy to the spiritually dead. Faith is not the one work God rewards; it is the empty hand that receives Christ and all his benefits. And the faith that receives Christ is never barren: those saved by grace are God's workmanship, created in Christ Jesus for the good works he prepared beforehand. We are saved by faith alone, but the faith that saves is never alone.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — "You have been saved" — salvation is something done to you

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-you-have-been-saved].title -->
"You have been saved" — salvation is something done to you
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-you-have-been-saved].body -->
Start at the heart of the diagram: the verb. Paul writes ἐστε [[sesosmenoi]] (este sesōsmenoi) — "you ARE having-been-saved," a two-word verb phrase the diagram draws as one predicate on the baseline. Two things about its form carry the gospel. It is PASSIVE: the Ephesians did not save themselves; saving was done TO them, by God. And it is a perfect participle with a present "you are": a completed rescue whose result is their present, settled condition. English translations render this exactly right — "you have been saved." Notice also what the verb is not: it is not "you have been improved," "instructed," or "encouraged." σῴζω (sōzō) is rescue language — what is done for the drowning, the dying, the dead (2:1,5). The gospel does not begin with advice; it begins with what God has done.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-you-have-been-saved].implication -->
Salvation is God's finished act before it is the believer's ongoing response. A Christian's standing does not rest on today's performance but on a rescue already accomplished and still in force.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-you-have-been-saved].caution -->
The perfect views a past act together with its standing result — it does not by itself promise "once saved, always saved" as a grammatical fact. Assurance in this passage comes from WHO saves (vv. 8–10 as a whole), not from a tense form alone.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — "By grace" — God's initiative stands at the front

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-by-grace].title -->
"By grace" — God's initiative stands at the front
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-by-grace].body -->
Now look at what hangs beneath the verb. Τῇ γὰρ [[chariti]] (tē gar chariti) — "for by grace" — is the sentence's very first phrase, and the diagram slants it under "you have been saved" as the ground on which the rescue rests. Grace, χάρις (charis), is undeserved favor: not a reward for the deserving, not assistance for those who first make themselves worthy, but God's free kindness to people who were "dead in trespasses" and "children of wrath" (2:1–3). The little word γάρ (gar, "for") tells you Paul is explaining verses 4–7 — the God who is "rich in mercy" and whose "great love" made us alive with Christ. Grace is where the explanation starts because God is where salvation starts.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-by-grace].implication -->
The gospel is good news, not good advice, because its first cause is God's generosity, not our effort. If salvation begins in grace, it cannot be forfeited by the weakness that grace stooped to save.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — "Through faith" — the open hand that receives

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-through-faith].title -->
"Through faith" — the open hand that receives
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-through-faith].body -->
A second phrase hangs beneath the same verb: διὰ [[pisteos]] (dia pisteōs), "through faith." The preposition matters. Paul does not say we are saved "because of" our faith or "on the basis of" faith, as though faith were the payment; διά (dia) with this case marks the channel something comes THROUGH. Grace is the ground; faith is the open hand that receives what grace gives. The diagram makes the pairing visible: two phrases, side by side under one verb — by grace, through faith — cause and channel, gift and reception. Faith does not purchase salvation any more than a beggar's outstretched hand purchases bread. It looks away from itself to Christ, and in receiving him receives everything.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-through-faith].implication -->
Salvation is through faith ALONE, but faith is not the one work God accepts in place of the others — it is not a work at all in that sense. It is the instrument, never the merit; the receiving, never the earning.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-through-faith].caution -->
Do not let "faith alone" shrink into "belief that costs nothing." Faith receives a PERSON — Christ — and entrusting yourself to him reorders everything (as verse 10 is about to show).
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — "Not from yourselves — it is God's gift"

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-gods-gift].title -->
"Not from yourselves — it is God's gift"
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-gods-gift].body -->
Paul now stops, points back at everything he has just said, and closes a door: καὶ [[touto]] οὐκ ἐξ ὑμῶν (kai touto ouk ex hymōn) — "and this — not from yourselves." The diagram draws it as its own little clause: the subject [[touto]] ("this"), a negated "from" phrase where a source would stand, and no verb at all — Greek can leave "is" unwritten, and the bluntness is part of the force. Then comes the positive counterpart, another verbless clause: θεοῦ τὸ [[doron]] (theou to dōron) — "GOD'S is the gift," with "God's" thrown forward for emphasis. The whole saving reality Paul has just described — being saved by grace through faith — is not from ourselves but is God's gift. Salvation does not start in us, is not achieved by us, and is not financed by us. From first to last it is given.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-gods-gift].implication -->
A gift can only be received, never invoiced. If the whole of salvation — grace, rescue, and the very faith that receives it — comes wrapped as God's gift, then the believer's posture is forever gratitude, not negotiation.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-gods-gift].caution -->
[[touto]] is neuter, while both "grace" and "faith" are feminine nouns — so the grammar alone does not single out faith as the gift's narrow antecedent. The stronger and safer reading needs no such claim: Paul's "this" gathers up the WHOLE saving event just described, faith included within it, and calls it all God's gift.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — "Not from works, so that no one may boast"

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-not-from-works].title -->
"Not from works, so that no one may boast"
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-not-from-works].body -->
Paul closes a second door, and this one has a stated reason. οὐκ ἐξ [[ergon]] (ouk ex ergōn) — "not from works" — repeats the same "from" preposition he has just denied: salvation's SOURCE is not our doing, our record, our religious or moral achievement. Then the diagram shows a purpose clause hanging beneath, introduced by ἵνα (hina, "so that"): μή τις [[kauchesetai]] (mē tis kauchēsētai) — "so that no one may boast." The logic is airtight. If salvation were from works, even in part, the saved could claim even that part of the credit; heaven would have a wing built on human bragging rights. God has arranged salvation precisely so that this can never happen. Where works are excluded from the ground of salvation, boasting is excluded from its result — "so that the one who boasts must boast in the Lord" (1 Corinthians 1:31).
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-not-from-works].implication -->
The gospel humbles utterly and comforts utterly with the same stroke: nothing of yours contributed to your salvation, so nothing of yours can disqualify you from grace. All the glory goes to God — which is exactly where a saved sinner wants it.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 6 — Created in Christ Jesus for good works — the gift bears fruit

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-created-for-good-works].title -->
Created in Christ Jesus for good works — the gift bears fruit
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-created-for-good-works].body -->
Verse 10 is the surprise ending — and the diagram lets you watch Paul relocate a word. αὐτοῦ γάρ ἐσμεν [[poiema]] (autou gar esmen poiēma): "for we are HIS workmanship," with "his" thrown to the front of the sentence. Beneath it hangs [[ktisthentes]] (ktisthentes, "created") — creation language, the same passive pattern as verse 8: God acts, we are made. And where are we created? ἐν [[christo]] Ἰησοῦ (en Christō Iēsou), "in Christ Jesus" — union with Christ is where the new creation happens. Now watch the relocated word: [[ergois]] (ergois, "works") — the very noun verse 9 excluded — reappears, but hanging under a different verb. Works stood beneath "saved" as a denied SOURCE ("not from works"); now good works stand beneath "created" as the stated PURPOSE. And they come prepared: οἷς [[proetoimasen]] ὁ θεός (hois proētoimasen ho theos), "which God prepared beforehand, so that we might [[peripatesomen]] — walk — in them." Grace does not produce lawless people; it produces God's handiwork, walking a road God paved in advance.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-created-for-good-works].implication -->
Paul teaches that salvation is God's gracious gift, received through faith and not earned by works. Because salvation is not from us, boasting is excluded. Yet grace does not leave the believer unchanged: those saved in Christ are God's workmanship, created for the good works God prepared beforehand. Works are not the root of salvation but its fruit — not the price of grace but the path of the graced.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: steps[step-created-for-good-works].caution -->
Do not swing to the opposite error: verse 10 never makes good works optional. The same God who saves apart from works prepares works for the saved — a Christian without any fruit is a contradiction this passage does not allow. We are saved by faith alone, but the faith that saves is never alone.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### σεσῳσμένοι · sesōsmenoi

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[sesosmenoi].surface -->
σεσῳσμένοι
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[sesosmenoi].transliteration -->
sesōsmenoi
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[sesosmenoi].lemma -->
σῴζω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[sesosmenoi].gloss -->
having been saved
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[sesosmenoi].parsing -->
perfect passive participle, nominative masculine plural (with ἐστε, este, "you are" — a periphrastic perfect)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[sesosmenoi].explanation -->
"Saved" — rescue language, paired with "you are" to say: a completed rescue whose result is your present condition. The passive voice puts the acting entirely on God's side: the saved did not save themselves.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[sesosmenoi].implication -->
The believer's standing rests on an accomplished act of God, not on today's spiritual performance.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[sesosmenoi].caution -->
The perfect presents a past act with a standing result; it is a portrait of the rescue's completeness, not a grammatical proof-text about perseverance on its own.
<!-- @end -->

### χάριτί · chariti

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[chariti].surface -->
χάριτί
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[chariti].transliteration -->
chariti
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[chariti].lemma -->
χάρις
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[chariti].gloss -->
by grace
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[chariti].parsing -->
noun, dative feminine singular (dative of cause/means: the ground of the saving)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[chariti].explanation -->
"Grace" — God's free, undeserved favor. The dative hangs it beneath the verb as the basis on which the rescue happened, and Paul fronts it to the head of the sentence: grace leads his whole explanation.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[chariti].implication -->
Grace is mercy to the undeserving — not assistance to those who first make themselves worthy. Salvation begins in God's generosity.
<!-- @end -->

### πίστεως · pisteōs

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[pisteos].surface -->
πίστεως
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[pisteos].transliteration -->
pisteōs
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[pisteos].lemma -->
πίστις
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[pisteos].gloss -->
faith
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[pisteos].parsing -->
noun, genitive feminine singular (object of διά, dia, "through")
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[pisteos].explanation -->
"Faith" — trust that receives. With διά (dia, "through") it names the channel salvation comes through, not the payment it costs: grace gives, faith receives.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[pisteos].implication -->
Faith is the instrument of salvation, never its merit — an open hand, not a wage.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[pisteos].caution -->
Faith's power lies entirely in the One it lays hold of. It saves because it receives Christ, not because believing is itself a meritorious act.
<!-- @end -->

### τοῦτο · touto

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[touto].surface -->
τοῦτο
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[touto].transliteration -->
touto
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[touto].lemma -->
οὗτος
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[touto].gloss -->
this
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[touto].parsing -->
demonstrative pronoun, nominative neuter singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[touto].explanation -->
"This" — Paul's backward point at what he has just said. Being neuter, it most naturally sweeps up the whole preceding statement — the entire being-saved-by-grace-through-faith — and declares all of it "not from yourselves."
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[touto].caution -->
Because "grace" and "faith" are both feminine, the neuter does not grammatically single out "faith" as the gift. No overclaim is needed: the gift is the whole saving reality, and faith is inside it.
<!-- @end -->

### δῶρον · dōron

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[doron].surface -->
δῶρον
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[doron].transliteration -->
dōron
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[doron].lemma -->
δῶρον
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[doron].gloss -->
gift
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[doron].parsing -->
noun, nominative neuter singular (predicate of a verbless clause; θεοῦ, theou, "God's" stands first for emphasis)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[doron].explanation -->
"Gift" — a present, freely given. The clause has no verb, just the stark equation "God's — the gift," with "God's" thrown forward: whatever salvation is, its giver is God.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[doron].implication -->
A gift is received, not earned, and it says at least as much about the giver's heart as the receiver's worth.
<!-- @end -->

### ἔργων · ergōn

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergon].surface -->
ἔργων
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergon].transliteration -->
ergōn
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergon].lemma -->
ἔργον
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergon].gloss -->
works
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergon].parsing -->
noun, genitive neuter plural (object of ἐκ, ek, "from, out of")
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergon].explanation -->
"Works" — deeds, achievements, one's moral and religious record. With the negated ἐκ (ek, "from"), Paul denies that works are salvation's SOURCE. Watch this same noun return in verse 10 under a different verb — as purpose, not source.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergon].implication -->
Nothing you have done qualified you for salvation, and nothing you have failed to do disqualifies you from grace.
<!-- @end -->

### καυχήσηται · kauchēsētai

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[kauchesetai].surface -->
καυχήσηται
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[kauchesetai].transliteration -->
kauchēsētai
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[kauchesetai].lemma -->
καυχάομαι
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[kauchesetai].gloss -->
may boast
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[kauchesetai].parsing -->
aorist middle subjunctive, 3rd person singular (in a ἵνα μή, hina mē, "so that not" purpose clause)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[kauchesetai].explanation -->
"Boast" — to claim credit, to glory in oneself. The purpose clause states why God arranged salvation without works at its root: so that no person could ever stand in heaven pointing at themselves.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[kauchesetai].implication -->
A works-based salvation would manufacture pride; a grace-based salvation manufactures praise. God gets all the glory — and the saved sinner is glad of it.
<!-- @end -->

### ποίημα · poiēma

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[poiema].surface -->
ποίημα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[poiema].transliteration -->
poiēma
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[poiema].lemma -->
ποίημα
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[poiema].gloss -->
workmanship, handiwork
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[poiema].parsing -->
noun, nominative neuter singular (predicate nominative; αὐτοῦ, autou, "his" fronted for emphasis)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[poiema].explanation -->
"Workmanship" — a thing made, a work of craftsmanship. Paul flips the vocabulary of "works" on its head: before we ever work for God, we ARE God's work. "His" stands first in the Greek sentence: HIS handiwork is what we are.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[poiema].implication -->
The Christian life is not self-construction. Whatever good is being built in a believer has a Maker, and the Maker signs his work.
<!-- @end -->

### κτισθέντες · ktisthentes

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ktisthentes].surface -->
κτισθέντες
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ktisthentes].transliteration -->
ktisthentes
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ktisthentes].lemma -->
κτίζω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ktisthentes].gloss -->
having been created
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ktisthentes].parsing -->
aorist passive participle, nominative masculine plural
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ktisthentes].explanation -->
"Created" — the Bible's word for what only God does. It is passive, like "having been saved" in verse 8: the same pattern of divine action and human receiving. Salvation is nothing less than new creation (2 Corinthians 5:17).
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ktisthentes].caution -->
The aorist simply views the creating as a whole event; the emphasis is on WHO creates, not on a tense-derived timetable.
<!-- @end -->

### Χριστῷ · Christō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[christo].surface -->
Χριστῷ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[christo].transliteration -->
Christō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[christo].lemma -->
Χριστός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[christo].gloss -->
Christ
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[christo].parsing -->
proper noun, dative masculine singular (object of ἐν, en, "in"; with Ἰησοῦ, Iēsou, "Jesus" in apposition)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[christo].explanation -->
"In Christ Jesus" — Paul's signature phrase for union with Christ, the place where the new creation happens. Grace, faith, gift, and new life are not abstractions; they are found in a person, or not at all.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[christo].implication -->
Salvation is in Christ ALONE: to be saved is to be joined to him — created in him, alive in him, and carried by him.
<!-- @end -->

### ἔργοις · ergois

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergois].surface -->
ἔργοις
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergois].transliteration -->
ergois
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergois].lemma -->
ἔργον
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergois].gloss -->
works
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergois].parsing -->
noun, dative neuter plural (object of ἐπί, epi, "for, unto"; with ἀγαθοῖς, agathois, "good")
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergois].explanation -->
The same noun verse 9 excluded, now welcomed back in a new position: with ἐπί (epi, "for"), good works are the goal we were created FOR — not the source we were saved FROM. Same word, different preposition, opposite role.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[ergois].implication -->
Grace relocates works: out of salvation's foundation, into salvation's purpose. Works are the fruit of the rescue, never its root.
<!-- @end -->

### προητοίμασεν · proētoimasen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[proetoimasen].surface -->
προητοίμασεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[proetoimasen].transliteration -->
proētoimasen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[proetoimasen].lemma -->
προετοιμάζω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[proetoimasen].gloss -->
prepared beforehand
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[proetoimasen].parsing -->
aorist active indicative, 3rd person singular (subject: ὁ θεός, ho theos, "God")
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[proetoimasen].explanation -->
"Prepared beforehand" — God readied the good works in advance, the way a host sets a table before the guests arrive. Even the believer's obedience travels a road God paved first.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[proetoimasen].implication -->
Grace goes ahead of us at every step: God prepared the salvation, gives the faith-received gift, and has already prepared the path of the new life.
<!-- @end -->

### περιπατήσωμεν · peripatēsōmen

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].surface -->
περιπατήσωμεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].transliteration -->
peripatēsōmen
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].lemma -->
περιπατέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].gloss -->
we might walk
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].parsing -->
aorist active subjunctive, 1st person plural (in a ἵνα, hina, "so that" purpose clause)
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].explanation -->
"Walk" — the Bible's everyday picture for a way of life. The passage that began with us dead (2:1) ends with us walking; the purpose clause makes the new life the stated goal of the new creation.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-ephesians-2-8-10 :: greekTerms[peripatesomen].implication -->
The gospel's finish line is not a decision card but a transformed daily walk — ordinary steps, on a prepared road, in Christ.
<!-- @end -->
