# Romans 9:6–13 — the word of God has not failed

<!-- guide guide-romans-9-6-13 · Romans 9:6–13 · advanced -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: title -->
Romans 9:6–13 — the word of God has not failed
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: reference -->
Romans 9:6–13
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: summary -->
Paul defends God's faithfulness through his choice of Jacob over Esau.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: devotionalFrame -->
Paul answers whether God’s word has failed by tracing Israel, promise, and election through Scripture — from Isaac to Jacob and Esau — so the diagram shows the joints of his argument for God’s faithfulness. Keep your own Bible open as you go; the diagram adds nothing to what your translation already says.
<!-- @end -->

**Confessional frame**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: confessionalFrame -->
This guide teaches this passage as one of Scripture’s clearest statements of God’s sovereign, gracious election of individual persons to salvation — a choice grounded in God’s own calling, not in birth order, ancestry, or foreseen merit. That is exactly why Paul, having just said this, anticipates an objection in the very next verse — "Is there injustice with God?" (9:14) — a question that only makes sense if the choice he has described discriminates between particular people, not merely assigns nations a role in history. Many in the Reformed tradition have read the passage this way for precisely this reason, and this guide states that reading plainly in its main text. The corporate/vocational alternative is represented fairly in the debate note above; Christians who hold it are reading the same grammar in good faith, but settling the fuller doctrinal question is ultimately a matter for the whole counsel of Scripture, not for a syntax diagram alone.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: debateSummary.issue -->
Paul’s language here — God’s purpose "according to election," a choice made before either brother had done anything, and "Jacob I loved, Esau I hated" — is one of Scripture’s clearest statements about election. But election of WHOM, to WHAT? Readers have long disagreed about whether Paul is describing God’s sovereign choice of individual persons unto salvation, or God’s choice of a covenant line/nation for a role in redemptive history. This guide teaches the first reading — individual, unconditional election — as its standard position in the main text above, and represents the second fairly below.
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
Individual, unconditional election (this guide’s standard reading)
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
On this reading, Paul’s point is that God freely and graciously chooses specific persons for salvation, apart from anything they do. The removal of birth order and behavior ("not yet born... not having done anything good or bad") is read as removing every possible human ground for God’s choice, and Jacob and Esau become a clear test case for that principle: God’s choice preceded any distinguishing act at all.
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: debateSummary.views[0].cautions[0] -->
Paul’s own vocabulary in this stretch is corporate ("Israel," "seed," "children"), so this reading has to argue that the corporate language carries an individual, soteriological point rather than assume it.
<!-- @end -->

### View 2

**View 2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: debateSummary.views[1].label -->
Corporate / vocational election (alternate reading)
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: debateSummary.views[1].summary -->
On this reading, Paul is describing which LINE would carry God’s promise and which ROLE nations (Israel through Jacob, Edom through Esau) would play in redemptive history — not a decree about Jacob’s and Esau’s personal eternal destinies. "Jacob I loved, Esau I hated" quotes Malachi 1:2–3, addressed centuries later to the nations descended from the brothers; "hated" is read as covenantal preference or relative "loved less" — an idiom attested elsewhere (Genesis 29:31; Luke 14:26) — rather than personal animus toward Esau the man.
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: debateSummary.views[1].cautions[0] -->
This reading can understate how far Paul pushes the principle in the rest of the chapter (compare 9:16, 9:18, which speak in terms of mercy and hardening, not only history or vocation); it should not be used to strip the passage of any bearing on grace.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
The syntax does real work here: it isolates God’s "purpose according to election" from birth order, ancestry, and prior conduct, and it sets "not from works" against "from him who calls" as the two candidate grounds for that purpose — ruling the first one out. What the grammar cannot decide on its own is the SCOPE of the election in view — individual, corporate, or (as many hold) both together in different senses. That question is worked out through how Paul continues the argument across Romans 9–11 and through the reader’s wider theology, not by this passage’s parsing alone.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — It is not as though God’s word has failed

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-word-not-failed].title -->
It is not as though God’s word has failed
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-word-not-failed].body -->
Paul states his conclusion before he argues for it. The Greek denies an entire claim at once: "it is not such, that the word of God [[ekpeptoken]]." Look at what the diagram does with that denial — it treats the whole statement "the word of God has failed" as the OBJECT of an implied verb "is," so the denial falls on the claim itself, not on one word inside it. Whatever hard question Israel’s unbelief raises for the rest of the chapter, Paul rules out one answer immediately: God’s own word is not the problem.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-word-not-failed].implication -->
This is Paul’s anchor for the whole passage. Everything in verses 6–13 — the distinctions, the Scripture quotations, the hard case of Jacob and Esau — exists to show HOW God’s word has not failed, not whether it has.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-word-not-failed].caution -->
The verb [[ekpeptoken]] is perfect tense — it presents "failing" as a settled, complete state, the very state of affairs Paul is denying ever came about. That is what the tense contributes to the picture; it does not by itself prove the theological point. Paul spends the next three chapters making the actual case.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — Children of the flesh, children of the promise

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-flesh-and-promise].title -->
Children of the flesh, children of the promise
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-flesh-and-promise].body -->
Paul has just said not all who descend from Israel belong to Israel (v. 6b), and not all of Abraham’s physical offspring are children of promise (v. 7) — only the line through Isaac. Verse 8 states the principle behind both examples, and the diagram shows it as two matched clauses joined by "but": "these" — the children of the [[sarkos]] — are not thereby the children of God; rather, the children of the [[epangelias]] are counted as offspring. Physical descent (σάρξ, sarx, flesh) and God’s spoken word (ἐπαγγελία, epangelia, promise) sit on opposite sides of the same picture.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-flesh-and-promise].implication -->
Paul is not questioning God’s word about Israel — he is showing that it was never anchored in bloodline to begin with. That was already true in Abraham’s own family, so this is not a new exception invented to explain Israel’s unbelief; it is how the promise always worked.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-flesh-and-promise].caution -->
"Flesh" here means ordinary physical descent, not something sinful in itself — Ishmael was every bit Abraham’s son by nature. The contrast is about what CARRIES the promise (God’s spoken word) versus what does not (ancestry alone), not about the flesh being evil.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — God’s purpose according to election

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-purpose-according-to-election].title -->
God’s purpose according to election
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-purpose-according-to-election].body -->
The hardest case comes next: Rebekah’s twins, conceived by the same father at the same time (v. 10). Paul says God spoke about them before either was born or had done anything good or bad — "in order that God’s purpose [[ekloge]] might stand, not from works but from [[kalountos]]." The diagram sets "πρόθεσις" (prothesis, purpose) as the subject of that clause, "according to election" as the phrase describing it, and "not from works" against "from the one who calls" as the two candidate grounds for it. Grammar cannot remove birth order or behavior more thoroughly than this: neither son had even been born, let alone acted.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-purpose-according-to-election].implication -->
Removing birth order and behavior from the picture makes God’s calling, not human achievement, the stated ground of his purpose here. Paul is describing God’s choice between Jacob and Esau as individuals — made before either had done anything, for better or worse — and grounded in nothing but the God who calls. That is exactly why Paul goes on to anticipate an objection in the very next verse, "Is there injustice with God?" (9:14): a question that only makes sense if God’s choice discriminates between particular people, not merely between nations after the fact.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-purpose-according-to-election].caution -->
[[ekloge]] is Paul’s own word here. Grammar can rule out one thing with precision — that God chose based on foreseen works — without independently proving every further point a reader might draw from it; this guide reads the WHO of that choice as individual persons (see above), while the corporate/vocational alternative is presented fairly in the debate note below.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — The elder shall serve the younger

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-elder-serve-younger].title -->
The elder shall serve the younger
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-elder-serve-younger].body -->
Before Rebekah’s sons were even born, she was given a word about them: "the elder will serve the younger." The diagram shows this as a quotation embedded inside the sentence — grammatically the object of "it was said to her" — so you can see it functioning exactly as Scripture: not Paul’s own commentary, but a quoted oracle he is building his argument on.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-elder-serve-younger].implication -->
This first oracle (echoing Genesis 25:23) names the relationship between the brothers themselves before Paul intensifies it. He is building toward the sharper, unmistakably personal pair of verbs in the very next line — "Jacob I loved, Esau I hated" — where the same choice is stated of each brother by name, not merely of the nations that would one day descend from them.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — Jacob I loved, Esau I hated

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-jacob-loved-esau-hated].title -->
Jacob I loved, Esau I hated
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-jacob-loved-esau-hated].body -->
Paul caps the argument with Malachi’s own words, and the diagram shows just how tightly parallel they are: two short clauses, the same shape, the same tense, one verb apiece — "Jacob I [[egapesa]]... Esau I [[emisesa]]." The symmetry is the point: God is described as acting, deliberately and personally, toward each brother, without waiting for either to earn it.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-jacob-loved-esau-hated].implication -->
This is the line every reader has to reckon with honestly: before Jacob or Esau had done anything, good or bad, Paul says God loved one and hated the other — a choice between two individual persons, not a verdict on nations pronounced after the fact. Paul is not softening it; he is quoting it as Scripture’s own description of God’s freedom to choose apart from anything either brother had done. (Readers differ on how the corporate dimension of Malachi’s original setting bears on Paul’s point here — see the debate note below — but Paul’s own argument turns on birth order and personal conduct, not national history.)
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: steps[step-jacob-loved-esau-hated].caution -->
"Hated" translates a real word, and readers should not soften it into nothing. But Hebrew and Greek both use love/hate language for relative preference as well as emotion (compare Genesis 29:31; Luke 14:26, where "hating" one’s family means loving them less by comparison). Which sense is at work here — and what it implies about Esau himself — is exactly the question the debate note below addresses; the parallel grammar shows only that both verbs are asserted of God with equal directness.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### ἐκπέπτωκεν · ekpeptōken

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekpeptoken].surface -->
ἐκπέπτωκεν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekpeptoken].transliteration -->
ekpeptōken
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekpeptoken].lemma -->
ἐκπίπτω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekpeptoken].gloss -->
has failed
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekpeptoken].parsing -->
perfect active indicative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekpeptoken].explanation -->
Literally "has fallen out/off" — the verb behind "failed." Paul denies this is the state of affairs, and the whole clause "that the word of God has failed" is grammatically the thing being denied, not merely described.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekpeptoken].implication -->
Everything Paul argues in verses 6–13 exists to show HOW this denial holds up — that God’s word was never a promise about bloodline, so Israel’s unbelief does not break it.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekpeptoken].caution -->
The perfect tense presents the failing as a completed, standing state — exactly the state Paul says never came about. That is what the tense contributes; it does not by itself carry the argument.
<!-- @end -->

### σαρκὸς · sarkos

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[sarkos].surface -->
σαρκὸς
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[sarkos].transliteration -->
sarkos
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[sarkos].lemma -->
σάρξ
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[sarkos].gloss -->
flesh
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[sarkos].parsing -->
noun, genitive feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[sarkos].explanation -->
Ordinary physical descent — "the children of the flesh" are those who are simply, biologically, someone’s offspring. Paul sets this against "the children of the promise" as two different grounds for who counts as God’s people.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[sarkos].caution -->
Not a moral judgment on the body or on physical descent itself — Ishmael was Abraham’s son "according to the flesh" without that being sinful. The contrast is about what carries God’s promise, not about flesh being bad.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐπαγγελίας · epangelias

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[epangelias].surface -->
ἐπαγγελίας
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[epangelias].transliteration -->
epangelias
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[epangelias].lemma -->
ἐπαγγελία
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[epangelias].gloss -->
promise
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[epangelias].parsing -->
noun, genitive feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[epangelias].explanation -->
God’s own spoken word (as in the promise to Abraham and Sarah, quoted in v. 9). Being a "child of the promise" means being included by what God said and did, not by ordinary birth.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[epangelias].implication -->
This is why the diagram’s contrast matters pastorally: belonging to God’s people was always a matter of his word taking hold, not of pedigree — which is good news for every reader, not only for physical descendants of Abraham.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐκλογὴν · eklogēn

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekloge].surface -->
ἐκλογὴν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekloge].transliteration -->
eklogēn
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekloge].lemma -->
ἐκλογή
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekloge].gloss -->
election
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekloge].parsing -->
noun, accusative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekloge].explanation -->
"Choosing" or "selection" — the object of the preposition "according to" in "God’s purpose according to election." This is Paul’s own technical word for what he is describing; the diagram shows it modifying "purpose," the thing that "might stand."
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekloge].implication -->
Paul anchors "purpose" to "election" and then to "him who calls," not to "works" — the sequence of the sentence itself puts God’s initiative first.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[ekloge].caution -->
The word tells you THAT Paul is talking about God choosing; it does not by itself tell you the SCOPE of that choice (individual salvation, a covenant line, or both). See the guide’s debate note.
<!-- @end -->

### καλοῦντος · kalountos

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[kalountos].surface -->
καλοῦντος
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[kalountos].transliteration -->
kalountos
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[kalountos].lemma -->
καλέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[kalountos].gloss -->
calling
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[kalountos].parsing -->
present active participle, genitive masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[kalountos].explanation -->
A participle used as a noun: "the one who calls." Paul names God this way — by his present, ongoing action — as the alternative ground to "works" for his purpose "according to election."
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[kalountos].implication -->
God is described here by what he DOES (calling), not by a static title. The contrast "not from works but from him who calls" makes God’s own initiative, not human performance, the stated basis for what follows.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[kalountos].caution -->
A present-tense participle here describes God as one who calls; it is not making a separate claim about how long or how often — read it as "the calling one," not as a claim about frequency.
<!-- @end -->

### ἠγάπησα · ēgapēsa

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[egapesa].surface -->
ἠγάπησα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[egapesa].transliteration -->
ēgapēsa
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[egapesa].lemma -->
ἀγαπάω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[egapesa].gloss -->
I loved
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[egapesa].parsing -->
aorist active indicative, 1st singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[egapesa].explanation -->
God speaking in the first person, quoting Malachi 1:2: "Jacob I loved." The diagram places it in a clause perfectly parallel to the next one, with Jacob as its direct object.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[egapesa].caution -->
The aorist views the loving as a simple whole, not as a comment on how long it lasted or how it felt moment to moment. Do not read "instant, one-time love" into the tense itself — the point is the parallel with the next verb, not the tense’s length.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐμίσησα · emisēsa

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[emisesa].surface -->
ἐμίσησα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[emisesa].transliteration -->
emisēsa
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[emisesa].lemma -->
μισέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[emisesa].gloss -->
I hated
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[emisesa].parsing -->
aorist active indicative, 1st singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[emisesa].explanation -->
The matching verb for Esau, quoting Malachi 1:3. Grammatically it is built exactly like "I loved" in the clause before it — same person, same tense, same voice, a matching direct object — which is what makes the pair so striking.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[emisesa].implication -->
The grammar’s symmetry is deliberate: God is described acting toward each brother with equal directness, before either had done anything to earn either response.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-romans-9-6-13 :: greekTerms[emisesa].caution -->
Hebrew and Greek both use love/hate language for relative preference as well as raw emotion (compare Genesis 29:31; Luke 14:26). Whether "hated" here means comparative covenantal preference or something stronger is a real interpretive question — the parsing alone (aorist, active, matching "loved") does not settle it. See the guide’s debate note.
<!-- @end -->

---

# 1 Timothy 2:11–15 — a woman, learning, and two appeals

<!-- guide guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 · 1 Timothy 2:11–15 · advanced -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: title -->
1 Timothy 2:11–15 — a woman, learning, and two appeals
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: reference -->
1 Timothy 2:11–15
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: summary -->
Paul instructs Timothy on women, learning, and authority in the church.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: devotionalFrame -->
Few passages carry as much pastoral weight as this one, and it deserves care. The steps below show what the Greek sentence actually says and how its pieces connect. This guide is written from a confessional Reformed stance that receives Paul’s instruction here — along with most of the church across its history — as a standing pattern for the church’s teaching offices, grounded not merely in the Ephesian situation but in the created order Paul appeals to in verse 13 (see the confessional note beneath the steps). Because this passage is debated more actively among evangelicals than most, the strongest form of the alternative reading is also summarized fairly below, with honest cautions for both. Read slowly, keep your Bible open beside the diagram, and let the text carry its full weight.
<!-- @end -->

**Confessional frame**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: confessionalFrame -->
Historic confessional Reformed teaching has generally read this passage (together with parallel texts) as establishing a settled pattern that reserves the teaching and governing offices of the visible church to men, while fully affirming women’s indispensable ministry in prayer, in teaching children and other women (Titus 2:3–5), in prophecy (1 Corinthians 11:5), and in every spiritual gift not tied to those offices. This is offered here as one confessional tradition’s reading, clearly labelled and distinct from the fair summary of both views above — not as a substitute for weighing them.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: debateSummary.issue -->
What do Paul’s instruction not to permit a woman to teach or to αὐθεντεῖν (authentein, “exercise authority”) over a man (2:11–12), and his appeal to Adam and Eve (2:13–14), establish for the church: a permanent pattern for how the gathered church orders teaching and governing authority between men and women, or a corrective addressed to a specific problem in the Ephesian church?
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
Complementarian: the historic, majority reading, grounded in creation order
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
This is the reading the church has held through most of its history, and the majority reading among evangelicals today: Paul states a durable instruction for the gathered church, not a passing fix for a local crisis — a woman is not to teach or exercise authority over a man in that setting. Paul grounds the instruction not in the Ephesian situation but in the created order itself — Adam was formed first, then Eve, and Eve, not Adam, was deceived — pointing past the immediate circumstances to how God ordered the man–woman relationship from the beginning. On this view the ordinary sense of αὐθεντεῖν (authentein, “to exercise authority”) stands, and the instruction coheres with Paul’s comparable teaching elsewhere about the ordering of teaching and governing roles in the gathered assembly (e.g. 1 Corinthians 14:34–35).
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: debateSummary.views[0].cautions[0] -->
This view has to take care that a restriction on teaching/governing roles in the gathered assembly is not extended beyond what the text addresses into every sphere of life or every kind of speech.
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — caution 2**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: debateSummary.views[0].cautions[1] -->
It must hold the creation appeal alongside Paul’s full teaching elsewhere honoring women’s giftedness, ministry, and authority in other spheres (Romans 16; Philippians 4:2–3; Titus 2:3–5; Acts 18:26).
<!-- @end -->

### View 2

**View 2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: debateSummary.views[1].label -->
Egalitarian: a situational correction for Ephesus
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: debateSummary.views[1].summary -->
On this reading, Paul answers a specific, local problem: false teachers had already led some astray in Ephesus (1 Timothy 1:3–7, 19–20), and women there appear among those vulnerable to being deceived by such teaching (compare 1 Timothy 5:13; 2 Timothy 3:6–7), likely owing to a lack of the theological training given to men. On this view αὐθεντεῖν (authentein) — a word found nowhere else in the New Testament and only rarely in surviving Greek of the period — may denote not ordinary teaching authority but something more forceful: to “domineer,” or to seize or assume authority illegitimately. Paul’s concern, then, is a specific, disruptive exercise of self-appointed authority by untrained women, not women teaching or leading as such; the appeal to Adam and Eve illustrates being deceived (paralleling the false teachers’ effect on the untrained) rather than stating a timeless creation ordinance for church office.
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: debateSummary.views[1].cautions[0] -->
This view rests significant weight on a disputed sense of a very rare word, drawn from a small body of comparative evidence — that lexical case has to be argued on its own terms, not assumed.
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — caution 2**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: debateSummary.views[1].cautions[1] -->
It should not flatten the Adam-and-Eve appeal into “mere illustration” if the flow of Paul’s argument (marked by γάρ, gar, “for”) suggests he intends it to carry more argumentative weight than that.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
The diagram settles some things plainly: οὐδέ (oude, “nor”) ties “to teach” and “to αὐθεντεῖν” (authentein, “exercise authority”) into one coordinate pair, not two independent rules; ἀλλά (alla, “but”) sets a real, positive alternative (“but to be in quietness”) alongside the negated pair; and γάρ (gar, “for”) formally attaches the Adam-and-Eve sentences to verse 12 as Paul’s stated reason, grounding it in the creation account rather than leaving it unsupported. What the grammar cannot settle by itself: whether the present tense of ἐπιτρέπω (epitrepō, “I am not permitting”) expresses a timeless rule or a decision for a live situation — Greek present-tense verbs carry both uses, and only context can choose between them; and precisely what the rare verb αὐθεντεῖν, found nowhere else in the New Testament and rendered “exercise authority” by some and “domineer” by others, meant in ordinary use. Those are lexical, historical, and whole-canon questions, not questions the syntax diagram can answer on its own — it clarifies exactly what is joined to what and where each appeal attaches, and leaves the weighing to the reader, alongside sound teaching and pastoral counsel.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — Let her learn, in quietness

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-let-her-learn].title -->
Let her learn, in quietness
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-let-her-learn].body -->
Paul’s sentence opens positively: [[manthaneto]], “let her learn” — a command, not only a restriction. The diagram puts γυνή (gynē, “a woman”) on the left of the subject–predicate divider and the imperative [[manthaneto]] on the right, with two phrases hanging beneath the verb: “in [[hesychia]]” and “in all submissiveness.” The instruction to learn comes first; the manner named alongside it is quietness and submissiveness — words this same letter also uses for a peaceable, orderly life (1 Timothy 2:2).
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-let-her-learn].implication -->
Before anything is restricted, something is commanded: a woman is told to be a learner. In a setting where the theological training needed to teach had rarely, if ever, been given to women, being told to learn was itself significant.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-let-her-learn].caution -->
[[hesychia]] describes a settled demeanor, not literal silence; the diagram shows it modifying HOW she learns, not whether she may speak at all.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — One verb governs the whole sentence

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-i-do-not-permit].title -->
One verb governs the whole sentence
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-i-do-not-permit].body -->
Verse 12 turns from command to restriction, and the diagram shows exactly how much weight rests on a single verb: [[epitrepo]], “I do not permit,” spoken by Paul in his own voice and joined to what came before by δέ (de, “and/but”). Everything that follows — “to teach,” “to exercise authority,” “but to be in quietness” — is one large complement clause hanging beneath this one verb as its direct object. The next two steps open up what is inside that clause.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-i-do-not-permit].implication -->
Whatever Paul is restricting, the diagram shows it is restricted by one deliberate verb of permission — the same verb used elsewhere in the New Testament for granting or withholding permission in ordinary situations (e.g. Matthew 8:21; Acts 21:39–40).
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — Two infinitives, joined as one pair

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-oude-pair].title -->
Two infinitives, joined as one pair
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-oude-pair].body -->
Inside that clause, the diagram shows a small coordinate structure: “to teach” (διδάσκειν, didaskein, with the woman named as its understood subject) and “to [[authentein]] over a man” are joined by [[oude]] (“nor”) into a single pair. [[authentein]] itself takes ἀνδρός (andros, “a man,” genitive) as its own object — the diagram draws that connection directly, a solid line from the infinitive to its object. Whatever Paul is not permitting is this joined pair, taken together.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-oude-pair].caution -->
Coordination with [[oude]] shows these two actions are treated as a matched pair grammatically; it does not, by itself, say whether Paul means two closely related expressions of one problem or two distinct activities.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — A negative half, and a positive alternative

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-not-but].title -->
A negative half, and a positive alternative
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-not-but].body -->
Zooming out one level, the diagram shows that paired clause (teach / [[authentein]]) negated as a whole by οὐκ (ouk, “not”), then joined by ἀλλά (alla, “but”) to a second clause: “but to be in [[hesychia]]” — the same word for quietness used already in verse 11. Negative half and positive half stand side by side as one coordinate unit, and that whole unit is what [[epitrepo]] governs.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-not-but].implication -->
The diagram lets you see plainly that Paul frames the restriction and its positive alternative as one balanced statement — not permitting X, but Y — echoing the same “quietness” named as the manner of learning back in verse 11.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 5 — For: the appeal to Adam and Eve

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-gar-adam-eve].title -->
For: the appeal to Adam and Eve
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-gar-adam-eve].body -->
Verses 13–14 are formally attached to what precedes by [[gar]] (“for”) — the diagram draws this connective joining the two sentences at the top level, marking everything that follows as Paul’s stated reason. The first sentence sets Adam and Eve side by side: Adam was formed πρῶτος (prōtos, “first”), then Eve. The second contrasts them again: Adam “was not deceived,” but the woman, [[exapatetheisa]] (“having been deceived”), “has come into transgression.” The diagram shows this participle feeding directly into the main verb as the stated reason for what happened to her.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-gar-adam-eve].implication -->
The grammar is clear that Paul offers this pair of statements as support for verse 12, clear about the sequence (Adam, then Eve), and clear about who was and was not described as deceived. Paul reaches back past the Ephesian situation to the created order itself — which is why the historic, majority reading of the church has taken this appeal as grounding a lasting pattern for the church rather than a merely local fix. That is not the only reading held by faithful evangelicals, though, and the strongest form of the alternative is taken up fairly below.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: steps[step-gar-adam-eve].caution -->
Notice the two different verbs: Adam “was not deceived” (ἀπατάω, apataō); Eve is described with the compound ἐξαπατάω (exapataō), which many read as intensified — “thoroughly deceived.” The grammar records that difference; it does not by itself explain why the text draws it.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### μανθανέτω · manthanetō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[manthaneto].surface -->
μανθανέτω
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[manthaneto].transliteration -->
manthanetō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[manthaneto].lemma -->
μανθάνω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[manthaneto].gloss -->
let her learn
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[manthaneto].parsing -->
present active imperative, 3rd singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[manthaneto].explanation -->
The verb is positive: an imperative command to learn, not merely a prohibition. Third-singular imperatives like this are a common Greek way of giving an instruction about someone not directly addressed (“let her…” / “she is to…”).
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[manthaneto].implication -->
The sentence opens with something commanded FOR the woman, not only something withheld from her: she is told to learn.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[manthaneto].caution -->
The present tense presents the learning as an ongoing process; by itself it does not say how long or in what circumstances the instruction applies — the tense describes how the action is pictured, not a rule about duration.
<!-- @end -->

### ἡσυχίᾳ · hēsychia

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[hesychia].surface -->
ἡσυχίᾳ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[hesychia].transliteration -->
hēsychia
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[hesychia].lemma -->
ἡσυχία
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[hesychia].gloss -->
quietness, tranquility
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[hesychia].parsing -->
noun, dative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[hesychia].explanation -->
This noun describes a settled, peaceable demeanor — the same word this letter uses elsewhere for a peaceful, orderly life (1 Timothy 2:2). It is not the ordinary Greek word for being mute; “quietness” or “peaceableness” captures it better than “silence.”
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[hesychia].caution -->
Word meaning is not the whole picture — how the term functions in this argument, and alongside the New Testament’s fuller teaching about women’s speech and ministry, belongs to the larger interpretive question above.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐπιτρέπω · epitrepō

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[epitrepo].surface -->
ἐπιτρέπω
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[epitrepo].transliteration -->
epitrepō
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[epitrepo].lemma -->
ἐπιτρέπω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[epitrepo].gloss -->
I do not permit / I am not permitting
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[epitrepo].parsing -->
present active indicative, 1st singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[epitrepo].explanation -->
Paul speaks in his own first-person voice: “I am not permitting.” The diagram shows this single verb governing everything that follows as one large complement clause — “to teach… nor to exercise authority… but to be in quietness” is all one thing that is, or is not, permitted.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[epitrepo].caution -->
Greek present-tense verbs can describe either a general, standing policy or a decision in a live situation; the tense form alone does not decide which sense Paul intends here — see the discussion above.
<!-- @end -->

### αὐθεντεῖν · authentein

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[authentein].surface -->
αὐθεντεῖν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[authentein].transliteration -->
authentein
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[authentein].lemma -->
αὐθεντέω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[authentein].gloss -->
to exercise authority over
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[authentein].parsing -->
present active infinitive
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[authentein].explanation -->
This verb occurs nowhere else in the New Testament (a hapax legomenon), and only rarely in surviving Greek from this period. The diagram shows it taking ἀνδρός (andros, “a man”) as its object, joined to διδάσκειν (didaskein, “to teach”) by οὐδέ (oude, “nor”). What the verb itself denotes — ordinary authority, or something more forceful like “domineer” or “usurp authority” — is a genuine lexical question.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[authentein].implication -->
Because the diagram can show exactly what αὐθεντεῖν is coordinated with and what its object is, it clarifies the sentence’s shape; it cannot, by grammar alone, settle which shade of meaning the rare verb carries.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[authentein].caution -->
Rare words are decided from a small number of surviving examples; readers should hold conclusions about this word with real caution in either direction.
<!-- @end -->

### οὐδὲ · oude

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[oude].surface -->
οὐδὲ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[oude].transliteration -->
oude
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[oude].lemma -->
οὐδέ
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[oude].gloss -->
nor
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[oude].parsing -->
correlative negative conjunction
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[oude].explanation -->
οὐδέ links διδάσκειν (didaskein, “to teach”) and αὐθεντεῖν (authentein, “to exercise authority”) into a single joined pair beneath one negation — grammatically, whatever is restricted is this pair taken together, not two separate, independent rules.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[oude].implication -->
The diagram shows one coordinate node holding both infinitives; how the two actions relate in meaning — a single combined idea, two closely related actions, or two of a kind — is part of the passage’s wider debate.
<!-- @end -->

### γὰρ · gar

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[gar].surface -->
γὰρ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[gar].transliteration -->
gar
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[gar].lemma -->
γάρ
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[gar].gloss -->
for
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[gar].parsing -->
postpositive conjunction
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[gar].explanation -->
γάρ formally attaches what follows — the whole Adam-and-Eve sentence — to verse 12 as its stated ground. The diagram shows this connective joining the two sentences at the top level, so whatever verses 13–14 says is offered by Paul as a reason for verse 12, not a separate, unrelated thought.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[gar].implication -->
The grammar is plain that an appeal to Adam and Eve is offered as support for what precedes it; exactly how that support works (as illustration, as an argument from creation order, or both) is where the larger debate begins.
<!-- @end -->

### ἐξαπατηθεῖσα · exapatētheisa

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[exapatetheisa].surface -->
ἐξαπατηθεῖσα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[exapatetheisa].transliteration -->
exapatētheisa
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[exapatetheisa].lemma -->
ἐξαπατάω
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[exapatetheisa].gloss -->
having been deceived
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[exapatetheisa].parsing -->
aorist passive participle, nominative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[exapatetheisa].explanation -->
Verse 14 uses two related verbs: Adam “was not deceived” (ἀπατάω, apataō, v.14a), while the woman is described with the intensified compound ἐξαπατάω (exapataō), “having been thoroughly deceived.” The participle attaches as the reason feeding the main verb γέγονεν (gegonen, “has come to be”): she has come into transgression by way of having been deceived.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-1-timothy-2-11-15 :: greekTerms[exapatetheisa].caution -->
The aorist participle views the deceiving as a completed event prior to the coming-into-transgression; it does not by itself explain WHY only Eve is described this way — that question belongs to the wider discussion, not the grammar alone.
<!-- @end -->

---

# Titus 2:13 — our great God and Savior

<!-- guide guide-titus-2-13 · Titus 2:13 · intermediate -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: title -->
Titus 2:13 — our great God and Savior
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: reference -->
Titus 2:13
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: summary -->
Titus calls Jesus Christ our great God and Savior in one breath.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: devotionalFrame -->
Paul is not writing a creed here — he is telling Titus how godly people wait. But the sentence he reaches for quietly does something enormous: in a single breath it calls Jesus Christ "our great God and Savior." Keep your own Bible open and follow the diagram to see exactly how the Greek holds that together.
<!-- @end -->

**Confessional frame**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: confessionalFrame -->
Read confessionally: this verse names one person, Jesus Christ, as "our great God and Savior," and stands with Romans 9:5 and 2 Peter 1:1 as another place Scripture calls him God outright, worshiping him as fully divine alongside the Father — consistent with the church's historic confession of Christ's full deity.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: debateSummary.issue -->
Does "our great God and Savior Jesus Christ" name one person — Jesus called both God and Savior — or two: God the Father, and separately the Savior Jesus Christ?
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
One person — the standard reading
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
One article (τοῦ — tou, "the") governs both "God" and "Savior," joined by καί (kai, "and") — the pattern Granville Sharp described for singular, personal, non-proper nouns. That shape points to a single referent: Jesus Christ is here called both "God" and "Savior." This is the reading followed above, and it is also the reading of most modern grammarians and English translations (ESV, NIV, NASB, NKJV, CSB, and others); the identical construction appears again in 2 Peter 1:1 ("our God and Savior Jesus Christ"), and arguably in Romans 9:5.
<!-- @end -->

### View 2

**View 2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: debateSummary.views[1].label -->
Two persons — an alternate reading
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: debateSummary.views[1].summary -->
A minority reading takes "the great God" as the Father and "our Savior Jesus Christ" as a second, distinct figure introduced alongside him, treating the shared article more loosely than the construction generally requires.
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: debateSummary.views[1].cautions[0] -->
This view has to explain why θεός (theos, "God") and σωτήρ (sōtēr, "Savior") — both singular, personal, non-proper nouns joined by one καί (kai, "and") under one article — would behave differently here than the same construction does elsewhere in the New Testament.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
The single-article, one-καί (kai, "and") shape strongly favors one referent rather than two, since θεός (theos, "God") and σωτήρ (sōtēr, "Savior") meet exactly the conditions (singular, personal, non-proper) the construction requires. But grammar establishes a strong likelihood, not an airtight proof by itself — it opens and constrains the question rather than settling every theological implication on its own.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — Waiting for one glorious appearing

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-two-things-awaited].title -->
Waiting for one glorious appearing
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-two-things-awaited].body -->
Paul describes believers as people "awaiting" one reality that has two faces: the [[elpida]] ("hope") we hold onto now, and its ἐπιφάνειαν (epiphaneian, "appearing") — the moment that hope becomes visible. The diagram joins "hope" and "appearing" as a matched pair under one article, connected by [[kai]]: not two separate hopes, but one future reality named first as a hope, then as its unveiling.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-two-things-awaited].implication -->
Everything Paul has just said about living "self-controlled, upright, and godly" (v. 12) leans forward on this one verse: Christian character is shaped by what we are waiting for.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — One article holds two titles together

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-one-article-two-nouns].title -->
One article holds two titles together
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-one-article-two-nouns].body -->
Now look at whose glory is appearing: "the glory of [[tou]] great [[theou]] and ([[kai]]) [[soteros]] of us." In Greek there is only one definite article here — [[tou]] — and it stands in front of [[theou]] alone. [[soteros]] gets no article of its own; instead it is joined to [[theou]] by [[kai]] as a matched partner sharing that same "the." The diagram draws this exactly: one article branching to "great God," that noun linked by a coordinator to "Savior" standing right beside it.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-one-article-two-nouns].caution -->
This pattern (often named for the 18th-century grammarian Granville Sharp) is narrow and well-defined: it applies reliably to singular, personal, non-proper nouns joined by [[kai]] under one article — exactly what [[theou]] and [[soteros]] are here. It is not a claim that every Greek "the X and Y" phrase works this way; it is this specific shape doing specific work.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — God and Savior — one name follows: Jesus Christ

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-one-person-jesus-christ].title -->
God and Savior — one name follows: Jesus Christ
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-one-person-jesus-christ].body -->
The diagram carries this one step further: the whole phrase "our great God and Savior" stands beside [[iesou]] [[christou]] — Jesus Christ — in apposition, meaning the second phrase renames the first. Grammatically, "our great God and Savior" and "Jesus Christ" are not two people introduced back to back; they are one person, named twice — first by title, then by name.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-one-person-jesus-christ].implication -->
Read together, the sentence Paul has built says something remarkable in a single breath: Jesus Christ is "our great God and Savior." Few verses in the New Testament call Jesus God this directly.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-one-person-jesus-christ].caution -->
A minority of readers take "the great God" and "our Savior Jesus Christ" as two separate figures loosely paired rather than one person identified by both titles — see the debate note below for how the grammar bears on that question.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — The glory that belongs to him

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-whose-glory].title -->
The glory that belongs to him
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-whose-glory].body -->
Follow the genitive chain all the way back: the ἐπιφάνειαν (epiphaneian, "appearing") is the appearing of "the glory," and that glory belongs, grammatically, to "our great God and Savior Jesus Christ." What believers are waiting to see unveiled is not an abstract brightness — it is the very glory of this one person.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-whose-glory].implication -->
This is why Paul can call it a "blessed" hope: what appears is not judgment first of all, but the glory of the very one who — the sentence goes on to say — already gave himself for us (v. 14).
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: steps[step-whose-glory].caution -->
The diagram traces a grammatical relationship — glory belonging to this person — it does not by itself unpack every facet of what that glory will look like when it appears; Paul leaves that for the rest of the letter and the church's larger hope.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### ἐλπίδα · elpida

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[elpida].surface -->
ἐλπίδα
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[elpida].transliteration -->
elpida
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[elpida].lemma -->
ἐλπίς
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[elpida].gloss -->
hope
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[elpida].parsing -->
noun, accusative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[elpida].explanation -->
The "hope" Paul says every believer is waiting for — paired in the same breath with its "appearing," as one future reality described from two angles rather than two separate hopes.
<!-- @end -->

### τοῦ · tou

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[tou].surface -->
τοῦ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[tou].transliteration -->
tou
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[tou].lemma -->
ὁ
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[tou].gloss -->
the
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[tou].parsing -->
definite article, genitive masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[tou].explanation -->
The single article that opens "the great God" — in the diagram it attaches only to θεοῦ (theou, "God"); "Savior" (σωτῆρος, sōtēros) is joined to that same article by καί (kai, "and") rather than taking one of its own.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[tou].implication -->
This is the hinge of the whole construction: one article covering two nouns joined by "and" is what ties "God" and "Savior" to a single person rather than two.
<!-- @end -->

### θεοῦ · theou

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[theou].surface -->
θεοῦ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[theou].transliteration -->
theou
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[theou].lemma -->
θεός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[theou].gloss -->
God
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[theou].parsing -->
noun, genitive masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[theou].explanation -->
The first of the two matched titles under the one article — a singular, common noun (not a proper name), exactly the kind of word this construction is built to join to another title for one person.
<!-- @end -->

### καὶ · kai

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[kai].surface -->
καὶ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[kai].transliteration -->
kai
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[kai].lemma -->
καί
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[kai].gloss -->
and
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[kai].parsing -->
conjunction
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[kai].explanation -->
The small word that joins "Savior" to "God" under the single article τοῦ (tou, "the") — ordinary in itself, but doing careful work here: joining two titles under one shared article rather than introducing a new person with a new one.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[kai].caution -->
Not every Greek "X and Y" carries this force. The reading depends on the whole shape here — one article, two singular non-proper nouns, one καί — not on the conjunction by itself.
<!-- @end -->

### σωτῆρος · sōtēros

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[soteros].surface -->
σωτῆρος
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[soteros].transliteration -->
sōtēros
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[soteros].lemma -->
σωτήρ
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[soteros].gloss -->
Savior
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[soteros].parsing -->
noun, genitive masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[soteros].explanation -->
The second matched title, joined to "God" under the same article — like θεοῦ (theou, "God"), a singular common noun rather than a proper name, which is exactly the shape Granville Sharp's rule describes.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[soteros].implication -->
Sharing an article with "God" and standing right beside "Jesus Christ," this word identifies the Savior as the very person just called God.
<!-- @end -->

### Ἰησοῦ · Iēsou

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[iesou].surface -->
Ἰησοῦ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[iesou].transliteration -->
Iēsou
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[iesou].lemma -->
Ἰησοῦς
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[iesou].gloss -->
Jesus
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[iesou].parsing -->
proper noun, genitive masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[iesou].explanation -->
The name that the whole "God and Savior" phrase leans on in apposition — grammatically restating who has just been described by those two titles.
<!-- @end -->

### Χριστοῦ · Christou

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[christou].surface -->
Χριστοῦ
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[christou].transliteration -->
Christou
<!-- @end -->

**Lemma**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[christou].lemma -->
Χριστός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[christou].gloss -->
Christ
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[christou].parsing -->
proper noun, genitive masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-titus-2-13 :: greekTerms[christou].explanation -->
"Christ" — "Anointed One," the title marking Jesus as God's promised king — set directly beside his name, completing the identification: "our great God and Savior, Jesus Christ."

# 2 Peter 1:1 — our God and Savior

<!-- guide guide-2-peter-1-1 · 2 Peter 1:1 · intermediate -->

## Overview

**Guide title**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: title -->
2 Peter 1:1 — our God and Savior
<!-- @end -->

**Reference**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: reference -->
2 Peter 1:1
<!-- @end -->

**Picker summary**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: summary -->
Peter calls Jesus Christ both our God and our Savior in one phrase.
<!-- @end -->

**Devotional frame**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: devotionalFrame -->
Peter is not writing a doctrinal essay here — just a letter opening. But before he has finished his very first sentence, the grammar he reaches for quietly calls Jesus Christ "our God and Savior." Follow the diagram to see exactly how the Greek holds that title together.
<!-- @end -->

**Confessional frame**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: confessionalFrame -->
Read confessionally: this verse stands with Titus 2:13, John 1:1, and Romans 9:5 as another place Scripture calls Jesus Christ God outright, worshiping him as fully divine alongside the Father — consistent with the church's historic confession of Christ's full deity.
<!-- @end -->

## Debate summary (fair-views note)

**The issue**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: debateSummary.issue -->
Does "our God and Savior Jesus Christ" name one person — Jesus called both God and Savior — or two: God the Father, and separately the Savior Jesus Christ?
<!-- @end -->

### View 1

**View 1 — label**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: debateSummary.views[0].label -->
One person (the standard reading)
<!-- @end -->

**View 1 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: debateSummary.views[0].summary -->
One article (τοῦ, tou, "the") governs both "God" and "Savior," joined by καί (kai, "and") — the pattern Granville Sharp described for singular, personal, non-proper nouns. That shape points to a single referent: Jesus Christ is here called both "God" and "Savior." Most modern grammarians and translations (ESV, NIV, NASB, NKJV, and others) read it this way, and Peter uses the identical construction elsewhere in this letter (2 Peter 1:11; 2:20; 3:2, 18, "our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ") where all sides agree only one person is meant.
<!-- @end -->

### View 2

**View 2 — label**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: debateSummary.views[1].label -->
Two persons (an alternate reading)
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — summary**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: debateSummary.views[1].summary -->
A minority reading takes "God" as the Father and "Savior Jesus Christ" as a separate figure added alongside, treating the shared article more loosely. This reading has to explain why the same repeated construction in Peter's later chapters is not read the same way, and it runs against how this grammatical pattern normally behaves.
<!-- @end -->

**View 2 — caution 1**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: debateSummary.views[1].cautions[0] -->
This view is held more often for prior theological reasons than because the Greek naturally reads that way.
<!-- @end -->

**How the grammar opens the question**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: debateSummary.grammarOpensQuestionHow -->
The single-article, one-καί (kai, "and") pattern strongly favors one referent, and Peter's own repeated use of the same shape later in this letter makes a different meaning here unlikely. But grammar establishes a strong probability, not an ironclad proof by itself — it opens and constrains the question rather than settling every theological implication on its own.
<!-- @end -->

## Steps

### Step 1 — A letter opens: writer, then readers

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-overview].title -->
A letter opens: writer, then readers
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-overview].body -->
Like most ancient letters, this sentence has no main verb — it simply names the writer, [[symeon]] Peter, and then turns "to those" who share his faith. The diagram shows this shape: [[symeon]] as subject over a supplied predicate, with everything else in the verse hanging underneath as description.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-overview].implication -->
Even a greeting has structure worth noticing: Peter names himself, then turns immediately to the people he is writing to. Everything that follows — his role, their faith, its ground — is packed into modifying phrases around that simple frame.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 2 — A servant and apostle — of one Lord

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-servant-apostle].title -->
A servant and apostle — of one Lord
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-servant-apostle].body -->
Peter describes himself with two roles, "servant and apostle," joined by [[kai_1]] — but both roles point to a single genitive, "of Jesus Christ." One person governs two titles. Watch for this shape again at the end of the verse, where it does even more work.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-servant-apostle].implication -->
Peter is content to be both a slave and a messenger — as long as both titles belong to Jesus Christ. It is a small foretaste of the pattern the diagram will show again in a moment, this time naming Jesus himself.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 3 — A faith equal to ours

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-faith-equal].title -->
A faith equal to ours
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-faith-equal].body -->
Peter, an apostle who walked with Jesus, writes to readers who never did — yet he calls their faith "equally precious" ([[isotimon]]) to his own, a faith they have "obtained" through something outside themselves. The diagram carries that faith on the main line, with its source named just below.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-faith-equal].caution -->
The grammar simply names the faith as "equal in standing"; Peter does not spell out here every way apostle and reader compare. The weight of the claim comes from the sentence he is about to finish.
<!-- @end -->

### Step 4 — One article, two titles, one name

**Step title**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-granville-sharp].title -->
One article, two titles, one name
<!-- @end -->

**Body**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-granville-sharp].body -->
Here is the ground of that faith: "through the [[dikaiosyne]] of [[tou]] [[theou]] ἡμῶν (hēmōn, "our") [[kai_2]] [[soteros]] Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ (Iēsou Christou, "Jesus Christ")" — "the righteousness of our God and Savior Jesus Christ." Notice what the diagram shows: the article [[tou]] attaches only once, to [[theou]] ("God"); [[soteros]] ("Savior") is joined to it by [[kai_2]] as a matched partner under that same article, and the whole phrase leans on the one name Jesus Christ — one article, two titles, one person, Jesus Christ himself named God and Savior.
<!-- @end -->

**Why it matters**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-granville-sharp].implication -->
One article governing both singular nouns means "God and Savior" names one person: Jesus Christ. This is the identical construction behind Titus 2:13 ("our great God and Savior Jesus Christ"), and Peter uses the very same shape again later in this letter — "our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ" (2 Peter 1:11; also 2:20; 3:2, 18) — where everyone agrees only one person is meant. Read that way here too, this verse stands alongside Romans 9:5 as another place the New Testament calls Jesus Christ God outright. Peter grounds his readers' faith in the righteousness of the very person he is writing to them about.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: steps[step-granville-sharp].caution -->
The rule this depends on (often named for the grammarian Granville Sharp) is narrow and well-defined: it applies to singular, personal, non-proper nouns joined by [[kai_2]] under one article — exactly what [[theou]] and [[soteros]] are here. It is not a general rule that any "X and Y" phrase means one thing; it is this specific shape, and it is what ties "Savior" to the "God" just named as one and the same person.
<!-- @end -->

## Greek terms

### Συμεὼν · Symeōn

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[symeon].surface -->
Συμεὼν
<!-- @end -->

**Transliteration**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[symeon].transliteration -->
Symeōn
<!-- @end -->

<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[symeon].lemma -->
Συμεών
<!-- @end -->

<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[symeon].gloss -->
Simon
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[symeon].parsing -->
proper noun, nominative masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[symeon].explanation -->
The Hebrew/Aramaic form of Peter's first name (as in Acts 15:14), used alongside his more familiar Greek name Πέτρος (Petros, "Peter") — the writer identifying himself at the very start of the letter.
<!-- @end -->

### καὶ · kai

**Surface (must match the token)**

<!-- @end -->

<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[kai_1].transliteration -->

<!-- @end -->

καί
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[kai_1].gloss -->

<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[kai_1].parsing -->
conjunction
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[kai_1].explanation -->
The simple coordinating "and" joining "servant" and "apostle" — two titles Peter claims for himself, both governed by the one name that follows, "of Jesus Christ."
<!-- @end -->

### ἰσότιμον · isotimon

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[isotimon].surface -->

<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[isotimon].gloss -->
equally precious, of equal standing
<!-- @end -->

**Parsing**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[isotimon].parsing -->
adjective, accusative feminine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[isotimon].explanation -->
A rare compound word (equal + honor/value) describing the faith of Peter's readers as of the same worth as an apostle's own faith — a faith Peter says they "obtained," not earned.
<!-- @end -->

### δικαιοσύνῃ · dikaiosynē

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[dikaiosyne].explanation -->

<!-- @end -->

### τοῦ · tou

<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[tou].implication -->
This is the hinge of the whole construction: one article covering two nouns joined by "and" is what ties "God" and "Savior" to a single person rather than two.
<!-- @end -->

### θεοῦ · theou

**Surface (must match the token)**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[theou].surface -->

theou

θεός
<!-- @end -->

**Gloss**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[theou].gloss -->
God
<!-- @end -->

<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[theou].parsing -->
noun, genitive masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[theou].explanation -->
The first of the two matched titles under the one article — a singular, common (not a proper name) noun, exactly the kind of word this construction is built to join to another title for one person.
<!-- @end -->

**Caution**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[kai_2].caution -->
Not every "X and Y" in Greek carries this force — the reading depends on the specific shape here (one article, two singular non-proper nouns, one καί), not on the conjunction alone.
<!-- @end -->

### σωτῆρος · sōtēros

<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[soteros].surface -->

Savior
<!-- @end -->

noun, genitive masculine singular
<!-- @end -->

**Explanation**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[soteros].explanation -->
The second matched title, joined to "God" under the same article — and, like θεοῦ (theou), a singular common noun rather than a proper name, which is exactly the shape Granville Sharp's rule describes.
<!-- @end -->

**Implication**
<!-- @field guide-2-peter-1-1 :: greekTerms[soteros].implication -->
Sharing an article with "God" and standing right beside "Jesus Christ," this word identifies the Savior as the very person just called God — the same pairing Peter uses repeatedly later in this letter (1:11; 2:20; 3:2, 18).
